§ 09 · AFTER THE MIGRATION

Post-migration backlog

Deliberately out of scope — parity first. Each becomes cheap once the new foundation exists.

  • The save format, in one piece — DONE. The three things S9 established were one problem, done together in three commits after S9 closed. The load gate stopped consulting the version, which is what made the field usable; isVersionHigherThan now does what its name says, with compare and isVersionAtLeast beside it. Save format 2.0.0 stores canonical centimetres and stamps "units": "cm", so all twenty-five save/load unit combinations round-trip exactly where only the five matching pairs did, and a metre-era file can no longer collapse a plan to a single point. material_colors became sparse — a colour only where somebody chose one. The pre-S8 values are still not re-interpreted, and the reason survived the version field arriving: a stamp tells you a file is old, not what is inside it, and nothing in a 0.0.2a file separates a colour the model author baked in from one the user picked. Old files are read as written and written straight back, so nothing is discarded; the ambiguity stops being created rather than being resolved retroactively. tests/fixtures/v1/ is the frozen corpus, metres-room.blueprint3d included. 828 tests.
  • Quirk fixes with tests: the preserved Utils.pointInPolygon/isClockwise bugs, listener-accumulation edge cases, skybox/ground exclusion for OBJ export (see P9) — each as an isolated, testable change.
  • Features from the index2 reference: environment-map picker, camera nudge/pan buttons, item context menu, item-loading indicator (its EVENT_ITEM_LOADING/LOADED events already exist in the library) — their API surface is already catalogued.
  • Undo/redo: the event backbone + serialized snapshots make a command stack tractable now.
  • Performance: texture cache + disposal (today TextureLoader re-loads per wall redraw and never disposes), instanced furniture, and 2D rAF coalescing — still open, and S9 measured it: no requestAnimationFrame anywhere in the 2D path, view.draw() called synchronously from 14 sites, three of them inside the pointermove handler. Picked up as RM-002 R-04 and R-05, with the leak quantified — and both closed since, by P3 and P6. The site count was right and the cost was worse than it reads: all three pointermove draws fire on the same event during a drag, so it was three repaints per event rather than one.
  • TypeScript (optional): the lib is now clean ESM with JSDoc; incremental checkJs adoption is low-friction if ever wanted — the user's chosen target for this migration is JavaScript, so this stays parked. Un-parked by RM-002 R-07, at the JSDoc level only: checkJs with no .ts files, so the JavaScript decision stands.
  • WebXR: setAnimationLoop is already the render driver; the old A-Frame vrtest can be replaced by a native XR mode.

Method. This plan is grounded in a full multi-pass audit of the codebase at commit ca4796b (2026-08-13): every file in src/scripts/ read and inventoried; both demo apps traced feature-by-feature into the API contract in §01/§07; every THREE.* symbol verified against a live three@0.185.1 install (and the vendored controls diffed against pristine r98 copies); the ESM circular-import crash reproduced empirically; asset formats verified by reading file headers; dependency verdicts grep-verified. File:line references throughout point at the audited commit.

architect3d · Migration Roadmap RM-001 rev A · 10 sprints ≈ 20 weeks · single-developer cadence — S3's conversion tooling and S8's visual pass carry the natural slack for spillover from the S4 and S6 heavies. Plan reviewed by three independent adversarial passes (factual accuracy, sequencing, completeness) before delivery; 21 findings folded back in.

§ 10 · REDLINE

Where the project actually stands

The honest headline first, because it changes what this document is for. Dependency currency verified with npm outdated and npm view per package, not from memory.

0
npm outdated is empty. Vue, Vite 8, Vitest 4, three r185, Tailwind 4, ESLint 10, Reka UI — all at latest published
870
tests across 17 files, all passing · 0 lint errors, 28 deliberate warnings
3
CI steps — test, lint, library build — on dev and master only
0
type checks, coverage floors, browser tests, size budgets or dependency policy

So there is no technology upgrade to do. The stack is current and the code is well tested for a library of its age. What is thin is everything between the tests passing and the site being live — and the load-bearing global state that a second instance, a second embedder, or a reactive settings panel would each run straight into.

One defect, found and fixed during the review

npm test was exiting 1. All 870 assertions passed; the run failed anyway. Clicking a catalog item calls the real GLTFLoader, and under Node a relative URL throws synchronously out of new Request — past addItem, past the Vue event handler, out of the test — which Vitest reports as an unhandled exception and fails the run on.

FrameLocation
TypeError: Failed to parse URL from models/js-glb/cb-rochelle-gray_baked.glb—
FileLoader.loadthree/build/three.core.js:44617
GLTFLoader.loadthree/examples/jsm/loaders/GLTFLoader.js:319
Scene.addItemsrc/scripts/model/scene.js:230
onAddItemsrc/app/App.vue:295

CI does not run on amit, by policy — so this would have gone red on the merge to dev, not here. Fixed in edf0c59 by stubbing Scene.setItemLoader, the seam the library already provides, alongside the renderer stub the suite already installs.

The interesting part is what turned up when the fix was attempted properly, in the library, by giving addItem the failure path it lacks. Two things pushed back, and both are findings in their own right — see R-01.

§ 11 · ARCHITECTURE FINDINGS

Seven findings, ranked by what they cost today

Not by how interesting they are. Every one is grep-verified against the working tree; the three empirical ones were reproduced, not inferred.

NºFindingSevLocation
R-01Item loader has no failure path, and the convention standing in for one is unsafehighmodel/scene.js:222–247
R-02Global mutable singletons make a second instance impossiblehighcore/configuration.js:21
R-03Configuration is the one change vector that broadcasts nothingmedcore/configuration.js:30–70
R-04A texture is allocated per wall per redraw, and none are ever releasedhighthree/edge.js:29, 195
R-05The 2D canvas repaints synchronously inside pointermovemedfloorplanner.js ×14
R-06The package ships raw source, with three as a hard dependencymedpackage.json:6–15, 52–56

R-01 · The item loader has no failure path high

Both loaders are called with a null onError and nothing around them. A 404, a malformed .glb, or a URL the environment cannot parse dispatches EVENT_ITEM_LOADING and then dispatches nothing at all.

Three things depend on a balance that cannot be relied on: an embedder's loading indicator, Scene.unloadableItemCount, and the app's undo gate — which counts loads in flight and needs an eight-second timer purely to survive a count that never comes back down. SETTLE_BACKSTOP_MS is documented as a backstop; it is really a workaround.

The formatless branch already models the intended fix: dispatch EVENT_ITEM_LOADED with item: null, so every listener counting loads stays balanced whether or not it knows the failure mode exists. That convention does not survive contact with the Controller. Generalising it made Controller.itemLoaded dereference the null immediately:

ReproducedLocation
TypeError: Cannot read properties of null (reading position_set)three/controller.js:139

So the null-item branch that already ships has never been exercised with a Controller attached. Second, a characterization test pins addItem to propagate a ReferenceError from item.js:111, which any try/catch swallows. Per §01's preserve-or-fix policy a characterization failure is a signal to re-check the change, so the attempt was reverted rather than the expectation edited.

LANDED — P3
  • Done as proposed. addItem dispatches exactly one LOADING and exactly one LOADED whatever happens, the failure carrying a null item; Controller.itemLoaded null-checks; the catch covers starting a load and nothing else, so the installed itemLoader stays outside it and the DOM-boundary characterization test still gets its ReferenceError, unchanged
  • The suite now passes with P1's app-shell stub removed — that stub was working around exactly this. Kept for determinism, with its reason rewritten to say so
  • SETTLE_BACKSTOP_MS stays, but as a genuine backstop rather than a workaround wearing the word: the only path that can still leave a load unresolved is an embedder's own setItemLoader, which is arbitrary code under no obligation to call back
AS PROPOSED
  • Guard Controller.itemLoaded against a null item
  • Narrow the catch so it wraps only the loader dispatch, never loaderCallback
  • Add a real onError to both loaders
  • Re-point the characterization test at the behaviour actually wanted, as a deliberate change with its own changelog line
  • Once the count is trustworthy, SETTLE_BACKSTOP_MS becomes a true backstop and the UI can finally say "couldn't load that chair"

R-02 · Global mutable singletons block a second instance high

Four pieces of state are module-level and mutable: config, renderProfile, floorplannerPalette, and the static Scene.unloadableItemCount. Two BlueprintJS instances on one page share units, scale, grid spacing, snap tolerance, the whole 2D palette and the render profile. Change one, both move. (Five, in fact — P7 found wallInformation too. The count below is the survey as written, left as the diagnosis.)

Not hypothetical — the test suite already pays for it. The theme suites snapshot a PRISTINE_PALETTE at import time to stop cross-test pollution corrupting the library-defaults assertions.

RE-SCOPED — P4 MEASURED IT AND THE ESTIMATE WAS WRONG

P4 paired this with R-06 at 4–5 days for both. R-06 is done; this is not, and it is not a few days' work. The measurement, taken before starting rather than after:

SingletonReadersVerdict
Scene.unloadableItemCount1 sitedone per-instance, static kept as the process total
floorplannerPalette1 fileTractable, low value — the app has one 2D view, and useTheme sets the palette globally on purpose
renderProfile6 files in three/Tractable, but the parity grid is calibrated against classic; changing how the profile is resolved risks the frozen goldens for a benefit nobody has asked for
config + Dimensioning46 + 224 call sitesthe real work

Why the last row is the whole finding. The headline motivation — two designs side by side — needs units and scale to be per-instance. Doing the other three delivers none of it. Dimensioning is a pure static API with 224 call sites across 15 source files, 130 of them in a characterization suite that pins the static form; floorplanner/ alone holds 34 Configuration reads and 35 Dimensioning calls.

THE DESIGN, IN STAGES — EACH SHIPPABLE ALONE
  • What makes it possible at all: Corner already takes floorplan as its first constructor argument, and Floorplan is the only factory for Corners and Walls — a Wall reaches one through this.start.floorplan. So the model layer needs no new plumbing, only a config on the Floorplan. It also has just 3 Configuration reads
  • Stage 1 — Floorplan accepts an optional config and the model layer's 3 reads go through it
  • Stage 2, the crux — Dimensioning gains an instance form and the existing statics delegate to a module-default instance. That is the trick that makes 224 call sites a non-event: they all keep working untouched, the 130-test suite keeps passing, and only a view that wants its own units switches over. The same pattern render_profile.js and floorplannerPalette already use
  • Stage 3 — floorplanner/ takes its config from the Floorplan it is drawing
  • Stage 4 — renderProfile per Main, re-baselining the parity grid deliberately
LANDED — P7, ALL FOUR STAGES
  • The estimate was right and the design was the whole job. Every stage rests on one move: the class gains an instance form and the existing statics become one-line forwards to a module-level default. Nothing had to be migrated for that to work, so the 169 Dimensioning. calls and 133 Configuration. calls still in the test suite are untouched and still pass — they read the shared default, which is what they always did. 59 this.configuration, 43 this.dimensioning and 45 this.renderProfile reads exist beside them
  • Stage 1 — Floorplan takes an optional Configuration and carries a Dimensioning bound to it; Model and BlueprintJS thread it. The model layer's three reads go through configurationOf(floorplan), which falls back to the default so a Corner built by hand still behaves as it did. As predicted, three lines: Corner already took a floorplan, and a Wall reaches one through start.floorplan
  • Stage 2 — Dimensioning gains the instance form, statics forward. The 224-call-site number that made this a phase of its own turned out not to need a single edit
  • Stage 3 — floorplanner/ reads through the plan it draws: 46 Configuration reads, 45 Dimensioning calls, one getter pair per class. One of the 45 was inside an Image.onload where this is the image, not the sheet — a mechanical rewrite would have produced a silent undefined there, so the closures were found first and converted by hand
  • Stage 4 — renderProfile per Main, threaded to Skybox, Lights, Floorplan3D and through it to every Edge and Floor. The parity risk this table flagged did not materialise, and not by luck: the same delegate-to-a-default pattern means the default resolution path is identical rather than equivalent, and the parity harness imports nothing from the library — it drives a served page. A viewer only has its own look if it is handed one
  • There were five singletons, not four. wallInformation — the wall-measurement flags and label prefixes the 2D view reads — was missed by the survey and found by Stage 3, because it was the only thing left in floorplanner_view.js still reaching for module scope. Same treatment; the export is now the default configuration's own object, by identity
  • 25 tests, and the shape of them is the point. Every assertion is a pair that must not interfere — not "this design reads 250" but "this one reads 250 while that one reads 300", which the singleton cannot pass. 11 of the 21 headless ones fail if Floorplan ignores its configuration argument. Four more run in chromium and ask the same question of the pixels, because a per-instance value computed correctly and then drawn through a shared one would satisfy every number above
  • Coverage moved on all four measures at once — 76.38 lines, 76.48 statements, 63.43 branches, 74.31 functions — which is what a change touching the model, the 2D view and the 3D view does when it arrives with a suite of its own. 931 headless tests, 27 in the browser
WHAT IS STILL SHARED, DELIBERATELY
  • floorplannerPalette. The one singleton left, and the re-scope table above already called it "tractable, low value". Nothing changed that: the application has one 2D view, useTheme sets the palette globally on purpose, and two plans on a page almost certainly want to look alike. It is a page-level theme rather than a per-design setting, and it is now the only thing in floorplanner/ that is
  • The application itself takes no configuration. App.vue builds one design and reads the statics in 26 places; passing it a configuration of its own would change nothing except which object those 26 reads resolve to. The point of P7 is that an embedder can, not that everybody must

R-03 · Configuration broadcasts nothing med

Every other mutation in the library announces itself through EventDispatcher. Configuration is a class of static methods over a plain object, so a consumer that wants to react to a unit or scale change has to poll or be told out of band.

That is what killed the settings-panel zoom slider — it read Number(config.scale) off a non-reactive object, rendered 1 once, then sat there reading 1 while the plan was at 300%. It was removed rather than fixed, because there was nothing to hook.

LANDED — P3
  • EVENT_CONFIG_CHANGED, carrying the key, the new value and the old — and only on an actual change. Callers write from watchers and computed setters that re-run for unrelated reasons, so announcing a no-op write turns one user action into a redraw storm and a two-way bound control into a loop
  • Not by extending EventDispatcher: Configuration is a namespace of statics that nobody instantiates, so extending it would mean nothing. One module-level dispatcher with the listener API forwarded as statics gives callers the same addEventListener they use everywhere else
  • This replaced a characterization test asserting the opposite. It described a real gap rather than a quirk worth keeping, so the expectation changed with the code — which is what the preserve-or-fix policy asks for. Wall snapshotting is unchanged: broadcasting a change is not reacting to one
  • The payoff was three stale controls, not the zoom slider. Snap-to-grid, snap distance and grid resolution are writable from the plan overlay too, and the panel read them once at mount. Two tests pin it, and both fail without the fix. The zoom slider stays deleted — it could be made to work now, but the plan has its own readout, stops, fit, wheel and shortcut

R-04 · A texture per wall per redraw, never released high

Edge.updateTexture() runs new TextureLoader().load(url, callback) — a fresh loader and a fresh Texture every call. It is wired to redraw(), which is wired to EVENT_REDRAW. Nothing disposes the old one, so every redraw of a room leaks one GPU texture per wall surface. edge.js:29 loads the shared wall lightmap once per Edge rather than once per scene; Floor does the same for floors.

The 3D view has otherwise-careful disposal — Skybox, Lights, Controller and Main all release properly. Textures are the gap.

LANDED — P3
  • Refcounted per URL, as proposed — but handing out Texture.clone()s rather than one shared Texture. The obvious cache is wrong here: a wall sets repeat from its own width and height, so two walls sharing one Texture would both take whichever set it last. A clone keeps repeat, wrap and colorSpace per surface while .source — the decoded image and the GPU upload behind it — is shared. Verified against three rather than assumed, including that a clone taken before the image lands still receives it
  • Two further leaks surfaced. Floor never removed its EVENT_CHANGED listener, and Floorplan3D.redraw() discarded floors with removeFromScene(), which only takes them out of the scene graph — so every edit that rebuilt the plan left another subscribed floor holding another texture. Floorplan3D had no dispose() at all, so a torn-down viewer stayed subscribed to the model and redrew a scene it no longer belonged to
  • Six tests. The one that matters redraws every edge five times and asserts the handle count does not move; putting the missing release back makes it read 58 where it should read 18

R-05 · The 2D canvas repaints inside pointermove med

There is no requestAnimationFrame anywhere in the 2D path — the only one in the library is Main's render loop. view.draw() is called synchronously from fourteen sites, three reached from the pointermove handler. Every mouse movement is a full canvas repaint on the input thread, and on a large plan that is where dragging starts to feel heavy.

LANDED — P6
  • Fourteen sites was the count of call sites, not of repaints. A drag reaches all three of the pointermove draws on every single event — updateTarget, then the pan branch, then the drag branch — so a press and three moves cost ten full canvas repaints, not four. Measured rather than reasoned about: the no-frame-clock test asserts the exact number, so the figure this paragraph quotes is under test
  • invalidate() as proposed, plus flush(), which the proposal did not anticipate and the tests immediately needed — without it the only way to wait out a coalesced draw is to await a frame, which is neither synchronous nor deterministic. draw() is deliberately untouched: still immediate, still public, because embedders and the browser tier read the canvas straight after calling it
  • Not mechanical, and not covered by the existing suite. One test failed, which was the behaviour change announcing itself, and it became the proof of the fix rather than a casualty. Twelve new jsdom tests on a hand-driven frame clock (installFrameClock, because jsdom's own rAF fires off a 16 ms timer and every assertion would have been a race) and three more in chromium against the compositor's real one. Verified by making invalidate() synchronous again: eight fail
  • The ResizeObserver notice P5 had to swallow is gone, and so is the swallow. Both observers — FloorplannerView2D's and Main's — resized a canvas from inside the callback watching its own container, which is a layout write during observation and is precisely what ResizeObserver loop completed with undelivered notifications reports. Measuring stays in the callback where it is correct; only the write moved to the frame. tests/browser/setup.js is deleted, so tier 2 now fails on any window error. Proven by putting both defects back and watching the run break

R-06 · The package ships raw source, three as a hard dependency med

main points at src/scripts/blueprint.js and files publishes src/scripts — 47 files, 110 kB packed, unbundled and untyped. No sideEffects hint (so a bundler cannot tree-shake confidently), no subpath exports, and no engines, .nvmrc or packageManager pinning the toolchain that CI pins to Node 22 while the dev machine runs 24.

The sharper problem: three is a dependency. An embedder who already uses three gets a second copy — two class identities, instanceof silently false across the boundary, several hundred kB duplicated. This is the exact failure the S4 notes describe from the old bundled loader repacks, reintroduced one level up.

PROPOSAL
  • three and bezier-js → peerDependencies with peerDependenciesMeta
  • Publish a built dist/ ESM entry beside the IIFE; emit .d.ts from the JSDoc (R-07)
  • Add sideEffects: false and engines

R-07 · The JSDoc is decorative med

The library is thoroughly annotated — @param, @returns, @property, even @type {import('./useBlueprint.js').BlueprintStore}. None of it is verified. A wrong type costs nothing and drifts silently, and the annotations are load-bearing for anyone reading the code.

LANDED — P2
  • 40 files checked, 0 errors: all 12 of core/, all 16 composables, blueprint.js, and the 11 SFCs that were already clean. Opt-in is // @ts-check on the first line — inside <script setup> for a component, which is also what makes vue-tsc check that component's template at all
  • It found real defects on the first pass. BlueprintJS's constructor documented five options that did not exist — @param {Object} - options, where a stray hyphen swallowed the parameter name, so every options.* line attached to nothing. Main.controller was typed as permanently null because init() assigns it through a scope alias, which made getController() useless to every caller the checker looked at. threeCanvasElement was documented string while the application passes null. Two JSDoc types named three classes their module never imported
  • And it rediscovered the polygon-predicate bug family independently, from four arity mismatches, with no knowledge of the S0 characterization suite that had already pinned them. Running them confirms it: pointInPolygon, polygonPolygonIntersect and polygonInsidePolygon are constant false, polygonOutsidePolygon is constant true, and pointInPolygon2 — the sibling nobody refactored — is the only correct one. All four are pinned with @ts-expect-error rather than corrected, which fails if somebody fixes a signature without re-baselining the characterization tests. A pin, not a silencer. The ledger is at the top of core/utils.js
  • tsconfig.build.json emits 44 declaration files, carrying the JSDoc through. Not wired into the package yet — that is P4 — but proven while the reason was fresh
AS PROPOSED — read T-01 before acting on it
  • checkJs against the existing JSDoc. No .ts files, no source rewrite — a tsconfig.json with allowJs, checkJs, noEmit, opted in file by file with // @ts-check
  • Plus declaration + emitDeclarationOnly to produce the .d.ts R-06 wants
  • §09 parked this as "low-friction if ever wanted". Worth un-parking: it is the cheapest gate on the list by a wide margin, and the JavaScript-not-TypeScript decision stays intact
§ 12 · TECHNOLOGY DECISIONS

Four questions worth asking in August 2026

Three of the answers are "not yet", and the reasons matter more than the verdicts. Sources listed in the footer; all claims dated.

T-01 · ADOPT, CAREFULLY

TypeScript — checkJs, but pin to 6.x, not 7

TypeScript 7.0 went stable on 8 July 2026 — the Go-native compiler, measured at 8–12× faster, VS Code's own full check dropping 126s → 10.6s. npm view typescript version gives 7.0.2 today.

It is the wrong version for this project. 7.0 ships no stable programmatic API — that lands in 7.1, several months out — so typescript-eslint and the template type-checkers behind Vue, Svelte and Astro cannot run on it. With 29 single-file components, losing vue-tsc is disqualifying.

Use typescript@6 + vue-tsc. Revisit 7.x when 7.1 restores the API — the tsconfig.json written now carries over unchanged.

T-02 · WATCH

Vue 3.6 & Vapor — take the bump, skip the hype

3.6 hit RC on 18 July 2026 with Vapor feature-complete and a reactivity core rewritten on alien-signals; stable expected Q4. Vapor drops the virtual DOM for opted-in components and reports 2–4× on render-heavy trees.

This app will barely notice. Its cost is canvas rasterisation and WebGL, not vDOM diffing — the shell is a rail, a dock and a status bar.

Take 3.6 when stable, for the reactivity and memory improvements, which are free. Vapor is not the lever here; R-05 is.

T-03 · HOLD

three.js WebGPU — do not migrate yet

WebGPURenderer is still marked experimental in r185. WebGLRenderer remains the recommendation for WebGL2 applications, though it is now in maintenance — no larger features planned, and the nodes/TSL material system is WebGPU-only going forward. A slow-moving cliff, not a present risk.

Worth recording: render_profile.js, written for the classic/studio split, is precisely the abstraction a backend swap would use, and the renderer is constructed in one place behind a factory the tests already stub. When WebGPU stabilises this is a contained change — an argument the profile abstraction earned its keep.

T-04 · ADOPT — THE REAL GAP

Vitest 4 browser mode

Browser mode went fully stable in Vitest 4, with visual regression via toMatchScreenshot and Playwright trace support built in. It is already installed — vitest@4.1.10 is in the tree.

Ten of the seventeen suites run under jsdom, where canvas.getContext('2d') is a stub that records call names and WebGL is a renderer stub. The 2D view's actual pixels have never been rendered in a test, and the studio profile has never been composited in one. The npm run parity harness does drive real Chrome — manually, and calibrated against the classic profile the app no longer uses.

Caveat from the docs, worth designing around: screenshots are environment-sensitive (fonts, GPU, browser build), so baselines must be captured in the same container CI runs and stored per platform.

§ 13 · BUILD GATES & SEQUENCE

What the gates do not catch

CI runs test, lint and the library build, on dev and master. Here is the full surface, and what each gap has already cost or would cost.

GapSevWhat it means today
Exit code unwatchedhighDemonstrated in §10. 870 green assertions, exit 1, nobody the wiser — because CI never runs on the branch the work happens on
No demo build in CIhighbuild:demo runs only in the deploy job, on master. A broken Vue template or Tailwind token reaches master before anything notices
No docs build in CImedSame shape. VitePress failures surface at deploy time
No type checkmedSee R-07. Extensive JSDoc, zero verification. Closed by P2 for 40 files; the ledger of the rest is in tsconfig.json
No coverage floormed870 tests and no idea which lines they reach. No @vitest/coverage-v8 installed, so the number was unknown — measuring it was step one, not setting a threshold. Measured: lines 74.91%, statements 75.07%, branches 61.51%, functions 73.05%. Thinnest areas: items/ 48%, floorplanner/ 64%, three/ 74%
No real-browser testhighCanvas 2D is a call recorder; WebGL is a stub. Neither renderer's output is ever checked. See T-04. Closed by P5
No visual regressionhighThe 3D view was just substantially rebuilt. The parity grid that would catch a regression is manual and calibrated against the profile no longer shipped. Partly closed by P5: the renderers are now checked by pixel readback, but screenshot diffing across the chrome is still absent — see the tier 2 note
No bundle budgetmedThe demo is a single 1,129 kB chunk (305 kB gzip) with no manualChunks, and three is eagerly imported even in plan-only
No asset budgetmedpublic/ is 16 MB, dist-demo/ 25 MB. Garden.png alone is 3.4 MB — a PNG environment map. The .glb models carry no Draco or meshopt compression. Closed by P6: public/ is 10.62 MB and dist-demo/ 17.22 MB, and the budget that would have caught it — a ceiling on the largest single file — now exists. Draco and meshopt were not the lever; see §13
No accessibility checkmed29 components, 16 aria-labels, 12 roles, one tabindex, two focus-visible rules. Reka UI supplies much of this for free — which is exactly why an automated check is worth having: it tells you where it did not. Closed by P5, and it did: no <main> landmark at all, a duplicate banner and contentinfo from dialog headers, and 122 thumbnails whose alt repeated their own button's name
No PR triggermedDeliberate, to conserve minutes. The consequence is that nothing runs before a merge, which is how the exit-1 would have reached dev
No dependency policylowNo Dependabot, no Renovate, no SECURITY.md. npm audit is clean for production; 3 dev-only advisories (VitePress, no fix available)
No provenancelowNot published yet. If it is: npm trusted publishing over GitHub OIDC is GA, needs no long-lived token, and attaches SLSA Build L3 attestation automatically

Status. P1 has landed. Eight of the thirteen rows above are closed: the exit code is watched (coverage run, thresholds enforced), the demo and docs builds are in CI, the coverage floor is recorded, both budgets are enforced, and the no-PR-trigger gap is covered from the other side by the tier 0 hook rather than by paying for PR runs. P2 has since closed the type-check row for 40 files. P5 then closed the real-browser and accessibility rows and half of visual regression, and P6 closed the asset budget row — adding the per-file ceiling that would have caught it in the first place. Three remain open: visual regression across the chrome, which needs a containerised baseline, and unscheduled — dependency policy and provenance. The table is left as the diagnosis rather than rewritten, so what was found stays legible next to what was done.

The proposed gate ladder

Built around the standing constraint that Actions minutes are worth conserving. The design principle: push the cheap, high-yield checks to the developer's machine where they cost nothing, and spend runner minutes only on what genuinely needs a runner.

TIER 0 · LOCAL PRE-COMMIT — 0 ACTIONS MINUTES, ~2s ON STAGED FILES — BUILT
  • ESLint on staged .js / .vue — errors block, the 28 deliberate warnings do not, matching what npm run lint does in CI
  • vitest related --run --passWithNoTests on the staged files' suites
  • npm run typecheck — a whole-project check on every commit, however many files were staged. It is the one task here that cannot take a file list: tsc checks a program, and handing it individual paths makes it ignore tsconfig.json and answer a different question from CI. Under two seconds, so there is no reason to want the cheaper wrong answer. That is why the lint-staged config moved out of package.json into lint-staged.config.mjs — only a function task can decline the appended paths
  • Via simple-git-hooks (lighter than Husky, one dev dependency) plus lint-staged, installed by "prepare": "simple-git-hooks". Two wrong turns worth recording, because both looked right and neither was: a prepare wrapper that skipped when CI was set did nothing, since the dependency's own postinstall had already written the hook and honours SKIP_INSTALL_SIMPLE_GIT_HOOKS rather than CI; dropping prepare and leaving it to that postinstall then failed the other way, because npm runs a dependency's postinstall only when the dependency is actually installed, so npm install on an unchanged tree left no hook. prepare runs every time, the CLI honours the same variable the postinstall does, and the workflows set it on the install step — one variable, both paths. All three cases verified from clean clones
  • Escape hatches, because a gate with no override gets disabled wholesale: git commit --no-verify for one commit, SKIP_SIMPLE_GIT_HOOKS=1 to neuter the hook, SKIP_INSTALL_SIMPLE_GIT_HOOKS=1 to not install it

This is the single highest-value item in this document. It catches the §10 class of failure on the machine that caused it, and it makes the no-PR-trigger policy safe rather than merely cheap.

TIER 1 · EVERY CI RUN — ~90s, REPLACED THE PREVIOUS 3 STEPS — BUILT
  • Test with coverage — @vitest/coverage-v8, thresholds in vitest.config.mjs set from the first measured run, ratcheted, never lowered
  • Lint — as before; eslint-plugin-vuejs-accessibility lands with the P5 accessibility work rather than here
  • All three builds — library, demo and docs
  • Size budgets — tools/check-budget.mjs against limits committed in tools/budget.json: demo JS and CSS gzipped, the library IIFE gzipped, and raw bytes for dist-demo/ and public/. Gzip for what is served as text, raw for trees of already-compressed images and models. JS is summed across every file in the assets directory rather than matched by name, so P6's code splitting cannot read as a saving
  • Typecheck — vue-tsc --noEmit alone, not tsc as well: vue-tsc is a superset that checks the .js files exactly as tsc does and checks SFC templates against their script blocks. Verified by breaking each in turn rather than assumed
  • Coverage and budget numbers go to the run summary rather than an uploaded artifact, which keeps the workflow's promise to consume no repository storage
  • Still one job: the expensive part is npm ci, and splitting the builds across parallel jobs would pay for it three times to save perhaps thirty seconds. The deploy job also runs the budgets — before it folds the docs into dist-demo/, since that would otherwise inflate the tree it is measuring
TIER 2 · REAL BROWSER — SEPARATE JOB, RUNS IN PARALLEL — BUILT
  • Vitest browser mode with the Playwright chromium provider. Note for anyone reading the older docs: Vitest 4 takes a provider factory from @vitest/browser-playwright, not the string 'playwright' — the string form fails at startup
  • The 2D plan, rasterised. Seven tests that draw a real room into a real canvas and read the pixels back: the ground is the themed background, a themed grid draws in the themed colour, the room fills, a palette change repaints without reconstructing the view. The first tests anywhere that check the palette against output rather than against a recorded strokeStyle
  • The 3D view, composited. Six tests through a real WebGL2 context — SwiftShader on a runner with no GPU — reading the framebuffer with readPixels. That the frame is not a flat clear colour, that the studio profile composites and not only classic, that the two profiles differ by more than 5% of pixels, and that moving the camera changes the picture
  • axe-core over the booted application, in every layout and with the catalog drawer open. Drawing surfaces excluded by selector rather than by disabling rules, so a missing button label anywhere else still fails
TIER 2 · WHAT WAS DROPPED, AND WHY

No toMatchScreenshot. The tier was specified with screenshot diffing and it is not there. Two reasons, and the second is the one that would still hold with unlimited time:

  • A baseline cannot honestly be produced here. Reference images are environment-bound — font rasterisation, GPU and browser build all move the bytes, which is why Vitest names them per platform and why its own documentation asks for a container. CI is Linux; this machine is macOS with no Docker. Committing a macOS baseline gives the Linux runner nothing to compare against, and the first CI run would fail creating one — with no way to retrieve it, because this workflow deliberately uploads no artifacts
  • Pixel assertions are the better test for this content anyway. What these tests want to know is "does the grid draw in the grid colour" — a contract, checkable by reading a known coordinate and comparing it to the palette value the library exports. That is deterministic everywhere, stores nothing, and when it fails it names the wrong colour instead of reporting that 0.3% of pixels moved

What screenshots would add over this is CSS and layout regression across the chrome, which the pixel tests do not cover. That is real, and it is the case for revisiting — with a containerised baseline job, so the reference is generated in the same environment that compares it. Until then npm run parity remains the visual-diff tool, and it is still manual and still calibrated against classic.

TIER 3 · SCHEDULED & ON DEMAND — WEEKLY / workflow_dispatch
  • Full parity grid against the frozen goldens
  • Lighthouse against the deployed Pages site
  • npm audit and Dependabot, grouped weekly so it does not become noise

The asset half of P6 done

The gap row said “the .glb models carry no Draco or meshopt compression”, and that framing turned out to be looking at the wrong thing. The 165 models hold 5.2 MB between them. The textures hold 9.8 MB, and 6.6 MB of that sat in 23 PNGs with no alpha channel at all — photographs and baked lightmaps in a lossless container. The other 152 PNGs carry real transparency, are catalog thumbnails, and total under 1 MB. There is no cleverness in the fix: PNG is the wrong container for continuous-tone imagery, and re-encoding 21 of those files as JPEG took public/ from 15.03 MB to 10.62 MB — a 29% reduction in everything served, against maybe 15–20% for Draco on geometry that is a third of the weight.

LANDED — P6
  • Quality is measured, not asserted. There is no image library on this machine and none in the dependency tree — no sharp, no ImageMagick, no PIL — so sips encodes and chromium decodes: playwright is already a devDependency, and every conversion is read back and compared with its original pixel by pixel. asset-pipeline/texture-compression.json records PSNR, mean absolute error and worst single channel per file. Garden clears 38.0 dB at 3434 kB → 844 kB; the baked model maps run 37.8–47.3 dB. A test fails any conversion under 36 dB, so the floor is enforced rather than remembered
  • The finding that changed the scope: rooms/textures/ is a public URL contract. Those paths are serialized into saved designs — floorplan.newFloorTextures carries a url, and the fixtures under tests/fixtures name rooms/textures/hardwood.png because real saved plans do. Renaming a file there breaks documents that already exist, and nothing in the repository would have noticed. hardwood.png is 476 kB of photograph in a lossless container — exactly what a compression pass reaches for — and it stays. rooms/textures/envs/ is exempt and carries the single biggest win: no catalog offers it, so no design can name it, and its only reference in the project is Skybox.defaultEnvironment
  • tests/asset-integrity.test.js is the gate that finding earned. Every asset URL in every saved fixture must resolve — through resolveModelUrl first, so the S3 legacy shim is checked too. Every image URI in all 165 .glb files must resolve beside it and declare the matching mime type. Every catalog entry must exist. Verified by renaming hardwood.png and watching it fail by name. A missed reference is otherwise a runtime 404 and nothing else: TextureLoader logs and carries on, GLTFLoader renders the base colour, and the application looks like it works
  • Budgets ratcheted down, which is a direction a budget almost never moves. public-total 16.5 → 11.16 MB, demo-total 24 → 18.12 MB; leaving the old ceilings would have handed the next change 4 MB of slack it did not earn. And a new one: public-largest, a ceiling on the biggest single file, which is the limit that would have caught this. The tree was 15.03 MB against a 16.5 MB ceiling — comfortably inside budget — while 22% of it was one photograph. A tree total cannot see that
  • A characterization test had to be re-read rather than re-baselined. “Copies textures byte-for-byte rather than re-encoding them” is a claim about the S3 converter — that it ran in Node with no canvas round-trip — and that claim is untouched. What changed is that byte-equality with the source stopped being the way to observe it for 12 maps. The test now pins the re-encoded set to exactly what P6 recorded, so a texture cannot quietly stop matching its source without being on that list, and conversion-report.json is left alone: it is what S3 did, and editing it to stay current would destroy the thing it is for
NOT DONE, AND WHY
  • No Draco, no meshopt. Both need a decoder wired into GLTFLoader — a WASM blob fetched at runtime and a loader configuration — to compress the third of the payload that is geometry, after the two thirds that is texture has just come down 45%. It is the right next step for the model tree and it is a change to the load path, which is its own review. The numbers to beat are recorded
  • No resizing. The three 1500×2297 wood textures are 2.2 MB of already-lossy JPEG and are plainly larger than a wardrobe rendered a few hundred pixels tall needs; halving them would save roughly 1.6 MB. That is a resolution decision — it changes what the renderer samples — and P5's tier proves composition, not fidelity. It belongs to whoever owns the art direction. Logged in the report with its numbers rather than taken quietly
  • No code splitting. The budget rationale in the tier 1 block above refers to “P6's code splitting”; that was never in the phase description and is not here. The measurement it anticipated is in place regardless — JS is summed across the assets directory rather than matched by name, so splitting one chunk into several still cannot read as a saving

Sequence

Ordered so each phase is independently shippable and each one makes the next safer. Estimates are for a single developer at the cadence RM-001 ran at.

Roughly four to five weeks end to end, and the first phase is the one that matters most. If only one thing gets done: P1. A pre-commit hook and three extra CI steps would have caught the only live defect in this review, for zero recurring cost.

ARCHITECTURE HARDENING · DRAWING Nº RM-003

Architecture Hardening Program

Six sequential sprints that turn architect3d from a viewer that works into a document editor that can be trusted with somebody's work. Every finding below was measured against the working tree, not inferred — the numbers in §16 come from running the library, and the ones that surprised me are marked.

§ 14 · EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

One theme: nothing owns anything

RM-002 fixed seven specific defects. This is the pattern underneath them, and it is a single one — the library has no concept of ownership, so nothing knows what it is responsible for releasing, replacing, cancelling or identifying.

6
meshes, geometries and materials abandoned by every Floorplan.update() — measured, one square room
25
EVENT_UPDATED dispatches to open a four-wall file. Each one is a full 3D teardown and a camera recentre
3
geometry.dispose() / material.dispose() call sites in the whole of src/
4/4
malformed documents that destroy the open design. One of them does not even throw

The load-bearing sentence in this document is that these are not five problems. A resource leaks because no object was ever declared its owner; a stale callback lands because no load was ever declared current; a room loses its name because identity was derived instead of assigned; undo re-downloads a sofa because history has no unit smaller than the document. Each sprint below installs one missing ownership relation, and the ones after it get cheaper because it exists.

What this program is not. It is not a rewrite, and §18 says so at length and with guardrails. Every sprint keeps the public EventDispatcher surface, keeps every static API working, and is independently shippable and independently revertible. The pattern that made RM-002 P7 a non-event — an instance form, with the existing statics forwarding to a module default — is the same pattern used throughout, because it has now been proven on this codebase against 302 call sites that did not have to move.

Architecture health metrics

Nine numbers. Every one is measurable today, most of them are measured below, and each sprint moves specific rows. A metric nobody can compute is a slogan, so the "how" column is not decoration.

NºMetricTodayTargetHow it is measuredSprint
M-1GPU geometries retained after 50 edit cyclesgrows0 growthrenderer.info.memory.geometries before/after — three r185 increments it on upload and decrements it on disposeA0
M-2GPU textures retained after 50 edit cyclesgrows0 growthrenderer.info.memory.textures, plus textureCacheStats() handlesA0
M-3Model-layer meshes abandoned per update()60Identity-set over room.floorPlane/roofPlane and halfEdge.plane across N updatesA0
M-4EVENT_UPDATED per document open251Counter on floorplan, one loadSerialized of simple-room.blueprint3dA1 met
M-5Full Floorplan3D.redraw() per corner drag3 per event0projectionStats().full and cameraStats().recentred over a ten-step drag. Measured at 3, not 1: a corner with two walls announced itself three times — see LANDED — A2A2 met
M-6Malformed documents that destroy the open design10 of 100 of 10The corpus in §16 H-2, grown to ten, asserted as exportSerialized() byte-identical after the failureA1 met

On M-1 and M-2. These are the two that make the whole program checkable rather than argued about, and they cost nothing to add: renderer.info.memory is {geometries, textures}, maintained by the renderer itself — incremented where a resource is uploaded, decremented in the dispose handlers. Verified in the installed three@0.185.1 rather than taken from the docs. A test that mounts a viewer, edits fifty times and asserts the counter came back is the honest form of “we fixed the leaks”.

§ 15 · CURRENT & TARGET STATE

What owns what, before and after

Both diagrams show the same six layers. The difference is entirely in the arrows and in one new object.

Current state

Dashed borders mark objects that hold GPU or async resources nobody releases. The dotted arrows are the ones that make the current design hard to reason about: a view reaching across a layer boundary to add a mesh the model built, and a loader callback writing into whatever scene exists when it happens to return.

Target state

One new object, DesignRuntime, and one new contract, ChangeSet. Everything else is existing code given an owner. The runtime is not a god object: it holds no design data, only the six services a document needs and the identity that says which document they belong to.

src/app — Vue 3
selection by id, resolved against the current graph · history is a command stack with checkpoints · autosave through an async repository
▼ subscribes to typed ChangeSet, not to a bare event ▼

configuration · dimensioning · renderProfile · assetResolver · loadSession · resourceRegistry · documentId
legacy statics still resolve to the module-default runtime, so an embedder that never heard of this keeps working

▲ every GPU object created is registered to one owning registry, refcounted where shared ▲
ResourceRegistry
per viewer · refcounts shared
masters · dispose is a boundary,
not a call site
LoadSession

per scene · abort() is real in r185
stale callbacks rejected
AssetResolver
manifest → versioned URL
save files keep logical names
availability + preload policy
DesignDocument — validate · migrate · then swap
parse → validate → migrate → build off to the side → atomically replace. A document that fails never touches live state.

The one-sentence difference. Today a document load mutates the design in place and hopes; afterwards it builds a replacement and commits it. Everything else in this program follows from being able to say that sentence.

§ 16 · DECISION LOG

Eight findings, with the evidence

Every row cites a file and a line at fff1ebe. The four marked measured were produced by running the library, not by reading it — the script is throwaway and the numbers are reproducible from the description given.

NºFindingSevPrimary evidenceSprint
H-1Nothing disposes geometries or materials; the model layer leaks the hardesthighmodel/floorplan.js:816–878 · three/edge.js:85–97 · items/item.js:461A0
H-2A malformed document destroys the open design before it failshighmodel/model.js:53–61, 122–133A1
H-3Item callbacks land in whatever scene exists when they returnhighmodel/scene.js:199–212A1
H-4One undifferentiated EVENT_UPDATED drives five unrelated consumershighmodel/floorplan.js:877 · three/main.js:129, 403A2
H-5Room identity is derived from its corners, so editing a wall renames the roomhighmodel/room.js:53, 87–92 · model/wall.js:74, 202A3
H-6Undo is a document reload, so it re-fetches every modelmedapp/composables/useHistory.js:247–256A3
H-7The texture cache and the loaders are still process-widemedthree/main.js:525 · model/scene.js:37–38A4 closed
H-8Assets are unhashed, unversioned, and their URLs are a save-format contractmedpublic/ via Vite publicDir · index.html:13–31A5 closed

H-1 · Nothing disposes geometries or materials high

RM-002 R-04 fixed textures, thoroughly and with a refcounted cache. It did not touch geometries or materials, and the grep is stark: the entire src/ tree contains three geometry.dispose() or material.dispose() calls — two in three/controller.js:108–109 and one in three/skybox.js:208. Ten classes have a dispose(); Edge, Item, Room, HalfEdge, HUD, Scene, Model and Floorplan have none.

The worst offender is the model layer, which the documentation says holds no GPU resources at all. docs/architecture.md:71–74 states the model classes “hold no DOM node, no canvas and no GPU resource”, and blueprint.js:200–203 repeats it as the reason dispose() leaves the model standing. Both are wrong: Room.generatePlane() and generateRoofPlane() build a Mesh each (room.js:147, 159), and HalfEdge.generatePlane() builds a third (half_edge.js:240). They are invisible hit-test planes, which is presumably how they escaped the description — but a ShapeGeometry is a ShapeGeometry whether or not you can see it, and these are added to the live three scene by Floor.addToScene().

MEASURED — ONE SQUARE ROOM, 20 update() CALLS measured
QuantityCount
Room objects ever constructed21
Meshes ever constructed126
BufferGeometry ever constructed126
Material ever constructed126
Live at the end6
Abandoned, never disposed120

Six per update(), every time, with no user-visible change to the plan. Floorplan.update() sets this.rooms = [] and rebuilds every Room from scratch (floorplan.js:839, 846–874), and wall.resetFrontBack() nulls both HalfEdges on every wall (floorplan.js:833–835). The replaced objects hold three meshes between them and go out of scope holding their geometry and material. Combine this with H-4's twenty-five dispatches and opening a four-wall file abandons roughly 150 meshes, geometries and materials before the first frame is drawn.

The view layer leaks the same way, more visibly. Edge.remove() (edge.js:85–97) detaches three listeners and releases both textures — carefully, and that part is R-04's work — and then disposes none of the six meshes an Edge builds. Edge.redraw() calls removeFromScene() and immediately rebuilds, so every EVENT_REDRAW abandons another six. removeFromScene() is also asymmetric with addToScene(): it clears planes and basePlanes but not phantomPlanes (edge.js:118–132). Nothing pushes to phantomPlanes today, so it is latent rather than live — but it is the kind of latent that a debugging aid switched on for an afternoon turns into a duplicate-mesh bug.

Floor.dispose() (floor.js:188–194) detaches its listener, removes from the scene and releases the texture. The floor's ShapeGeometry, the roof's fan geometry and both materials survive. Floor.redraw() (floor.js:47–53) rebuilds both planes without disposing the pair it is replacing.

Item is the one with the clearest single defect. Item.removed() is an empty method (item.js:461–463), so Scene.removeItem() (scene.js:128–139) releases nothing at all: not the merged BufferGeometry, not the glTF materials or the textures GLTFLoader created for them — which never entered the refcounted cache and so are not covered by R-04 — not the two CanvasTextures, two PlaneGeometrys and two materials the dimension labels use, and not wirematerial. And one object is not merely undisposed but left in the scene: initObject() does this.scene.add(this.bhelper) (item.js:452–453) and nothing anywhere removes it. Delete a chair and its selection box remains a child of the three scene, holding a BoxHelper that points at an object no longer in the graph. errorGlow is milder but the same shape: createGlow() clones the entire item geometry (item.js:669) and hideError() only removes it from the scene.

The shared-cache boundary is now genuinely wrong, and the code says so. Main.dispose() ends with an unconditional clearTextureCache() (main.js:525), whose own comment reads: “Acceptable while two simultaneous viewers are not a supported configuration; it becomes R-02's problem when they are.” P7 made them supported and shipped tests/browser/two-designs.test.js to prove it. Disposing one viewer now empties the cache under every other live viewer on the page — each of which re-fetches and re-decodes every image it is still using on its next redraw. That is the exact condition the comment was written against.

DECISION — A REGISTRY PER OWNER, NOT MORE dispose() CALLS
  • Rejected: audit every construction site and add a matching dispose. That is what the current code is an attempt at, and it has three call sites and ten leaks. The failure mode is structural: redraw() paths construct in one method and discard in another, so the pairing is invisible at both ends. Adding thirty more calls produces thirty more places to forget one
  • Rejected: a scene-graph walk at teardown (scene.traverse, dispose everything found). It cannot see anything already detached — which is precisely what leaks here — and it will happily dispose a geometry another viewer is still drawing with
  • Chosen: an explicit ResourceRegistry, owned per viewer. Every geometry, material and texture is registered when created and released as a unit at a declared boundary. Shared entries are refcounted, which the texture cache already does correctly and is the model to copy. three's own guidance is the justification for taking this seriously: “three.js can not automatically clean these resources up… it's up to you to manage them”, and removing an object from the scene does not free anything
  • The ownership question the registry forces us to answer is the real deliverable. Floor.addToScene() adds this.room.floorPlane — a mesh built by the model, added by the view, owned by neither. Either the model owns hit-test geometry and the view borrows it, or the model stops building meshes. A0 picks the first, because the second changes picking

H-2 · A malformed document destroys the open design high

Model.loadSerialized() is six lines (model.js:53–61): dispatch EVENT_LOADING, JSON.parse, newRoom(), dispatch EVENT_LOADED. There is no validation and no version gate. newRoom() (model.js:122–133) then calls this.scene.clearItems() first, loadFloorplan() second — and loadFloorplan() opens with this.reset() (floorplan.js:635) before it has looked at anything. So the current design is gone before the new one has been examined at all.

MEASURED — OPEN A ROOM, THEN OPEN THIS measured
DocumentThrows?Design beforeDesign after
{}yes4 corners, 4 walls0, 0
{"floorplan":{"corners":{},"walls":[]}}yes4 corners, 4 walls0, 0
[]yes4 corners, 4 walls0, 0
{"items":[]}no4 corners, 4 walls0, 0

The fourth row is the one to look at. It does not throw, so EVENT_LOADED fires, the toast says the file opened, autosave writes the empty plan over the draft, and history records it as an edit. The application reports success and the work is gone. The other three at least surface an error — useDesignIO.loadDesign() catches it and shows “Could not open that design” (useDesignIO.js:130–143), which is true and also far too late.

Only a syntax error is safe, because JSON.parse throws before newRoom() runs. Every well-formed JSON document that is not a design is destructive.

H-3 · Item callbacks have no generation token high

Scene.addItem()'s loaderCallback closes over scope and pushes into scope.items (scene.js:199–212). Nothing records which document asked. Open design A with thirty items, then open design B while they are in flight: clearItems() empties the list, and thirty callbacks then resolve into B, adding A's furniture to B's scene and dispatching thirty EVENT_ITEM_LOADED. useHistory's settle gate counts those (useHistory.js:353–359), so the count it uses to decide when a restore has finished is measuring two documents at once. The same race covers New, Undo, Redo and viewer disposal.

The cancellation primitive already exists in the installed engine, and the library does not use it. three r185's LoadingManager has an abort() (three.core.js:44231) and FileLoader composes it — AbortSignal.any([this._abortController.signal, this.manager.abortController.signal]) at three.core.js:44620. GLTFLoader takes a manager and passes it down to its FileLoader and TextureLoader. But Scene constructs new GLTFLoader() and new OBJLoader() with no manager at all (scene.js:37–38), so every loader in every scene on the page shares the global DefaultLoadingManager — one shared abort surface for all documents, which is the same thing as none.

H-4 · One event, five consumers, no way to say what changed high

Floorplan.update() ends with a single EVENT_UPDATED (floorplan.js:877). Five things listen, and they want five different questions answered:

ConsumerLocationReactionWhat it actually needs
Floorplan3Dthree/floorPlan.js:26Tear down and rebuild every Floor and EdgeWhich walls and rooms changed
Mainthree/main.js:129, 403centerCamera() — moves the user's cameraOnly when the plan's extent changed, if ever
useHistoryuseHistory.js:362Debounced snapshot of the whole documentA committed edit boundary
useAutosaveuseAutosave.js:167Debounced full serialize + localStorage writeThe same boundary
2D viewfloorplanner/RepaintGeometry changes only

The second row is a live UX defect rather than a performance note: every corner drag recentres the 3D camera, because a corner move calls update() and Main cannot tell that from a document load.

And update() is called far more often than “when the plan changed” would suggest, because both factory methods call it — newCorner() at floorplan.js:374 and newWall() at floorplan.js:317. measured Opening simple-room.blueprint3d — four corners, four walls — dispatches EVENT_UPDATED 25 times; rich-design.blueprint3d dispatches it 39. Each one is a complete 3D teardown and rebuild plus a camera recentre. There is also an asymmetry worth fixing while we are here: removeWall() calls update() and removeCorner() does not (floorplan.js:389, 398–402).

The seed of the fix is already in the signature. update(updateroomconfiguration = true, updatecorners = null) is a crude two-level change description that some callers already use to skip room re-derivation. A typed ChangeSet is that idea finished, not a new concept imported from elsewhere.

H-5 · Identity is derived, so editing a wall renames the room high

Corner.id is a real assigned identity (corner.js:76). Nothing else in the model has one. Wall.id is [start.id, end.id].join() computed once at construction (wall.js:74, 202–205) — frozen, so it becomes a lie the moment a corner is merged, and two walls between the same pair of corners collide. Rooms are worse: they carry two derived keys that disagree with each other. Room.getUuid() sorts the corner ids (room.js:87–92) and backs floor textures; Room.roomByCornersId does not sort (room.js:53) and backs room names in metaroomsdata.

MEASURED — NAME A ROOM, TEXTURE IT, THEN DRAW A WALL THROUGH ONE OF ITS SIDES measured
  • Corners in the room: 4 → 5
  • getUuid() changed: yes — the floor texture no longer resolves
  • roomByCornersId changed: yes
  • Name survived: no — “Kitchen” became “A New Room”
  • The two keys agree with each other: no

Splitting a wall is an ordinary drawing action. It silently discards the room's name and its floor texture, and it does so through two separate key schemes that would have to be fixed independently. Floorplan.update() also replaces every Room object each call (21 objects for 21 updates, measured above), so useSelection's markRaw(evt.item) (useSelection.js:57) is holding a dead object the instant anything moves — the inspector then edits a room that is no longer in the plan.

H-6 · Undo is a document reload med

useHistory is well built and its own docblock argues the case honestly: snapshots cannot be subtly wrong, and command inverses for Corner.mergeWithIntersected would be a library rewrite. That reasoning was right when it was written and it is why A3 is staged rather than a rewrite. But the cost is now measurable: apply() calls loadSerialized() (useHistory.js:247–256), so every undo replays the entire load path — 25+ EVENT_UPDATED, a full 3D teardown per dispatch, and every item re-fetched, re-merged and re-uploaded. Undoing a corner nudge re-downloads the sofa. The count-based settle gate and its 8-second backstop (useHistory.js:84, 217–232) exist to manage exactly this.

There is also a fidelity gap already documented at docs/architecture.md:224–228: saveFloorplan() writes only corners its walls reach, so a corner with nothing attached is not in a snapshot and cannot be restored by one.

H-7 · Still process-wide after P7 med

P7 made configuration, dimensioning, the render profile, wallInformation and the unloadable-item count per-instance. Four things remain global, and the disposition differs:

GlobalLocationDisposition
Texture cache map + clearTextureCache()three/texture_cache.js:55, called main.js:525fix Sharing the decode is right; process-wide teardown is not. Registry-scoped release in A0, runtime-scoped in A4
GLTFLoader / OBJLoader on the default managermodel/scene.js:37–38fix A manager per scene is what makes abort() per document. A1
Scene.unloadableItemCount staticmodel/scene.js:329keep Documented process total, deliberately kept beside the instance field. No change
floorplannerPalettefloorplanner/floorplanner_view.jskeep P7 called it “tractable, low value” and nothing has changed. A page-level theme, not a per-design setting

H-8 · Assets are unhashed, and their URLs are a published contract med

168 catalog entries resolve to 165 .glb and 3 .gltf files, alongside 154 PNGs and 48 JPEGs under public/. Vite's documentation is explicit about what that directory means: assets there are “copied to the root of the dist directory as-is” — they do not enter the build graph and they never get hashed filenames, unlike imported assets which do. So the entire 10.62 MB payload is served under stable, unversioned names with no content addressing, and a changed model keeps its URL.

The obvious fix is unavailable, and that is the finding. These URLs are not internal: they are bare relative strings composed at runtime (index.html:13–31 explains why they must stay relative) and they are serialized into saved designs — model_url on every item, newFloorTextures[].url on every custom floor. P6 discovered the same thing about rooms/textures/ and built tests/asset-integrity.test.js to defend it. Hashing the filenames would break every document already saved. The indirection has to sit between the logical name in the file and the physical URL on the network, which is what an AssetResolver is.

Beyond caching, the gaps are: no availability policy (a 404 reaches R-01's failed() path and becomes a console line), no integrity, no preload or prefetch of likely items, no CDN-relocatable base, and no per-asset budget — P6 added a per-file ceiling on the tree, which is a different question from “what does placing this chair cost”.

Explicitly out of scope, restated from P6. Draco, meshopt and KTX2 are all supported by three r185's loader family and none is wired in. They remain the right next step for the geometry third of the payload and they are not part of A5, which is about delivery architecture rather than compression. A5 makes them cheap to add later by putting a manifest between the catalog and the network; it does not add them.

§ 17 · RISKS & DEPENDENCIES

What could go wrong, and what has to come first

Prioritized risk register

Ordered by exposure — likelihood against what it costs if it happens. The mitigation column is what makes each one survivable, not what makes it unlikely.

NºRiskImpactLikelihoodMitigation
K-1Over-disposal. A registry that releases a geometry another viewer or a pooled cache is still drawing with produces a black mesh or a WebGL error — worse than the leak it replaces, and intermittenthighmedRefcount everything shared, never dispose on a count above zero, and assert renderer.info.memory in both directions — it must come down after teardown and stay up while a second viewer lives. The two-viewer browser test P7 already shipped is the fixture
K-2Characterization drift. 931 headless tests pin current behaviour, several quirks deliberately. A ChangeSet that coalesces EVENT_UPDATED changes dispatch counts that tests may assert onhighhighThe standing rule from RM-001 §01 holds: a characterization failure is a signal to re-check the change. Where a count genuinely must change, retire the expectation deliberately with a changelog line, as the nine isVersionHigherThan tests were. A2 budgets for this explicitly
K-3Identity migration breaks old saves. Assigning stable ids means writing them into the save format; a document written before that has none, and the ids must be reconstructed deterministically or rooms lose their names on first openhighmedReconstruct from the existing derived keys on load — the old key becomes the seed of the new id, so a first open is a no-op and a subsequent save is upgraded. tests/fixtures/v1/ is the frozen corpus that proves it
K-4Incremental projection diverges from full redraw. A rebuild-only-what-changed path that misses a case leaves a stale wall on screen — and it will be a case nobody thought of, not a case they got wrongmedhighKeep redraw() as the reference implementation and ship a debug assertion that runs both and diffs the resulting scene graph. P5's browser tier compares frames, which is the only honest oracle here
K-5Command history gets an inverse subtly wrong, producing a design that is quietly incorrect rather than an undo that visibly fails — the exact objection useHistory's docblock raiseshighmedNever remove the snapshot. Commands are an optimisation layered over checkpoints; every N commands is a real snapshot, and a round-trip property test asserts apply(invert(c), apply(c, s)) === s on the serialized form. A command that cannot prove that stays a snapshot
K-6Async persistence loses a write on unload. IndexedDB is asynchronous, and pagehide does not wait for a promisemedhighThis is the one place Web Storage still earns its keep: a small synchronous recovery pointer written on pagehide, with the document body in IndexedDB. Justified in A5 rather than assumed
K-7Scope creep into a rewrite. Every finding here touches the same files, and “while we are in here” is how a hardening program becomes a rewritehighmed§18's guardrails, and the rule that each sprint ships alone. If a sprint cannot be described as a single revertible commit range, it is too big
K-8Public API breakage for an embedder using the documented event surfacemedlow§18. Every existing event keeps firing with its existing payload; ChangeSet is additive. The adapter is tested against the event table in docs/events.md row by row
K-9Coverage floor blocks a sprint — thresholds are a ratchet and new abstract seams are easy to under-testlowmedEach sprint's DoD includes its own tests. The floor is never lowered; if a file genuinely cannot be covered it is excluded explicitly with a reason, per vitest.config.mjs

Cross-sprint dependency graph

Strictly sequential in one place only — A2 needs A0's registry, because incremental projection means disposing what it replaces. Everything else has one hard edge and some soft ones.

A0 Resource ownership ── no dependencies. Start here. │ ├──▶ A1 Transactional loading ── soft: teardown of a rejected document │ │ wants a registry to release into │ │ │ └──▶ A3 Identity + history ── HARD: command boundaries are │ ▲ LoadSession boundaries │ │ ├──▶ A2 ChangeSet ──────────────┘ HARD on A0: incremental projection │ │ must dispose what it replaces, or it │ │ converts a redraw leak into a churn leak │ │ │ └──▶ A4 DesignRuntime ── soft: cleaner once ChangeSet exists, │ but P7 already did the hard half │ └──▶ A5 Persistence + assets ── HARD on A1 (validate/migrate is the schema pipeline) · soft on A4 Critical path: A0 → A2 → A3 A4 and A5 can run beside it.
WHY THIS ORDER AND NOT THE OBVIOUS ONE
  • A0 first, though H-2 is scarier. Data loss is the worse defect and the instinct is to fix it first. But A1's atomic swap means building a replacement document and then discarding one of the two — and discarding a document is exactly the operation that has no owner today. Doing A1 first means writing the teardown twice
  • A2 before A3, though identity feels more foundational. Stable ids are only observable through something that consumes them, and the ChangeSet is that consumer: “room r_7f3 changed” is the payload that makes an id worth having. Doing A3 first produces ids nothing reads
  • A4 last of the structural four, though it is the headline feature. P7 already delivered the half an embedder can see. What remains is collecting services into one object, which is a refactor that wants its collaborators to exist first
  • A5 can start any time after A1 and is the natural parallel track for a second pair of hands, because it touches almost none of the same files
§ 18 · COMPATIBILITY & NON-GOALS

What must not change

Public API compatibility strategy

The library is published, the event surface is documented in docs/events.md, and an embedder is entitled to all of it. Nothing in this program is a breaking change.

SurfaceGuaranteeMechanism
Every EVENT_* constant and payloadunchangedChangeSet is dispatched in addition. An adapter derives the legacy events from it, so a listener that never learns about ChangeSet sees exactly what it sees today. Tested row by row against the tables in docs/events.md
Configuration / Dimensioning staticsunchangedAlready forwarding to a module default since P7. A4 repoints that default at a module-default DesignRuntime — same object identity, same values
config, wallInformation exportsunchangedStill the default configuration's live objects, by identity. The suite and embedders mutate them directly and must keep being able to
Model.loadSerialized(json)unchanged signatureBecomes a thin wrapper over the document pipeline. Same argument, same events. It gains safety, not a new shape. A structured result is available through a new sibling method for callers who want one
Save format 2.0.0reads unchangedEvery document that opens today opens after. Stable ids are written as additive optional fields; a file without them has them reconstructed deterministically from the existing derived keys
Asset URLs in saved documentsunchangedThe resolver maps logical name → physical URL. What is in the file never changes, which is the whole reason the resolver exists rather than a hashing scheme
Scene.setItemLoader, Main.setRendererFactory, Utils.setRandomSourceunchangedThe enabling seams stay exactly as documented in tests/README.md. A1 adds a session around the loader, not in place of it
BlueprintJS constructor optionsadditive onlyruntime joins configuration and renderProfile as an optional key. Omitting it gives the shared default, as today
Item.removed(), Edge.remove()behaviourThese begin releasing resources, which is the point. Signature unchanged; an embedder subclassing Item and overriding removed() must call super.removed() — the one migration note in the program, and it goes in the changelog and the release notes

Explicit non-goals

Not in this program

  • No framework change. Vue 3 stays, and no part of this needs a state library. The library remains Vue-free
  • No renderer change. WebGPU is not here. WebGLRenderer is the target throughout
  • No visual change. Both render profiles produce the same pixels afterwards. That is an acceptance criterion, not an aspiration — P5's tier is what checks it
  • No new runtime dependency. Three and bezier-js remain the only two, both peers
  • No asset compression. Draco, meshopt and KTX2 stay out, per P6's reasoning. A5 makes them easier later
  • No multiplayer, no server, no CRDT. Nothing here implies a backend, and the document model is deliberately single-writer

Deliberately still shared

  • floorplannerPalette — a page-level theme. P7's reasoning stands unchanged
  • The texture decode — one image, however many viewers. Sharing the decode is correct; only the teardown boundary is wrong
  • Scene.unloadableItemCount — documented process total, kept beside the per-instance field
  • The preserved quirks — Utils.pointInPolygon and isClockwise keep their documented bugs. Room detection depends on them and they are backlog items with their own tests, not incidental casualties of a refactor

Guardrails — what not to do

Written as prohibitions because the failure mode of a program like this is well known: it starts as six sprints and becomes a rewrite in the third one.

DO NOT
  • Do not introduce a parallel model layer. No model2/, no “new” Floorplan beside the old one, no adapter between two representations of the same design. Every sprint edits the existing classes in place. Two model layers means every bug has to be found twice and the migration never finishes
  • Do not convert the library to TypeScript. The JavaScript decision is the user's and it stands; checkJs per file is the mechanism, and P2's ledger in tsconfig.json is the roadmap for extending it
  • Do not make ChangeSet the only signal. The legacy events keep firing. A sprint that would “clean up” by removing EVENT_UPDATED has broken every embedder to save a dispatch
  • Do not delete Floorplan3D.redraw(). It stays as the reference implementation that incremental projection is diffed against. It is also the fallback if K-4 bites
  • Do not replace snapshot history with commands. Commands are layered over checkpoints. The snapshot is the thing that cannot be subtly wrong and it is never removed
  • Do not rename an asset, ever. tests/asset-integrity.test.js exists because P6 nearly did. The resolver adds indirection; it does not license renaming
  • Do not edit a characterization expectation to make a sprint pass. RM-001 §01's ledger governs. A failure is a signal; retiring an expectation is a deliberate act with a changelog line
  • Do not lower a coverage threshold or a size budget. Both are ratchets. This is already written into vitest.config.mjs and tools/budget.json and applies here without exception
  • Do not batch two sprints into one commit range. Each must be independently revertible, because K-1 and K-4 are the kind of risk that surfaces a week later
§ 19 · IMPLEMENTATION

Six sprints

Estimates are for a single developer at the cadence RM-001 and RM-002 ran at. Every sprint ships alone, is revertible alone, and leaves the tree green on all three tiers.

A0
Resource ownership
A1
Transactional loading
A2
Typed ChangeSet
A3
Identity + command history
A4
DesignRuntime
A5
Persistence + assets
A0

Architecture baselines and resource ownership

doneH-11–2 weeksno dependencies

Objective. Give every GPU resource exactly one owner, and make the leak measurable before fixing it — so that the fix is provable rather than plausible.

ScopeExcluded
ResourceRegistry in core/ · disposal for Edge, Floor, Item, Room, HalfEdge, HUD · the model/view ownership boundary for hit-test meshes · registry-scoped texture release · renderer.info.memory harness Incremental projection (A2 — A0 makes the churn correct, not smaller) · the loader race (A1) · DesignRuntime (A4) · any change to what is drawn

Affected modules

core/resource_registry.js # new — register, release, refcount, stats three/edge.js # remove() disposes its 6 meshes; phantomPlanes symmetry three/floor.js # dispose() + redraw() release what they replace three/floorPlan.js # owns a registry; redraw() releases into it three/hud.js # gains dispose() — has none today three/main.js # registry lifetime; clearTextureCache() call removed three/texture_cache.js # release-by-owner beside the global clear model/room.js # dispose() for floorPlane/roofPlane model/half_edge.js # dispose() for plane model/floorplan.js # update() releases the rooms and edges it replaces model/scene.js # removeItem() actually releases items/item.js # removed() releases; bhelper removed from scene tests/resource-lifecycle.test.js # new tests/browser/gpu-memory.test.js # new — renderer.info.memory

Tasks

  1. Measure first. Land the harness before the fix: a helper that counts distinct meshes, geometries and materials constructed across N operations, and a browser test that reads renderer.info.memory. Both must fail — that is the baseline, and it is what makes the sprint's claim checkable
  2. ResourceRegistry. register(resource), release(resource), releaseAll(), stats(). Refcounted per resource; disposes at zero. Deliberately modelled on texture_cache.js, which already does this correctly for textures and is the proof the pattern fits this codebase
  3. Decide the model/view boundary and write it down. Room and HalfEdge build hit-test meshes; Floor.addToScene() adds them. Resolution: the model owns them and gains dispose(); the view borrows and never disposes what it did not build. Correct docs/architecture.md, which currently states the model holds no GPU resources
  4. Floorplan.update() releases the rooms and half-edges it replaces — the 6-per-call finding, and the largest single win in the sprint
  5. Edge, Floor, HUD, Item, Scene.removeItem() dispose through the registry. Remove bhelper from the scene in Item.removed()
  6. Scope the texture cache to its owner. Keep the shared decode; replace the unconditional clearTextureCache() in Main.dispose() with release of that viewer's handles. The global clear stays exported for embedders
  7. Re-run the harness and ratchet the numbers into the test as assertions
RATIONALE · ALTERNATIVES · COMPATIBILITY
  • Why a registry. Three's own guidance puts the responsibility on the application and says removing from the scene frees nothing. The current code accepts that responsibility in three places out of dozens. A registry makes the boundary a place rather than a habit
