  • Alternative rejected — scene-graph walk at teardown. Cannot see detached objects, which is what leaks; and would dispose resources a sibling viewer shares
  • Alternative rejected — FinalizationRegistry. Non-deterministic by specification. A GPU resource freed “eventually” is not freed
  • Alternative considered — object pooling instead of dispose-and-rebuild. Genuinely attractive for update()'s six-per-call churn, and it is really A2's answer, not A0's. A0 makes disposal correct; A2 makes it rare
  • Compatibility. No signature changes. One documented behaviour change: an Item subclass overriding removed() must call super.removed(). WallItem is the only override in-tree and is updated with the sprint
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • M-3 = 0. 100 update() calls on a two-room plan abandon zero meshes, geometries or materials
  • M-1 / M-2 = no growth. Mount a viewer, 50 edit cycles, unmount: renderer.info.memory.geometries and .textures return to their pre-mount values. Chromium, tier 2
  • Two viewers stay independent. Disposing viewer A does not change viewer B's renderer.info.memory and does not force a re-decode — asserted through textureCacheStats(). This is the K-1 guard and it extends tests/browser/two-designs.test.js
  • Nothing looks different. Tier 2 frame comparison unchanged for both profiles; npm run parity unchanged
  • Rollback. One commit range. The registry is additive and every disposal call site is a separate hunk, so a single offending class can be reverted without the sprint
  • DoD. All three tiers green · coverage ratcheted · docs/architecture.md corrected · CHANGELOG entry · the baseline numbers recorded in the test as the thing that must not come back

K-1 is the sprint's real risk. Over-disposal is worse than the leak, and it is intermittent. The mitigation is that the assertion runs in both directions — memory must come down after teardown and stay up while another viewer holds a handle.

LANDED — A0
  • Done as planned, and the measure-first order earned its keep. The suite was written before any fix and 19 of its 20 cases failed. The one that passed is the borrowed-mesh guard — “a Floor never disposes the model meshes it only borrows” — which passed vacuously because nothing disposed anything, and which had to keep passing afterwards. That is the K-1 tripwire, and having it green from the start is what made it meaningful at the end
  • M-3 = 0. Twenty update() calls on a square room now abandon nothing, where they abandoned 120 resources. M-1 and M-2 hold in chromium: ten edit cycles move renderer.info.memory by zero. Both gates verified by reverting the fix — the headless suite fails four cases and the GPU counter reports exactly 2 leaked geometries per cycle, which is the two room planes, the only model meshes that reach the GPU
  • The measurement had a hole in it, and reverting the fix is what found it. The first browser test drove edits with Corner.move(), which looks like the obvious edit and reaches update(false, ...) — the branch that dispatches EVENT_UPDATED and returns without re-deriving anything. That churns the view and never touches a Room or a HalfEdge, so the test passed with the model half of the fix reverted. It now calls update() explicitly as well. A gate proven only in the direction of success is not proven
  • Two leaks were found that the review had not. Skybox never disposed the ground photograph — a local variable, assigned as a material's map, and Material.dispose() in three does not touch its maps — nor the environment map, which lives in a shader uniform where nothing would ever have reached it. One large texture per viewer, and the second would have appeared the moment anybody entered walk-through mode
  • A latent hazard, recorded rather than fixed. Item extends Mesh and declares its own no-argument remove() meaning “take me out of the design”, which shadows Object3D.remove(child). Detaching one of an item's label planes through the ordinary call re-enters item removal and recurses until the stack gives out — which is how it was found. disposeObject goes through Object3D.prototype.remove instead. The shadowing is a real trap for any three internal that calls parent.remove(child) on an Item, but Item.remove() is public API and renaming it belongs in its own change
  • The ownership question was answered, and it is now a paragraph of documentation and one test. A Room owns its two hit-test planes; a Wall owns its two HalfEdge planes, because the half edge writes itself onto wall.frontEdge/backEdge and that pointer is the only way back to it — which is why the release has to happen immediately before resetFrontBack() nulls it, and why removeWall() needed its own release for the wall that has just left the array. The 3D Floor borrows the room's planes and never disposes them. docs/architecture.md said the model layer held no GPU resources at all; that is corrected, with the reason it was believable
  • Not zero, and the remainder is the boundary. Disposing a viewer leaves exactly two geometries uploaded per room — the model's own hit-test planes, which survive because BlueprintJS.dispose() deliberately leaves the model standing so a caller can serialize it or mount a new viewer over it. The test asserts that remainder is precisely the model's, by disposing the rooms afterwards and watching it reach zero
  • 951 headless tests (20 new) and 33 in the browser (6 new), all green, with no characterization drift. Coverage moved on all four measures — 77.23 lines, 77.27 statements, 63.94 branches, 75.09 functions — because disposal paths are reached by every test that builds a plan, not only by the twenty that assert on them. Three of the four floors were raised; branches gained less than a point and was left alone
  • One thing was relaxed, deliberately and in the open: the browser tier's test timeout, 15s to 30s. The profile-comparison test takes 8s on its own under SwiftShader and began timing out at 17s while still asserting correctly — the two frames differed by 99.99% of pixels against a 5% floor. The tier shares one CPU rasteriser, so new work is subtracted from every other test's budget. The memory suite was cut from 50 cycles to 10 first, which was not enough and should not have been the fix anyway: shrinking a real measurement to protect an unrelated test's margin is the wrong trade. Recorded in the config as an allowance rather than a ratchet
A1

Transactional loading and document validation

doneH-2H-31–2 weekssoft dep: A0

Objective. Make opening a document atomic — it either replaces the design completely or leaves it untouched — and make every in-flight load belong to a session that can be cancelled and superseded.

ScopeExcluded
DesignDocument validate/migrate · atomic swap in Model · LoadSession with a generation token · a LoadingManager per Scene · structured load results · stale-callback rejection IndexedDB (A5 — this sprint defines the pipeline the repository will use) · command history (A3) · any save-format field change · new error UI beyond wiring the existing toasts

Affected modules

model/document.js # new — parse, validate, migrate, describe model/load_session.js # new — generation token, abort, settle model/model.js # loadSerialized becomes validate → build → swap model/scene.js # own LoadingManager; callbacks check generation model/floorplan.js # loadFloorplan stops calling reset() first app/composables/useDesignIO.js # consume the structured result app/composables/useHistory.js # settle gate reads the session, not a counter tests/document-loading.test.js # new

Tasks

  1. The corpus first. Turn §16 H-2's four documents into a failing test that asserts the design survives. Add the malformed cases a real user hits: a truncated file, a .obj renamed, a design with a corner id referenced by no wall, one with a wall naming a missing corner
  2. DesignDocument.parse(json) → {ok, document, errors, warnings}. Validates shape before anything is touched. Reads the existing version and units fields — units is already honoured by cornerReader; version is currently read by nothing, deliberately, and becomes the migration pipeline's input rather than a load gate. RM-001's warning stands: the gate that consulted it was the bug
  3. Build off to the side, then swap. Construct the replacement Floorplan and item list without touching live state; commit by assignment. A failure discards the half-built replacement and the open design never moved
  4. LoadSession. A monotonic generation per Model. Every loader callback captures its generation and returns early if superseded. New, Open, Undo, Redo and dispose() all open a new session
  5. A LoadingManager per Scene, passed to both loaders. Superseding a session calls manager.abort() — real in r185, composed into FileLoader through AbortSignal.any. Today both loaders share the global default manager, so there is nothing per-document to abort
  6. Structured results out of loadSerialized's new sibling, so useDesignIO can say which item failed instead of “could not open that design”
  7. Simplify the settle gate. With a session, useHistory asks the session whether it is settled instead of counting events. SETTLE_BACKSTOP_MS can finally be deleted for every path except an embedder's own setItemLoader
RATIONALE · ALTERNATIVES · COMPATIBILITY
  • Why build-then-swap rather than try/rollback. A rollback needs a snapshot of the design to restore, which is a serialize on every open — and it would restore through the same destructive path. Building beside the live design is cheaper and cannot half-fail
  • Alternative rejected — validate only, keep in-place mutation. Cheaper, and it closes three of the four measured cases. It does not close the fourth, and the fourth is the one that reports success
  • Alternative rejected — a schema library. A runtime dependency for one document shape, in a library with exactly two peers. Hand-written validation is perhaps 150 lines and can produce messages that name the field
  • Alternative considered — sequence numbers on events instead of a session object. Equivalent for the race; no cancellation, no place to hang the manager or the settle state
  • Compatibility. loadSerialized(json) keeps its signature and its two events. It stops throwing on a malformed document — a behaviour change in the safe direction, since throwing was the symptom of the destruction. Anything relying on the throw gets the structured sibling
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • M-6 = 0 of 4. Every document in the corpus leaves the open design byte-identical, verified by comparing exportSerialized() before and after
  • M-7 = 0. Two loads with a deferred stub loader: not one item from the superseded document reaches the live scene, and the item events balance per session
  • M-4 drops. The load path stops calling update() per corner and per wall; one document open dispatches a small constant number rather than 25. Full delivery of M-4 = 1 is A2's
  • Observability. loadSession.stats() — generation, in flight, aborted, failed. This is what makes M-7 assertable and what an embedder's loading indicator should read
  • Rollback. The new path sits behind Model.loadDocument(); loadSerialized delegates to it in one line. Reverting that line restores the old behaviour exactly
  • DoD. Three tiers green · the four-document corpus committed as a fixture · docs/save-format.md gains a validation section · docs/events.md notes the balance guarantee · CHANGELOG
LANDED — A1
  • M-6 = 0, and the corpus grew from four documents to ten. The four from §16 plus a truncated file, an .obj renamed, a wall naming a corner that is not there, a corner whose coordinates are not numbers, an items that is an object, and bare null. Each is asserted the same way: exportSerialized() before and after must be byte-identical. Verified by bypassing validation, where 6 of the 10 fail — the other four were already safe because JSON.parse throws before the mutation, which is exactly the accident the finding described
  • M-7 = 0 and M-4 = 1, the latter ahead of plan. A1 was scoped to make the load-path dispatch count "a small constant", with 1 left to A2; batching loadFloorplan took simple-room from 25 to 1 and rich-design from 39 to 1. A2 inherits the ChangeSet contract and incremental projection without also having to do this
  • Two deviations from the plan, both deliberate. The plan said build a replacement Floorplan off to the side and swap it in. That is not what landed and should not have been: Model.floorplan is subscribed to by Main, Floorplan3D, Floorplanner2D and four composables, all by object identity, so swapping the object silently detaches every one of them. Validating the whole document first delivers the same guarantee — a bad document never reaches live state — without touching identity that half the codebase holds. The build-off-to-the-side step still happens, at the data level, which is what DesignDocument returns
  • The second deviation is the opposite of what the plan said, and the plan was wrong. It specified that loadSerialized would stop throwing. It still throws, because callers already catch it — useDesignIO among them — and a function that quietly stops reporting failure fails silently for every existing caller, which is the worst kind of change. loadDocument() is the structured sibling for callers who would rather branch. What actually improved is the message: it names the field rather than being whichever TypeError the mutation happened to hit first
  • A characterization test was retired, and it was the right one. items-and-scene.test.js carried a case named "throws on a design with no items array, after EVENT_LOADING and after replacing the floorplan", marked QUIRK, asserting the destruction in as many words. That is the defect A1 exists to remove, so the expectation was retired deliberately with a row in the retirement table rather than the change re-checked. The replacement pins the opposite claim, including that no EVENT_LOADING is dispatched — a listener that shows a spinner on LOADING and hides it on LOADED must not be left spinning by a load that never starts
  • A stale item is discarded, not merely ignored. The geometry and materials arrive whether or not anybody still wants them, and the callback is the only place that knows they are unwanted — so it disposes them. Without that line A1 would have quietly undone A0 on every superseded load, and there is a test that fails if it goes
  • Cancellation is real but is not what the correctness rests on. Each Scene now owns a LoadingManager, so abort() stops the fetches that design started — r185 composes the manager's signal into FileLoader through AbortSignal.any. It cannot reach an embedder's own setItemLoader, which is arbitrary code, and it cannot un-decode a model that has already arrived. The generation check is what guarantees the result; the abort only saves the bandwidth
  • The history settle gate stopped counting. useHistory maintained its own tally of loads in flight, which was correct only while one document was ever loading — a second load starting before the first had settled interleaved two documents in one number, and the gate could close on the wrong one's last item. It now asks scene.loadSession.settled. SETTLE_BACKSTOP_MS stays, still for the one path that escapes every guarantee: an embedder's loader that never calls back
  • 977 headless tests (26 new) and 33 in the browser, all green. A0's guarantee re-verified under A1: 252 resources built across 20 updates, 240 disposed, 12 live, 0 leaked
  • One of A0's own tests had to change, which is worth recording as the cost of batching. It asserted updates > 1 during a load — a guard against passing vacuously — and that guard is exactly what M-4 invalidated. The claim it protected is now asserted directly instead: resources were built, and none leaked
  • The size budget went over, and what it exposed is worth more than the limit. A0 and A1 added about 17 KB gzipped to the library IIFE — a registry, a validator, a session and disposal paths across nine files — and A1 went 2.7 KB past a ceiling A0 had already eaten the headroom of. Raising it needed a reason, so the bundle was measured: 443.9 KB gzipped, of which 163.4 KB is comments. The library builds are minify: false, so 37% of what an architect3d/iife consumer downloads is this project's documentation. That is not an argument for documenting less — the density is deliberate and it is why these findings were findable — but an unminified published bundle and a heavily-commented codebase do not sit well together. Both library limits are raised to fit the code that exists, and tools/budget.json now says not to raise them again without settling the minification question first
A2

Typed ChangeSet and incremental projection

doneH-42 weekshard dep: A0

Objective. Let the model say what changed, and let each consumer react to only its own kind — so that dragging a corner stops rebuilding the world and stops moving the camera.

ScopeExcluded
The ChangeSet contract and its six kinds · an adapter deriving today's events from it · incremental Floorplan3D · 2D repaint on geometry kinds · camera recentring gated on extent Removing any legacy event · stable ids (A3 — A2 addresses entities by current reference, A3 upgrades to ids) · a general reactive graph · the 2D view's whole-canvas repaint, which is correct for a canvas

The contract

ChangeSet { topology # corners/walls added, removed, reconnected — rooms may be re-derived geometry # existing entities moved or resized — room set unchanged surface # textures, colours, materials — no geometry implication items # furniture added, removed, moved selection # what is selected — never a model mutation view # configuration, units, render profile, palette } each kind carries the affected entities and a `reason` — 'load' | 'edit' | 'undo' | 'derive' consumers: Floorplan3D topology → rebuild affected · geometry → update transforms surface → swap material · items → nothing Main camera topology only, and only when the plan extent moved 2D view topology, geometry, surface, selection history any kind with reason 'edit', at the gesture boundary autosave the same boundary

Tasks

  1. Define the contract and the adapter together. The adapter is what keeps K-8 at zero: every legacy event is derived from a ChangeSet, and a test walks the tables in docs/events.md row by row asserting each still fires with its payload
  2. Batch the load path. newCorner() and newWall() stop calling update() unconditionally; loadFloorplan() wraps the whole build in one batch and emits one topology change. This alone takes M-4 from 25 to 1
  3. Gate the camera. Main subscribes to topology changes and recentres only when the plan's extent actually moved. The user-visible fix in this sprint
  4. Incremental Floorplan3D. Rebuild only the Edges and Floors named by the change; release the replaced ones through A0's registry. This is why A0 is a hard dependency — incremental rebuilding without disposal converts one big leak into a continuous one
  5. Keep redraw() and diff against it. A debug mode runs both and compares the resulting scene graph. Not shipped enabled; run in CI on the fixture designs
  6. Fix the removeCorner/removeWall asymmetry while the call graph is open
RATIONALE · ALTERNATIVES · COMPATIBILITY
  • Why a typed set rather than more event constants. More constants multiply the subscription surface and still cannot express “these three walls”. The payload is the point, not the name
  • Alternative rejected — a reactive dependency graph (signals, or an observable store). Real power, wrong shape: it would mean model objects become proxies, and docs/architecture.md's identity warning exists because === comparisons in room detection and EventDispatcher dedup break silently under proxies
  • Alternative rejected — dirty flags on entities. Cheaper, and it is roughly what needsUpdate already is. It cannot express why, so the camera still cannot distinguish a load from a drag
  • Alternative considered — extend update()'s existing two booleans. Honest and small, and it is the fallback if this sprint runs long. It does not give consumers a payload
  • Compatibility. Purely additive. The one observable difference is frequency: EVENT_UPDATED fires once per load instead of 25 times. That is the K-2 risk, and a test asserting a dispatch count is exactly the sort that gets retired deliberately, with a changelog line
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • M-4 = 1. One document open, one EVENT_UPDATED
  • M-5 = 0. Dragging a corner triggers no full Floorplan3D.redraw() and no centerCamera()
  • Every row of docs/events.md still fires, with its documented payload, asserted individually
  • The incremental path matches the reference. For each fixture design and each edit kind, incremental and full redraw produce the same scene graph — same mesh count, same materials, same transforms
  • Still no visual change. Tier 2 frames unchanged for both profiles
  • Observability. A counter per ChangeSet kind, and per consumer reaction, readable in tests. The 25 → 1 claim has to be measurable to be true
  • Rollback. Incremental projection sits behind a flag on Floorplan3D defaulting to the incremental path; flipping it restores full redraw with the ChangeSet still in place. The two halves revert separately
  • DoD. Three tiers green · adapter test covering the full event table · docs/events.md gains a ChangeSet section · any retired expectation listed in tests/README.md's retirement table · CHANGELOG

The longest sprint, and the one to timebox. K-4 says the missed case will be one nobody thought of. If the incremental path is not matching the reference by the end of week two, ship the ChangeSet and the camera gate — which are most of the user-visible value — and leave redraw() in place for a follow-up.

LANDED — A2
  • M-5 = 0, on both halves. A ten-step corner drag on a four-wall room ran 10 full Floorplan3D.redraw() calls and 10 centerCamera() calls before this sprint. It now runs zero of each, and the frame it produces is byte-identical to the one a full redraw produces — read back from a real WebGL context in tests/browser/incremental-projection.test.js, not inferred
  • The reference diff is the deliverable, not the projection. Three designs by ten edit kinds, each run twice — once incrementally, once with incremental = false — and compared mesh by mesh through tests/helpers/scene_graph.js: geometry type, vertex count, a checksum over every position, material type and colour and side and flags, texture identity, and the transform. Doing less work is only an improvement if the picture is unchanged, and this is the sentence that means it
  • Three of the ten edit kinds changed nothing, and the matrix was reporting that as coverage. Found by adding an anti-tautology guard — the reference scene must differ from the reference scene before the edit — which failed nine of thirty cases immediately. Re-deriving the rooms, adding an unconnected corner and curving a wall are all inert here: this projection draws a curved wall as a straight quad, because only HalfEdge's centre and length read the bezier and neither reaches a mesh. Those three moved into a group that asserts the opposite — the picture must NOT move — which is a real check and a different one. A case that cannot fail is worse than no case, because it reads like coverage
  • The camera gate was tested against a stand-in, and the stand-in passed with the gate deleted. The first version of the camera block reimplemented Main's rule in the test file and subscribed the same way. Deleting the kind check from Main broke nothing. A stand-in for the thing under test is not a test; the block now mounts a real Main over a fake renderer
  • And the fixed version still passed, for a second wrong reason. The drag moved a corner inward, where the other three corners still hold every extreme of the bounding box — so the extent check declined it and the kind check was never reached. Dragging outward is what makes the assertion mean “declined because it is a drag” rather than “declined because of where this one happened to go”. Four deliberate breaks were run against the finished suite; two of them exposed a hole rather than confirming a gate, which is what breaking them is for
  • A finding the review had not made: one corner move announced itself three times. A corner with two walls dispatched EVENT_UPDATED once from its own move and once from each wall — a wall listens to its corners, and Wall.updateControlVectors() calls update() of its own. Two of the three carried no payload at all. So the “one full rebuild per pointermove” in the finding was really three
  • A second: the A1 batch started after reset(). Invisible while the tested case was opening a document into an empty plan. Opening one over an existing design is the real case, and it announced thirteen changes before building anything — every wall and corner of the outgoing design re-deriving on its way out, each labelled an edit rather than part of the load. Found by the test asserting that a restoration is labelled undo, which is not what that test was looking for
  • Deviation: the floorplan emits two of the six kinds, not all six. surface looks as though it should be the third and should not be. Room.setTexture() and HalfEdge.setTexture() already dispatch straight to the Floor and Edge drawing them — per-entity, and already incremental, which is what this sprint is trying to achieve everywhere else. A plan-level broadcast on top would be new traffic, and autosave and history subscribe to the plan, so they would start recording texture changes they do not record today. That is a behaviour change, arguably an improvement, and not one A2 was asked to make. items, selection and view belong to Scene, Main and Configuration. All four are named in the contract, because a consumer writing a switch needs the whole vocabulary. A2 types the dispatch that exists; it does not invent dispatch
  • Reconciliation after a topology change still rebuilds everything, and that is the honest state of it. update(true) constructs a new Room and a new HalfEdge for every one, so a diff by identity finds nothing in common. It is written as a reconciliation rather than a rebuild because that is the shape that becomes genuinely incremental the moment A3 lands, with no further change to the class. What A2 delivers today is the geometry path — which is the one a drag takes, and the one the finding was about
  • The removeCorner/removeWall asymmetry is closed, and it was visible. getSize() reads the corner list, so a corner removed without a re-derivation kept inflating the bounding box the camera frames and the shadow camera is sized from. Corner.removeAll() now opens a batch, so what was one re-derivation per wall plus none for the corner is one for the whole gesture
  • Nothing was retired, in the sprint most likely to have to. K-2 named characterization drift as the high risk: coalescing EVENT_UPDATED changes how often it fires, and a test asserting a count would have had to go. None did. One expectation was wrong and was corrected rather than retired — A2 briefly stopped loadFloorplan returning early on a malformed file, and serialization.test.js caught it inside a minute
  • 1,049 headless tests (72 new) and 37 in the browser (4 new), all green. Coverage moved a whole point on all four measures at once — 78.10 lines, 78.16 statements, 65.42 branches, 76.14 functions — and all four floors are raised. Not because more tests were written, but because the reference diff drives paths no previous suite reached
  • The size budget went over again, and only the ESM entry did. lib-iife-gzip did not breach and was deliberately left at 1.7% headroom rather than pre-emptively widened, so A3 trips it — which is the forcing function A1's note asked for, not an accident. Re-measuring gave a worse number than A1's: the ESM bundle is 52.2% comments against the IIFE's 37.6%. The two want different answers, though. The IIFE is downloaded by a browser as written, so its comments are a real cost to every end user; the ESM entry is consumed through a bundler that strips them on the way to production, so its comments are a build-time cost and are also the JSDoc a typed consumer reads. “Minify the library” is the wrong shape of question
  • The typecheck ledger was wrong before this sprint, and was re-derived rather than incremented. It claimed 43 checked files; the real number was 45, because texture_cache.js has carried the pragma all along and was never listed, and src/app/composables had grown to 17. It is 46 now. A ledger nobody recounts is a comment, not a ratchet
A3

Stable identities and command history

doneH-5H-62 weekshard dep: A1, A2

Objective. Give every entity an identity that survives recomputation, so a room keeps its name when you draw through its wall — and make undo stop re-downloading the furniture.

ScopeExcluded
Assigned ids on Wall, Room, HalfEdge, Item · id-based room continuity across re-derivation · selection by id · command history over checkpoints · commands for the reversible operations only Converting every mutation to a command — explicitly staged, and the majority stay snapshots · a redo tree · collaborative editing · changing what a save file contains beyond additive optional ids

Identity model

EntityTodayAfterOld file
CornerUtils.guide() — already stableUnchangedAlready in the file
Wall[start.id,end.id] frozen at constructionAssigned id, kept through re-attachmentSeeded from the derived pair
RoomTwo disagreeing derived keysAssigned id; continuity by corner-set overlap across re-derivationSeeded from roomByCornersId, so names and textures carry over on first open
HalfEdgeNonewallId + sideDerived, never persisted
ItemNone — array position onlyAssigned idAssigned on load, written on next save

Tasks

  1. Assign ids and make metaroomsdata key off them. Room continuity across update() is the crux: a re-derived room inherits the id of the previous room it shares the most corners with, so splitting a wall keeps the room's name and floor texture. This is the measured H-5 defect and it is the sprint's headline
  2. Reconcile the two room keys. Floor textures use getUuid() and names use roomByCornersId. Both become the assigned id; both legacy forms are read on load and never written
  3. Selection resolves by id. useSelection holds {type, id} and looks the entity up on demand, so a re-derived room does not orphan the inspector
  4. Command history over checkpoints. A checkpoint is today's snapshot, taken every N commands and on every load. A command carries apply, invert and the ids it touches. Undo prefers the inverse and falls back to the nearest checkpoint plus replay
  5. Commands for the operations that are cleanly reversible — move corner, set texture, rename room, add/remove/move item, set wall attributes. Corner.mergeWithIntersected and anything re-deriving the room set stay snapshot-backed, exactly as useHistory's docblock argues
  6. Legacy direct mutation stays safe. An embedder writing straight onto a model object still gets a snapshot: a mutation observed through ChangeSet with no command boundary opens an implicit one and closes it at the gesture boundary. Nothing has to be converted for the history to stay correct
  7. Round-trip property test per command: apply(invert(c), apply(c, s)) serializes identically to s. A command that cannot pass does not ship as a command
RATIONALE · ALTERNATIVES · COMPATIBILITY
  • Why commands over checkpoints rather than instead of them. K-5 is the risk that a wrong inverse produces a silently incorrect design, and useHistory's docblock makes that argument better than this one does. Checkpoints bound the blast radius: a wrong inverse is corrected by the next checkpoint rather than persisting
  • Alternative rejected — keep pure snapshots and optimise the reload. Tempting, and it is genuinely most of H-6's cost. But an item that has not changed should not be touched at all, and only a command knows that
  • Alternative rejected — content-hash identity. Derives identity from state, which is exactly the current bug in a more sophisticated form
  • Alternative considered — UUIDs everywhere with no continuity rule. Stable but useless for rooms, because a re-derived room is a new object and would get a new id. The overlap rule is the whole value
  • Compatibility. Ids are additive optional fields; save format stays 2.0.0. A file without them opens identically and is upgraded on next save — K-3's mitigation, proved against tests/fixtures/v1/
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • H-5's measurement inverts. Name a room, texture it, draw a wall through one side: the name survives, the texture survives, the id is unchanged
  • M-8 = 0. Undo of a corner move triggers zero EVENT_ITEM_LOADING
  • Every fixture in tests/fixtures/v1/ opens unchanged and round-trips with ids added
  • Every command round-trips — the property test above, over generated edit sequences
  • Selection survives re-derivation. Select a room, edit a distant wall, and the inspector is still bound to the same room
  • Observability. History reports command count, checkpoint count and bytes retained, so the memory claim is checkable rather than asserted
  • Rollback. Two independent halves. Ids ship first and are inert without the history change; the command stack is behind a flag defaulting to the current snapshot path until the round-trip suite is green
  • DoD. Three tiers green · docs/save-format.md documents the optional id fields and the reconstruction rule · docs/architecture.md gains an identity section · CHANGELOG
LANDED — A3
  • H-5's measurement inverts. Name a room “Kitchen”, texture it, draw a wall through one of its sides: before this sprint it came back as “A New Room” on the default floor — and that is the literal failure message the suite prints when the fix is reverted, because the assertions are ordered to report the name a person loses rather than two unequal uuids. Name, texture and id all survive now, through a wall split, a corner merge, a corner deletion and a bare re-derivation
  • M-8 = 0. An undo of a corner move loaded every model in the design; it now loads none, keeps the same Item objects, and keeps their geometry and materials — asserted by watching for a dispose that must not come. Verified by restoring clearItems(), which fails nine of the eleven cases
  • The command layer was not built, and the numbers are why. This is the one deliberate omission in the sprint and it should be read as a decision to revisit rather than as work skipped. The plan called for commands with an invert each, over checkpoints, behind a flag defaulting to the snapshot path — so by the plan's own rollback section it was not the shipped path either. Its case rested on two claims, and both were measured on the largest fixture (23 corners, 30 walls, 20 items): a snapshot is 19.9 KB, fifty of them are 0.97 MB, and a restore takes 4.6 ms. Under a megabyte and under a frame — the useHistory docblock's own estimate was right all along. Against that, K-5 is the risk that a wrong inverse produces a design that is silently wrong, and the metric the layer existed to move is already at zero. Adding an inverse per operation to save 0.97 MB is not a trade worth making, and it is available to make later
  • What replaced task 7 is better than task 7. The plan's property test was apply(invert(c), apply(c, s)) serializes to s. With no commands there is nothing to invert, but the property it protects is the one that matters and is testable against what shipped: after any edit and an undo, the design is byte-identical. Twelve generated edit sequences over eight edit kinds, from a seeded source so a failure replays. It found three real defects on its first two runs, all in code written earlier the same day
  • Defect one: restoring a snapshot went through the drag path. The reconciliation called Item.moveToPosition, and FloorItem overrides it with placement rules — it clamps y to the floor and, if the target is outside a room, shows an error and returns without moving at all. Restoring is not a drag; the position in a snapshot was valid when it was written. Undo was silently lossy
  • Defect two: Item.setScale is relative. It multiplies into the current scale, so expressing “make it what it was” as a factor of 1 / current does not arrive: 1.5 × (1 / 1.5) is 0.9999999999999999, which serializes differently, which means an undo produces a document that differs from the one it restored. Item.applyScale is the absolute form, and recomputes halfSize from the geometry rather than accumulating it, so that cannot drift either
  • Defect three: the save file's item order was never stable. Items were written in scene.items order, which is the order their model files finished downloading in — so two saves of a design nobody had touched could differ, and after reconciliation they reliably did, because a reloaded item rejoins the array at the end. Not cosmetic: useHistory decides whether anything changed by comparing this string, so a reordering it cannot read the meaning of becomes a history entry for an edit nobody made. Items are written in id order now, and the pre-existing non-determinism goes with it
  • Deviation: the two room keys were reconciled, not replaced. The plan said both become the assigned id. They are still the derived strings, and the reason is what the code showed rather than what the plan assumed. Both maps are handed out by saveFloorplan() and adopted by loadFloorplan() by reference, with two characterization tests pinning that aliasing, so keying them by an id means translating at the file boundary in both directions — and the id is deliberately not persisted, because a file identifies a room by its corners and that is a description another build can read. Worse, an entry under an id has no way back: today a room you delete leaves its name under its corner key, so drawing it again brings the name back, and under an assigned id the id dies with the room. What the two keys needed was not to become one key but to stop being able to disagree, and they now move together, by one rule, in one place
  • A hack was deleted rather than generalised. Corner.combineWithCorner rewrote each attached room's name key by string-replacing the departing corner's id with this one's. Right instinct, three problems: a substring replace on a comma-joined key corrupts it whenever one corner id contains another; it knew nothing about the floor texture, which was lost anyway; and it was one caller's special case for a problem every other route had too. carryRoomIdentity() covers all of them
  • Two name collisions, one of them A0's finding again. An Item cannot have an id: Item extends Mesh extends Object3D, and three defines Object3D.id as a non-writable numeric counter, so the assignment silently does nothing and item.id stays a number that changes on every rebuild. It is designId. And EVENT_WALL_CLICKED carries a HalfEdge, not a Wall, while the 2D view's wall event really does carry a Wall — two different things behind one selection kind, found by the resolver looking in the wrong collection
  • A carve-out, stated rather than hidden: wall-bound items always reload. A WallItem holds a HalfEdge, and every load destroys and rebuilds the floorplan, so keeping one leaves it pointing at an edge that does not exist and detaching from it later. Lifting the exclusion needs wall ids that survive a save and load, which they do not — reset() destroys every wall and newWall assigns fresh ones
  • Four deliberate breaks, and the fourth found the hole. Disabling carryRoomIdentity, taking raw overlap instead of Jaccard, dropping the one-to-one rule and lowering the two-corner floor each fail their gate. The wall-bound carve-out did not — nothing caught it — until it got a test of its own
  • The browser tier was measuring contention, not tests. Found because gpu-memory.test.js passed in the working tree and timed out on two clean-checkout runs in three. It takes 26s on its own and 62s inside the tier: Vitest parallelises files, and parallel files on one CPU rasteriser do not overlap, they queue — each one's clock running while it waits. Serialised: 62.8s wall and 182.8s of test time becomes 67.1s wall and 64.9s. Four seconds of wall clock for two-thirds of the test time back, and a per-test timeout that measures the test. It is also the better answer to the problem A0 solved by moving the timeout from 15s to 30s
  • 1,112 headless tests (63 new) and 37 in the browser, all green. Nothing retired: three expectations were updated, all because getMetaData() gained a key. Coverage moved branches 65.42 → 66.78 and functions 76.14 → 77.26, both floors raised; lines and statements gained fractions and stay put

What A3 leaves for later. Wall ids are stable across editing and not across a document load, which is why wall selection does not survive an undo and why wall-bound items reload. Seeding a wall's id from its corner pair on load would fix both and is a small change — but it needs a disambiguator for the two-walls-between-one-pair case, which is exactly the collision this sprint removed, so it wants its own thinking rather than a corner of this one.

A4

Multi-document DesignRuntime isolation

doneH-71 weeksoft dep: A0, A1

Objective. Collect the per-document services into one object with an identity, so that disposing one viewer cannot reach into another — the condition Main.dispose()'s own comment says arrived with P7.

ScopeExcluded
DesignRuntime holding configuration, dimensioning, render profile, asset resolver, load session, resource registry and document id · a module-default runtime the statics resolve to · BlueprintJS({runtime}) · disposal scoped to the runtime floorplannerPalette — deliberately page-level, per P7 · deprecating any static · making the application take a runtime, which would change nothing but which 26 reads resolve to

Tasks

  1. DesignRuntime as a container, not a god object. It holds services and an id; it holds no design data. Floorplan, Model and Main reach it the way they already reach a Configuration
  2. The module default is a runtime. P7's defaultConfiguration becomes defaultRuntime.configuration — the same object by identity, so config and wallInformation keep working for the suite and for embedders that mutate them directly
  3. configurationOf(owner) generalises to runtimeOf(owner), keeping the same fallback so a Corner built by hand in a test still behaves as it does
  4. Disposal becomes a runtime operation. runtime.dispose() releases that document's registry and aborts its session. clearTextureCache() stops being called from Main.dispose()
  5. Extend the two-viewer browser suite to the full matrix: two runtimes, different configurations, different profiles, dispose one, assert the other is untouched in pixels and in renderer.info.memory
RATIONALE · ALTERNATIVES · COMPATIBILITY
  • Why now and not in P7. P7 delivered per-instance state one service at a time, which was right — each stage shipped alone. What it could not deliver is a single object to dispose, because there was nothing to hang a registry or a session on. A4 is the collection step, and it is short precisely because P7 did the hard part
  • Alternative rejected — dependency injection throughout. Threading seven services through every constructor for a library whose classes already reach their configuration through floorplan. One object on the path that already exists is less code and less churn
  • Alternative rejected — module-level registry keyed by document id. A global keyed by id is still a global; teardown ordering bugs come straight back
  • Compatibility. Every static keeps working, by the same mechanism that made P7 free — forwarding to a module default. runtime is an additive constructor option
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • Disposing one viewer changes nothing about another — not its pixels, not its renderer.info.memory, not its texture handle count. The H-1 finding, closed at the isolation level
  • All 302 static call sites still pass untouched, as they did through P7
  • Two documents, two configurations, two profiles, side by side in chromium, each disposable independently in either order
  • Observability. runtime.stats() — registry size, session state, cache handles — which is also what the acceptance tests read
  • Rollback. Mechanical and self-contained; the runtime is a container over services that already exist independently
  • DoD. Three tiers green · docs/architecture.md's “where the layers meet” gains the runtime · docs/getting-started.md shows two designs on a page · CHANGELOG
LANDED — A4
  • The sprint found its own finding, and it was the one the sprint exists to close. The first version resolved a document with no options to defaultRuntime — the obvious reading of “omit it and share the page-wide default”, and what P7's configuration option means. It is wrong for a runtime: Model.loadDocument calls loadSession.begin(), so two viewers sharing one session means opening a design in one abandons the furniture still arriving in the other. The suite caught it as an abort count that had stopped resetting between documents. The rule that shipped is settings default to shared, lifetimes never are: a document with no options gets a runtime of its own whose configuration, dimensioning and render profile are the module-level objects, by identity
  • Every static reads what it always read, and it is asserted rather than assumed. defaultRuntime.configuration === defaultConfiguration, .dimensioning === defaultDimensioning, .renderProfile === renderProfile, and config/wallInformation are still that configuration's live data. Not one of the 302 static call sites was touched
  • configurationOf and runtimeOf cannot disagree, by construction rather than by agreement. Every class that has a runtime exposes configuration as a getter over runtime.configuration, so there is one place the answer is kept. Checked over a table of six owner shapes the library actually builds, plus the one shape where they legitimately differ — an object literal carrying a bare configuration, which is the pre-A4 shape and belongs on the page's runtime because it has no document of its own
  • Two files moved into core/, and that is the whole of the structural cost. render_profile.js from three/ and load_session.js from model/, because the runtime holds both and core importing from either layer would have been the first time anything in core reached outside itself. Both have no imports at all, so the move is free — the same argument that already put units.js in core. Every export is re-exported from blueprint.js at the same name
  • Nothing took a new constructor argument. Floorplan, Model, Main, Floorplan3D and every Edge reach the runtime by a hop that already existed, which is what made A4 a short sprint. Edge gained an optional fifth parameter only so Floorplan3D can pass what it already knows; the four-argument form derives the same runtime from the half edge, so an embedder constructing one directly is not building meshes the document cannot count
  • The registry became countable. Edge asks the runtime for its registry instead of constructing one, so runtime.stats() can answer “how much GPU memory is this document holding” — a number nobody could get before. Edge.remove() hands the registry back, without which a document re-deriving its rooms would accumulate one spent registry per wall face per rebuild: a smaller leak than A0's, and still one. There is a test that fails if that line goes
  • One deviation, and it is a correction to the acceptance criterion. The plan said runtime.stats() should report “cache handles”. It does not, and should not: the texture cache is refcounted across the page, and A0's finding was precisely that describing that shared cache as one viewer's property is what made disposing A re-decode every image B was using. Reporting it per-runtime would re-describe a shared resource as an owned one — the same mistake in a new place. textureCacheStats() is page-wide and says so, and the browser tier reads both
  • No asset resolver, and it is not an omission. The A4 scope names one; A5 is the sprint that builds it. A property initialised to null that nothing reads is a promise, not a seam
  • Task 4's second half was already done. clearTextureCache() stopped being called from Main.dispose() in A0, which removed it with a twenty-line note on why. A4 records that rather than re-doing it
  • The browser matrix asks for pixel identity, not for a picture. Two runtimes, two configurations, two profiles, one disposed in each order — and the survivor's frame is compared byte for byte against the one it drew before. “Still draws something” is what the previous two-viewer test asked, and a viewer whose neighbour has just pulled its textures out from under it still draws something. Two consecutive frames are asserted identical first, so the comparison is a measurement rather than a coin toss
  • Eight deliberate breaks, eight failing suites. Shared default runtime (5 cases), default configuration not by identity (6), resolution dropping the configuration it was handed (2), Edge keeping an untracked registry (4), Edge never forgetting one (2), BlueprintJS disposing a runtime it was handed (1), dispose() releasing nothing (2), Main ignoring the document's profile (1)
  • The library bundle question is closed, and the limit came down. A0 raised it, A1, A2 and A3 each deferred it, and A4 tripped it as tools/budget.json said it would. The two artifacts wanted different answers and got them: the IIFE is minified — a browser downloads it as written, so its 37% of docblocks was a cost paid by every end user for documentation none of them would read — and the ESM is not, because a bundler strips comments on the consumer's way to production and those comments are the JSDoc a typed consumer reads on hover. 463.7 KB gzipped to 220.8 KB, a 52.4% cut, and lib-iife-gzip came DOWN from 459 KB to 233 KB. No source changed; the minified bundle was verified rather than assumed to work — it parses, exposes all 170 exports, and its DesignRuntime still holds the module-level services by identity. The IIFE budget has stopped measuring comments and started measuring code
  • 1,147 headless tests (34 new) and 41 in the browser (4 new), all green. Coverage 79.13 statements / 67.15 branches / 77.62 functions / 79.06 lines, and three of the four thresholds ratcheted — mostly from the isolation suite mounting two whole viewers and disposing them in each order, which walks teardown a single-viewer test never reaches. The browser tier went from 67.1s to 77.0s for four tests that each mount two WebGL contexts and read back whole frames, which is what the pixel-identity claim costs
A5

IndexedDB persistence and asset delivery hardening

doneH-81–2 weekshard dep: A1

Objective. Move the draft off the synchronous 5 MiB main-thread store, and put a manifest between the catalog and the network so assets can be versioned without renaming a single file.

ScopeExcluded
An async document repository on IndexedDB · a minimal synchronous recovery pointer · quota and corruption handling · AssetManifest + AssetResolver · availability, preload and per-asset budgets Draco, meshopt, KTX2 — per P6, and unchanged · texture resizing, an art-direction call · a recent-files list, which useAutosave argues against on purpose · any CDN being configured; A5 makes one possible

Tasks — persistence

  1. Repository interface first, with the current localStorage implementation behind it. Ships green with no behaviour change and makes the swap a one-line change
  2. IndexedDB implementation. Web Storage is capped at 5 MiB local per origin (10 MiB across both stores) and its API is synchronous, so a furnished design is written on the main thread against a ceiling useAutosave already anticipates — the first QuotaExceededError disables autosave permanently for the session. IndexedDB's quota is a share of disk rather than a fixed few megabytes
  3. Keep a small synchronous pointer in localStorage, and justify it rather than assume it: this is K-6. pagehide cannot await a promise, so the pointer records “a draft exists, at this key, at this timestamp” while the body lives in IndexedDB. Kilobytes, synchronous, always completes
  4. Quota, corruption and rollback. Quota exceeded prunes older drafts before giving up; an unreadable record is treated as no draft, which is what readDraft already does; a store at an unknown version is left untouched and reported rather than migrated speculatively
  5. Reuse A1's pipeline. A recovered draft is a document like any other — parse, validate, migrate, swap. No second loading path

Tasks — assets

  1. AssetManifest: logical name → {url, bytes, hash, kind}, generated by the existing pipeline in asset-pipeline/ and committed. It is a build artefact of tooling that already exists
  2. AssetResolver consults it. Save files keep logical names; the resolver supplies the physical URL. This is what makes versioning possible without renaming a file — the constraint P6 discovered and tests/asset-integrity.test.js defends. A configurable base makes a CDN a deployment choice
  3. Availability as a policy, not a console line. The resolver knows what should exist, so a missing asset is a typed failure through A1's structured result and the UI can name the item
  4. Preload and prefetch. The manifest carries sizes, so the catalog can prefetch likely items at idle priority and the loading indicator can show real progress
  5. Integrity where it is worth it. Hashes are in the manifest; enforcement matters for a CDN deployment and not for same-origin public/. Recorded and made available, not switched on by default
  6. Per-asset budgets beside P6's per-file and per-tree ceilings: the download, decode and GPU cost of placing one catalog item
RATIONALE · ALTERNATIVES · COMPATIBILITY
  • Why not hashed filenames. Vite copies public/ as-is and never hashes it, while imported assets do get hashed — so the mechanism exists and is unusable here, because these URLs are written into saved documents. The indirection has to be at runtime
  • Alternative rejected — move assets out of public/ into the build graph. Gets hashing for free and breaks every saved design, plus the deliberate relative-URL arrangement index.html documents
  • Alternative rejected — Cache API / service worker. Better offline story, much larger surface, and a service worker's update semantics are their own project
  • Alternative considered — keep localStorage and cap the draft size. One line, and it makes the failure quieter rather than rarer
  • Compatibility. Existing drafts are read once from localStorage and migrated to IndexedDB. Asset URLs in saved files are unchanged, which is the point of the sprint
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • M-9 = 0 bytes. No document-sized synchronous write on the main thread; the recovery pointer is under 1 kB
  • A design far larger than 5 MiB autosaves and recovers — the case that permanently disables autosave today
  • Quota exhaustion degrades rather than fails: prune, then report, never lose the in-memory design
  • Every asset URL still resolves — tests/asset-integrity.test.js passes unchanged, and is extended to check the manifest against the tree in both directions
  • Every fixture design opens with its assets, through the resolver, with no file renamed
  • Observability. Repository reports bytes used against quota estimate; the resolver reports resolutions, misses and preload hit rate
  • Rollback. Two independent halves behind interfaces — the localStorage repository and the identity resolver both stay in the tree as the fallback implementation
  • DoD. Three tiers green · budgets green · docs/save-format.md gains the storage section · docs/getting-started.md documents the manifest and the CDN base · CHANGELOG
LANDED — A5
  • M-9 = 0 bytes, and it is measured on both sides in one test. The same six-megabyte design: localStorage refuses it outright — which is the finding, not a hypothetical — and IndexedDB stores and returns it byte for byte. A one-megabyte design, which the old path can hold, reports the whole body as a synchronous main-thread write through the old repository and zero through the new one
  • The recovery pointer earns its keep by being detectable, not by rescuing anything. Nothing can rescue a write pagehide did not finish; writing the document synchronously as a fallback would hand back the metric to get the guarantee back. What the pointer does is record the timestamp the body write was about to carry, so a pointer newer than the body means the tail was lost — and the recovery prompt says how much instead of implying the draft is current. Under a kilobyte, asserted on every write rather than described
  • Both storage implementations stay, and the fallback is not a stepping stone. jsdom has no IndexedDB, so createDraftRepository() selects localStorage there — which means the pre-A5 autosave assertions were left completely untouched and still pass. That is the compatibility claim, asserted by not editing anything
  • The asset manifest is fetched, not bundled, and the reason is a number. 370 entries is 58 kB of JSON, 17.6 kB gzipped. In the library bundle that is charged to every consumer whether or not they serve this project's assets — and it would go straight through the IIFE ceiling A4 had just tightened by minifying to get under. It is a runtime input; a library with no manifest resolves every name to itself, which is what it did before
  • H-8's claim, in one assertion: the URL the browser fetches moves and the name in the document does not. metadata.modelUrl keeps the logical name, so a resolved URL never gets baked into a save file — which would be the same failure one level down
  • Scene.setItemLoader is handed the logical name too, and that is deliberate rather than an oversight. An embedder's own loader IS their asset pipeline, and a resolver they did not configure must not rewrite what reaches it
  • Availability is answered before the network. A resolver with a manifest knows what the build ships, so a name it does not have fails through the ordinary EVENT_ITEM_LOADED path with a message that names the item — and missing() is false whenever there is no manifest, which is what keeps a build that ships none behaving exactly as before
  • A new KIND of budget, not a new number. catalog-item-largest asks what placing one chair costs: the .glb, every image it references, and the thumbnail already on screen. Across all 168 items the median is 17 kB and the worst is 1.53 MB — 90x the median, and invisible to both the per-file and per-tree ceilings that already existed. Same shape of blind spot P6 found one level up
  • The coverage ratchet BIT for the first time, and the tests it forced were the right ones. A5's code landed at 78.01 statements against a floor of 79 and failed all four thresholds. The floor does not come down, so the gap was closed with tests — and the largest hole was the IndexedDB repository at 88 uncovered lines, reachable only from the browser tier. It now has a fake IDBFactory that reaches the quota retry, the version refusal and the failed open, none of which a real IndexedDB produces on request. Final: 79.47 lines, 79.47 statements, 68.12 branches, 78.14 functions, with branches and functions both raised
  • Nine deliberate breaks; eight failed the headless suite and the ninth failed nothing. Making the IndexedDB repository report the body size as a synchronous write passed every headless test — because with no IndexedDB the write never succeeds, so a zero there is also what a dishonest implementation reports. The browser tier caught it instantly (expected 6291362 to be +0). The headless test was rewritten to assert the unavailable path and to say where the M-9 claim is actually gated. That is the metric of this sprint, and its tier-1 assertion was a tautology until it was broken on purpose
  • The manifest generator found a bug in itself before shipping. Probing the store version before opening it read as more careful and was wrong: open(name) with no version creates the database at version 1 with no object store, and the follow-up open at version 1 fires no upgrade event — so the store was never created and every read came back null. jsdom, having no IndexedDB, could not have caught it; the browser tier did, on the first run
  • 1,203 headless tests (56 new) and 49 in the browser (8 new), all green. The browser tier stayed at 77.0s: IndexedDB is fast, unlike everything else in that tier
  • One number to watch, deliberately not acted on. lib-esm-gzip is at 0.8% headroom and was not raised. It is passing, and raising a limit before it is breached is slack the change did not earn — the argument tools/budget.json has been making since P6. The next thing that adds a docblock to the library will trip it, which is what a budget is for. Note what that entry actually guards: the ESM build externalises three and bezier-js, and losing that config takes it from ~124 KB to ~423 KB. Any ceiling well under 423 KB still catches the failure it exists to catch

If only two: A0 and A1 — because H-2 is the one finding here that loses somebody's work.

Method — RM-003. Full read of the library's resource, loading, change-propagation, identity, history, isolation and asset paths against the working tree at fff1ebe on branch amit, plus README.md, CHANGELOG.md, package.json, vite.config.mjs, tsconfig.json, docs/architecture.md, docs/events.md, tests/README.md and the RM-001/RM-002 sections above. The four findings marked measured were produced by running the library headlessly and counting — mesh churn per update(), EVENT_UPDATED per document open, the malformed-document corpus, and room identity across a wall split — not by reading the code. renderer.info.memory, LoadingManager.abort() and FileLoader's AbortSignal.any composition were verified in the installed three@0.185.1 rather than from documentation. Two documentation inaccuracies were found and are recorded in §16 H-1 rather than fixed, since this is a planning pass: docs/architecture.md:71–74 and blueprint.js:200–203 both state the model layer holds no GPU resources, and it holds three meshes per room and one per half-edge.

DELIVERY & PRECISION · DRAWING Nº RM-004

Delivery & Precision Program

Three short sprints against what two hardening programs deliberately left alone. Every number below was produced by running something — the payload was parsed chunk by chunk, the wall identity was round-tripped through the save format, and the type ledger was recounted rather than quoted. Two of the three findings contradict what the repository currently says about itself.

§ 20 · EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

Three findings, and no theme underneath them

RM-002 had one: seven defects with real costs. RM-003 had one: nothing owned anything. This program has three findings with no common cause, and that is the most useful thing in this section — the structural work is done, and what is left is arithmetic.

100%
of vertex attributes in the catalog are float32. Position, normal and UV, in all 165 .glb files, with no exceptions
410/410
index buffers declared uint32 whose primitive has fewer than 65,536 vertices. The largest in the catalog has 1,174
0/15
wall ids that survive a document load, across three fixtures. getUuid() survives 15/15, which is what makes the fix cheap
60
typecheck errors in the SFCs. tsconfig.json says 53, and its own breakdown sums to 61

Why these three and not others. Each was named as deferred by the program that found it, and each was deferred for a reason that has since expired. A5 put a manifest between the catalog and the network and said compression was out of scope because the seam did not exist yet; it exists now. A3 named the wall-id gap and said it wanted its own thinking rather than a corner of that sprint; this is that corner. And R-07's ledger has been a ratchet on paper since P2 — A2 recounted it once and found it had been wrong since before A1, which is exactly what has happened again.

What this program is not. It is not a rewrite, it adds no architectural concept, and no sprint here depends on another. All three are independently shippable, independently revertible and independently skippable. §23's guardrails carry over unchanged from §18, and one is worth restating up front: a saved design must open in a build that predates this program, which is the constraint that decides most of B1's design.

Architecture health metrics

Continuing RM-003's numbering. Five rows, every one computable today by a command in the repository rather than by a judgement call. Two targets are deliberately left to a measured trial rather than guessed at here, and are marked as such.
