§ 21 · DECISION LOG

Three findings, with the evidence

All three are marked measured because all three were produced by running something against the tree at 3cf1c24. Where a number contradicts what the repository says about itself, both are given.

NºFindingSeverityWhereSprint
G-1The catalog is served in the shape it was authored in, and nothing decides otherwisemedpublic/models/ · 370 manifest entriesB1
G-2A wall's identity is stable in memory and absent from diskmedmodel/wall.js:88 · model/floorplan.js:374, 837B2
G-3The JSDoc names types that do not resolve, and the ledger counting them is wronglowtsconfig.json:58–68 · three/main.jsB3

G-1 · The catalog is served as authored med measured

A5 gave every asset a logical name and a manifest that can point it somewhere else. Nothing yet uses that to change what is in the file. Parsing all 370 entries — and every .glb chunk by chunk, splitting the BIN into the buffer views an accessor points at, the ones an image points at, and the remainder — gives the composition:

KindCountRawgzip −9gz/rawWhat that ratio means
model (.glb/.gltf)1685.40 MB2.22 MB41.1%Float arrays compress. Transport already takes more than half of this
model-texture1613.05 MB2.95 MB96.8%PNG and JPEG are already entropy-coded. Transport takes 3.2%
texture140.94 MB0.82 MB87.3%Same
environment10.82 MB0.82 MB99.9%Same, and it is one file
thumbnail260.41 MB0.40 MB96.5%Same
Total37010.62 MB7.21 MB67.9%

Inside the 165 .glb files the split is 4.67 MB of geometry, 0.41 MB of JSON, and zero embedded images — every texture in this catalog is an external file, which is what makes the two halves separable at all. So the payload is 44.0% geometry and 49.2% pixels, and the two halves need opposite treatments: geometry is already 59.4% compressed by transport and wants a format change to gain more, while pixels are 96.8% incompressible over the wire and are paid in full on every load.

Two facts about the geometry make the first half unusually tractable, and neither needs a codec:

  • Every vertex attribute in the catalog is float32. Not most — all of them, across POSITION, NORMAL and TEXCOORD_0. A furniture model spans a couple of metres; storing its positions to 24 bits of mantissa is precision nothing downstream can use, and KHR_mesh_quantization exists for exactly this
  • Every uint32 index buffer is twice as wide as it needs to be. 410 primitives declare 32-bit indices; all 410 have fewer than 65,536 vertices, and the largest primitive in the entire catalog has 1,174. That is 152 KB recoverable by changing one field, with no visual difference of any kind and no new dependency. Corrected by the B1 trial: 152 KB is the RAW figure and the served saving is 13 KB. Three zero bytes in every four is exactly what gzip already eats — this document's own warning about headline ratios, applied to everything except this sentence.

Why the target for M-12 is left blank. Draco, meshopt and KTX2 all have well-known typical ratios and none of them has been run against this catalog. Quoting a published ratio as a target here would be exactly the failure this document's method exists to avoid, and it would be quoting it against a payload that gzip has already taken 32% of. B1's first task is the trial that produces the number; the budget ratchets to what the trial earns.

The constraint that shapes this sprint. A saved design names its models by URL, and A5's whole argument was that those URLs are a published contract. Changing a file's contents keeps that contract; changing its name or its decoder requirement does not. A build that ships a Draco-compressed .glb under the same name has silently made every older build unable to open documents that were saved before this program — the manifest's kind and a per-entry decoder hint are what keep that from happening, and they are the reason A5 built it.

G-2 · Identity is stable in memory and absent from disk med measured

A3 gave a wall an assigned id (wall.js:88) and left getUuid() returning the derived corner pair, because that is what the save file records. Round-tripping three fixtures through saveFloorplan() and loadFloorplan() measures the gap exactly:

FixtureWallswall.id survivesgetUuid() survivesCorner ids survive
simple-room40 / 44 / 48 / 8
curved-walls40 / 44 / 48 / 8
rich-design70 / 77 / 714 / 14

The second and third columns together are the finding. Nothing about a wall's identity survives, and everything needed to reconstruct it does — corner ids are persisted and come back intact, so the corner pair is a stable seed sitting in the file already. This is the same shape as A3's room-identity fix, where the old derived key became the seed of the new assigned id and a first open was a no-op.

The blocker A3 named is real, and it is worse than "two walls between one pair is conceivable". It is reachable through the ordinary API with no guard anywhere:

  • Floorplan.newWall() (floorplan.js:374) pushes a wall with no duplicate check. Calling it twice on the same pair produces two walls whose getUuid() is the same string
  • It survives the save format. Two records written, two walls rebuilt, one distinct getUuid() — because loadFloorplan() (floorplan.js:837) calls newWall() once per record and the guard it would need does not exist there either
  • Direction matters and must not. newWall(a,b) and newWall(b,a) give getUuid() values that differ as written and are equal when sorted. A seed that does not canonicalise gives the same wall two identities depending on which way it was drawn; a seed that only canonicalises collides on the duplicate case. It needs both: sort the pair, then add an ordinal

Not in any fixture. None of the three fixtures contains a corner pair with two walls, so this collision has no coverage today and cannot be caught by extending the corpus. It needs a constructed case, and that case is the sprint's real test rather than an afterthought — the failure mode of getting it wrong is two walls that share an id, which is the collision A3 removed from rooms, reintroduced one layer down.

G-3 · The JSDoc names types that do not resolve low measured

R-07's ledger in tsconfig.json is a ratchet with a recount rule, and A2 already caught it drifting once. Recounting it at 3cf1c24: the headline is right and the breakdown is not.

AreaErrorsWhat they are
src/scripts/three148108 of them in main.js alone — a quarter of the whole surface in one file
src/scripts/model11446 in floorplan.js, 20 in corner.js, 18 in room.js
src/scripts/items6331 in item.js. Includes 8 that are not type errors at all — see below
src/scripts/floorplanner4118 in floorplanner_view.js, 15 in floorplanner.js
src/app SFCs6028 in components/, 24 in inspector/, 8 in App.vue and siblings. The ledger says 53
src/app/main.js1The bootstrap, excluded from coverage too
Total427Which the ledger has right

The ledger is wrong twice over, and in different directions: it states 53 SFC errors where there are 60, and its own itemisation — 28 plus 24 plus 9 — sums to 61, agreeing with neither. A ledger whose parts do not add up to its total is a comment, which is the thing A2's note said it must not become.

Two clusters in the 427 are not really type errors and are worth separating out, because they are deletions rather than annotations:

  • 8 × TS6053 — files that do not exist. items/in_wall_item.js and items/in_wall_floor_item.js open with /// <reference path="../../lib/three.d.ts" /> and three more like it, pointing at .ts files from the pre-migration source that were never converted and are not in the tree. Dead directives, surviving because nothing resolved them until --checkJs did
  • 3 × TS1003 — JSDoc that does not parse. floorplan.js lines 254, 269 and 291 carry @see <https://threejs.org/...>, and the angle brackets make TypeScript expect an identifier. Three comments that are literally unparseable, fixed by removing two characters each

The rest divide predictably: 181 TS2339 (property does not exist on type), 43 TS2304 (cannot find name — JSDoc referring to Wall, Floorplan, Room, Corner and Mesh with nothing importing those types), and 109 across TS2531/TS18046/TS18047, which are the strict-null family that found real defects in P2.

§ 22 · RISKS & DEPENDENCIES

What could go wrong

Shorter than §17, and deliberately: no sprint here depends on another, so the sequencing risk that dominated RM-003 is absent. What remains is concentrated almost entirely in B1.

NºRiskImpactLikelihoodMitigation
L-1A re-encoded asset changes what is drawn. Quantisation is lossy by construction. Positions snapped to a 16-bit grid over the wrong bounding box, or normals oct-encoded badly, produce seams, faceting or a model that sits fractionally through the floorhighmedThe pixel tier is the oracle, and it already exists: P5's browser tests read whole frames back. Every re-encoded model is placed and compared frame-to-frame against the same model before re-encoding, and the per-model bounding box is asserted unchanged within a stated tolerance. A model that cannot pass ships unquantised — per-asset, not all-or-nothing
L-2A decoder requirement becomes a save-format break. A document saved today names a model by URL; if that URL later needs a Draco decoder the old build does not have, the document stops opening in ithighmedThis is what the A5 manifest was built for and it is a hard gate, not a guideline. Anything needing a decoder is a new entry, the old file stays, and the manifest points current builds at the new one. tests/asset-integrity.test.js checks the tree both directions and gets a case per codec
L-3The budget ratchets on a number the trial did not earn. Pressure to write down a good-looking figure before it is measured — the exact failure §21 G-1 declines to commitmedmedM-12's target stays empty until the trial reports, and the trial is task 1 of B1 rather than its conclusion. The two metrics that are pinned (M-10, M-11) are arithmetic and cannot come out differently
L-4A wall id seeded from corners collides, giving two walls one identity — the room-identity defect A3 removed, reintroduced one layer downhighmedCanonicalise the pair and carry an ordinal, and prove both halves: a wall drawn in reverse gets the same id, and two walls between one pair get different ones. Neither case is in any fixture, so both are constructed tests written before the change
L-5Type annotations change behaviour. Adding a type should be inert; adding a cast to silence an error can hide the defect the checker foundmedmedP2's rule, restated: an error is either fixed or annotated, never cast away. Any @ts-ignore or as carries a comment naming why, and the count of them is reported in the sprint's landing note so it cannot grow quietly
L-6Coverage floor blocks a sprint. A5 was the first time the ratchet bit rather than moved, and B1 adds tooling that is awkward to reach from a testlowmedUnchanged from K-9, and A5 is the precedent: the floor did not move and the tests it forced were the right ones. Encoding tooling lives in tools/, which is outside the coverage include, so the exposure is smaller than A5's

Sequencing

There is none to speak of. B1, B2 and B3 touch disjoint parts of the tree — public/ and tools/, the model layer, and annotations respectively — and can run in any order or in parallel. If only one thing gets done: B1, because it is the only one a user can feel. If only two: B1 and B2, because B3 changes no behaviour by design and is therefore the one that can always wait.

§ 23 · COMPATIBILITY & NON-GOALS

What must not change

§18's guardrails carry over verbatim and are not restated. What follows is what this program adds to them.

  • A design saved by 2.0.0 opens in 2.0.0 after this program. Not "opens in the version that ships it" — opens in the one that came before. Asset contents may change; asset names, and the set of names a build can resolve, may only grow
  • No asset is deleted or renamed. A re-encoded model is an addition, and the manifest is what makes the old name keep working. This is the load-bearing constraint on B1 and it comes straight from H-8
  • No new runtime dependency without a budget line. A Draco decoder is roughly 200 KB of WASM; loading it eagerly would undo the payload win it enables. Corrected: 200 KB is the raw wasm. Gzipped, three's glTF-only decoder is 74.8 KB — 63.2 for the wasm and 11.6 for its wrapper. If it ships, it ships lazily and behind the resolver, and it gets its own budget entry
  • Wall ids stay out of the save file. B2 reconstructs them on load from data the file already has, for the same reason A3 kept room ids unpersisted: a file identifies a wall by its corners, and that is a description another build can read
  • No threshold comes down. Coverage floors and size budgets are ratchets. A5 established what happens when one bites, and the answer was tests

Explicitly out of scope

  • An asset pipeline anybody has to run to develop. Re-encoding is a deliberate, committed operation with its output in the tree, exactly as make-asset-manifest.mjs is. A checkout must build and run with no extra tooling installed
  • The remaining 367 library type errors. B3 takes the SFC tier and the two deletion clusters. three/main.js alone is 108 errors and is a sprint of its own, if it is ever worth one
  • A duplicate-wall guard in newWall(). B2 makes the collision survivable; deciding whether two walls between one pair should be preventable at all is a modelling question, not an identity one
  • Progressive or streamed loading, and a service worker. Both are real answers to payload and neither is this program
§ 24 · IMPLEMENTATION

Three sprints

Each is independently shippable and independently revertible, and each fits in a single commit range. Effort assumes the same single-developer cadence as RM-003.

B1

Asset precision and delivery

doneG-11–2 weeksno dependencies

Objective. Stop serving the catalog in the shape its authoring tool emitted, using the seam A5 built and without renaming a single file. Measure before choosing, and let the trial set the budget rather than the other way round.

ScopeExcluded
A measured codec trial · index narrowing · attribute quantisation · texture re-encoding · manifest kind and decoder hints · a lazily-loaded decoder if the trial earns one · budget ratchet Renaming or deleting any asset · a required build step · progressive loading · a service worker · the 3 .gltf files, which are 0.32 MB and a different container

Tasks

  1. The trial comes first, and it is the deliverable of week one. Encode the whole catalog four ways — index narrowing alone, KHR_mesh_quantization, meshopt, Draco — and report bytes raw and gzipped against the 4.67 MB baseline, plus decode cost and decoder weight. gzip is the comparison that matters: transport already takes 59.4% of the geometry, so a codec's headline ratio is not its marginal win
  2. Take the free half regardless of the trial. 410 index buffers narrow from uint32 to uint16 with no codec, no dependency and no possible visual difference — 152 KB, and it is the change that proves the re-encode harness works before anything lossy runs through it. Subsumed: Draco replaces the index buffers wholesale, so this was measured (0.6% served) and then overtaken.
  3. Quantise attributes per asset, not per catalog. Each model is re-encoded, placed, and compared frame-to-frame against its unquantised self; one that does not match ships as it was. The per-asset decision is recorded in the manifest so the tree says which models are quantised and which are not
  4. Texture re-encoding is the other 49.2%, and gzip is doing nothing for it. Measure KTX2/Basis against the 161 model-texture files for bytes, decode time and GPU memory — supercompressed textures stay compressed on the GPU, which is a second win the byte count does not show
  5. Anything needing a decoder is a new manifest entry. The old name resolves to the old file forever; the manifest points current builds at the new one, and tests/asset-integrity.test.js gets a case per codec asserting both still resolve
  6. Ratchet the budget to what was earned. Runtime assets, Largest single asset and Costliest catalog item all come down by the measured amount, and a decoder that ships gets a budget line of its own
RATIONALE · ALTERNATIVES · COMPATIBILITY
  • Why now. P6 deferred this and A5 deferred it again, both times for the same reason: there was no indirection between the name in a document and the file on disk, so changing an asset meant changing a save-format contract. A5 built that indirection. This is the first sprint where the deferral no longer applies
  • Alternative rejected — compress everything with the best-looking codec. Draco has the best headline ratio and the worst decode cost, needs a 200 KB WASM decoder, and is measured against raw bytes in most published comparisons rather than against gzip. On a payload transport already halves, that comparison is not the one that matters
  • Alternative rejected — a build-time pipeline. Re-encoding on every build makes a checkout depend on native tooling and makes the served bytes a function of who ran the build. The committed-output model is what make-asset-manifest.mjs already established
  • Compatibility. No file is renamed or removed, so every existing document keeps resolving. A build with no manifest resolves every name to itself, unchanged since A5
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • M-10 = 0% and M-11 = 0, both by walking the tree rather than by trusting the encoder's report
  • Every re-encoded model matches its predecessor in the pixel tier, and its bounding box within a stated tolerance. A model that fails ships unquantised and the manifest says so
  • A design saved before this sprint opens after it, byte-identical on re-save — the H-8 claim, re-asserted at the level of file contents
  • Observability. resolver.stats() gains the codec mix actually served, so "what is this build shipping" is answerable at runtime rather than from the tree
  • Rollback. Per-asset and total. Point a manifest entry back at the original file and that model is unchanged; revert the manifest and the whole sprint is
  • DoD. Three tiers green · the trial's numbers recorded in the LANDED block whichever way they came out · docs/architecture.md gains a delivery section · budgets ratcheted · CHANGELOG
LANDED — B1
  • The trial ran first and it decided the sprint, which is what it was for. Four encodings over all 165 models, raw and gzipped: index narrowing 0.6% off served bytes, quantization 17.4%, meshopt 39.7%, Draco 63.8% net of its decoder — 2.094 MB of served geometry down to 0.687. Draco decodes 3.26× slower, which is 0.36 ms per model on a worker thread, against 0.5 MB more saved than meshopt. The catalog ships at 5.08 MB → 1.92 MB and the whole tree at 10.62 → 7.45 MB
  • M-12's target was left blank on purpose and the trial is why that mattered. Every published Draco ratio is quoted against raw bytes, and gzip had already taken 59.4% of this geometry before any codec ran. A target written in §21 would have been a number copied from a README; the one in the table now is a number this catalog produced
  • Two of this document's own claims were wrong, and both are marked in place rather than edited away. The "free 152 KB" of index narrowing is 152 KB raw and 13 KB served — three zero bytes in four is exactly what gzip eats, which is the warning §21 G-1 gives about everybody else's numbers and not about its own. And the decoder called "roughly 200 KB of WASM" is 74.8 KB gzipped. Neither changed the decision; both would have changed how it was argued
  • The verification was wrong three times before it was right, and each wrong version looked like a pass. Comparing bounding boxes reported 0.00002 cm — blind by construction, because Draco quantizes over the box, so the box is the last thing to move. Comparing vertex k to vertex k reported 243 cm, because edgebreaker reorders. Comparing sorted lists reported 185 cm on a model 200 cm across, because one merge and one split shift every subsequent element by one. Nearest-neighbour over world-space positions is the fourth version and the one that answers the question actually being asked
  • What caught the first wrong version was model-conversion.test.js, not the encoder. At 14 bits it failed 100 of 194 cases — every model missing on bounds and surface area — while the encoder's own check reported microns. Raising to 24-bit positions costs 6.7 points of compression and takes it to 22, all of them one assertion. Those seven bits are the difference between "the geometry moved" and "the hash changed"
  • The gates were moved to the oracle rather than the oracle to the gates. The encoder now refuses any model whose triangle count changes, whose distinct-position count changes, or whose worst vertex moves past 5 µm — the same tolerance the conversion suite already applied. 13 of 165 models trip one and ship exactly as authored. Losing compression on thirteen files costs kilobytes; weakening a 194-case oracle to accommodate them would have cost the ability to notice the next change of the same kind
  • One expectation retired, and it is the only one. The position hash — a sha of every distinct vertex at 3dp, which is bit-exactness renamed and which no lossy codec satisfies at any depth. At 30 bits it would pass on some models and fail on others, which is worse than failing honestly. The distinct-position count stays exact, as do bounds, surface area and triangle count at their original tolerances. The per-vertex guarantee moved to where it can be a number: 0.38 µm worst across the catalog, recorded per model and asserted from a checkout
  • The browser tier passed on the first run after the catalog was compressed, and that was not evidence. All 49 cases green — because every browser test stubs the loader, and not one had ever fetched a .glb. A catalog no build could decode would have sailed through. tests/browser/draco-models.test.js is the first to load a real model, and it asserts the frame changes when it arrives, because a decoder returning empty buffers produces a model that loads and draws nothing
  • One import cost 489 KB gzipped, and it was not the loader. three's DRACOLoader references its bundled decoders through new URL(..., import.meta.url), which a bundler reads as assets to emit — including a 719 KB pure-JS fallback for browsers with no WebAssembly, which cannot run WebGL either. The library IIFE went 226 → 716 KB and the demo 317 → 487 before a build plugin stripped them. It throws if it matches nothing, so a three upgrade that renames those constants fails the build instead of quietly restoring half a megabyte
  • writeBinary() embeds external images, which for this catalog is a defect and not an inefficiency. 21 models reference a texture and two of those textures are shared; embedding would have duplicated 933.6 KB while serving the same pixels twice, and turned a 22 KB model into 729 KB. The encoder assembles the GLB container by hand to keep every image a URI, and a test asserts no model smuggled one in
  • Task 4 was measured and deliberately not shipped. KTX2/Basis needs KTX-Software binaries that are not installed here, and quoting a published ratio in place of a measurement is the one thing this programme refuses to do. What is measurable is arithmetic, and it reframes the problem: 202 images weigh 5.23 MB on disk, transport takes 4.4% of that because they are already entropy-coded, and they occupy 164 MB of GPU memory once uploaded with mip chains. BC7/ETC2 would take that to 41 MB
  • A third kind of budget, for the same reason there was a second. rooms/textures/Ground_4K.jpg is 73 KB on disk and 16.9 MB in VRAM — 230×, and invisible to every ceiling in the file, all of which measure bytes at rest. P6 found a per-file blind spot, A5 found a per-catalog-item one, and texture-vram is this one. decoder-total is beside it, giving the vendored decoder a line that moves only when three is upgraded
  • Four budgets came down, one went up, two are new. Runtime assets 11.16 → 7.82 MB, deployed tree 18.12 → 15.07, largest single asset 1.38 MB → 889 KB, costliest catalog item 1.61 MB → 791 KB. The largest asset in the tree is no longer a model — it is the Garden environment map, which is the signal that geometry has stopped being the problem. lib-esm-gzip rose 128,000 → 134,000, breached by 26 bytes, exactly as the A5 note predicted when it declined to raise it pre-emptively
  • Seven deliberate breaks, seven failing gates, and one of them failed to fail first time because the fixture was one of the 13 uncompressed models — a bad break, not a bad gate, and re-run against an encoded one it failed correctly. 1,211 headless tests (8 new) and 54 in the browser (5 new)
B2

Wall identity across a document load

doneG-23–5 daysno dependencies

Objective. Reconstruct a wall's id on load from the corner pair the file already contains, so that wall selection survives an undo and wall-bound items stop reloading — the two consequences A3 named when it deferred this.

ScopeExcluded
A deterministic seed from the canonicalised corner pair plus an ordinal · reconstruction in loadFloorplan() · lifting A3's wall-bound-item carve-out · the two constructed collision tests Writing wall ids into the save file · a duplicate-wall guard in newWall() · touching room identity, which A3 settled

Tasks

  1. Write the two collision tests first, because neither case exists in any fixture: a wall drawn b→a must get the same id as the same wall drawn a→b, and two walls between one pair must get different ids that are stable across a reload. Both fail before the change
  2. Seed from the sorted pair plus an ordinal. Sorting is what makes direction irrelevant; the ordinal is what makes the duplicate case survivable. The ordinal is the wall's position among the walls sharing that pair, in file order — which is stable across a round trip because saveFloorplan() writes this.walls in array order and loadFloorplan() rebuilds in file order. Worth checking rather than assuming: a wall missing a corner is skipped on save (floorplan.js:682), so the ordinal is stable among walls that survive, which is the only set it has to be stable across
  3. Reconstruct in loadFloorplan(), not in the Wall constructor. A wall built by an editing action keeps its Utils.guide(), exactly as today; only the load path derives. This is the A3 pattern — a first open is a no-op and nothing about editing changes
  4. Lift the wall-bound-item carve-out. A3 excluded WallItem from item preservation because a HalfEdge could not be found again after a load. With stable wall ids it can, and the exclusion is what M-8's carve-out was about
  5. Assert selection survives. Select a wall, undo an unrelated edit, and the inspector is still bound to the same wall — the user-visible half, and the one a person would report
RATIONALE · ALTERNATIVES · COMPATIBILITY
  • Why it is cheap. Because corner ids already survive a load, 15 out of 15 — the seed is sitting in the file and needs no format change. That measurement is what turns this from a save-format decision into a load-path one
  • Alternative rejected — persist the wall id. It would work and it costs a format field. A3's argument holds: a file identifies a wall by its corners, an id assigned by one build means nothing to another, and an id that dies with the wall cannot be brought back by redrawing it
  • Alternative rejected — sorted pair with no ordinal. Simpler, and it silently gives two walls one identity in the one case that matters. The collision is reachable through newWall() with no guard and it survives a round trip — measured, not assumed
  • Compatibility. Purely additive to the load path. No save-format change, no public API change, and a document that has never seen this build opens identically
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • M-13 = 15 of 15 across the three fixtures, by the same census that measured it at zero
  • Both collision cases pass, and both fail when the ordinal or the canonicalisation is removed — two of the sprint's deliberate breaks
  • Wall-bound items no longer reload on undo, closing A3's stated carve-out
  • Rollback. One function on the load path; reverting it restores Utils.guide() per wall and nothing else moves
  • DoD. Three tiers green · docs/architecture.md's identity section gains walls · docs/save-format.md records that wall ids are reconstructed and not stored · CHANGELOG
LANDED — B2
  • M-13 = 15 of 15, by the same census that measured it at zero, plus the property an undo actually needs: two floorplans loaded from one file name their walls identically. The id is wall:<lo>~<hi>, sorted, with #n for the second and subsequent walls on a pair. Nothing was added to the save format — the seed was already in the file, which is what made this three days rather than a format migration
  • The tests were written first and 8 of 10 failed, which is the point; the 2 that passed are the interesting ones. "Two walls between one pair get different ids" passed vacuously against random guids, and had to keep passing afterwards — the same shape as A0's borrowed-mesh guard, green from the start and meaningful only at the end
  • A3's blocker was real, and it was not the one A3 named. The note said lifting the wall-bound-item carve-out needed wall ids that survive a load. It did, and that turned out to be the easy half: with stable ids the item still vanished, because reset() fires EVENT_DELETED on every wall and the item's own handler removed it before it could be re-bound. The item was killing itself on the way through, which is exactly the sentence A3's note gestured at without locating
  • A leaked listener, found while fixing that. changeWallEdge declared its handler as a fresh closure per call and then removed the new one from the old wall — detaching a function that had never been attached and leaving the previous one subscribed. Every re-bind leaked one, and there was no reference anywhere that could detach an item from a wall without destroying it. One held reference fixes the leak and is what makes releaseWall() possible; they are the same fix
  • Undo now reloads nothing at all. A3 got M-8 to zero for free-standing furniture and a window still reloaded on every restore. It does not now, and it comes back on the face it was on rather than the nearest one — the same answer everywhere except where two walls meet, which is where a window actually sits
  • Seven deliberate breaks, and two of them failed to fail. Both were real gaps rather than bad breaks. Numbering the ordinal by position in the FILE instead of per pair still produces unique, deterministic, round-trip-stable ids — and silently renames every wall whenever one is inserted before it, so adding a wall in one corner of a design would move the selection off a wall in the other. And stubbing the EVENT_DELETED handler to a no-op broke nothing, because nothing in the suite asserted that deleting a wall removes the window in it. Two tests now cover what the breaks walked through
  • One expectation retired, deliberately. A3's test asserted that a wall-bound item reloads and comes back as a different object — A3 pinning its own carve-out, and its note said as much. It asserts the replacement now: nothing reloads, the same object returns, and the wall lists it. Which list depends on addToWall: an InWallItem cuts a hole and lives in items, a WallItem hangs on the surface and lives in onItems — a distinction the first version of the assertion got wrong
  • A wall id still is not written to the file, and that is the A3 argument unchanged. A file identifies a wall by its corners and that is a description any build can read; an id assigned by one build would mean nothing to another, and an id that died with the wall could not be brought back by drawing it again. docs/save-format.md gives the derivation rule so a third-party reader can reproduce it
  • 1,225 headless tests (16 new), coverage 79.65 / 68.37 / 78.34 / 79.63. No threshold crossed a whole number, so none moved. No browser test was needed or added: this is a model-layer change with no pixels in it
B3

Type coverage: the application tier

doneG-31 weekno dependencies

Objective. Take the 60 errors in src/app to zero and delete the 11 that are not type errors at all, then fix the ledger that has now been wrong twice.

ScopeExcluded
60 SFC errors · 8 dead /// <reference> directives · 3 unparseable @see comments · the tsconfig.json ledger, recounted and with a rule that keeps it honest three/main.js's 108, and the rest of the 367 · any behavioural change · checkJs globally

Tasks

  1. Delete the 11 non-errors first. Eight /// <reference path="....ts"> directives in two items/ files pointing at files that do not exist, and three @see <url> comments whose angle brackets make the JSDoc unparseable. Pure removal, no annotation, and it takes the total to 416 before any real work starts
  2. Type the props that make a template checkable. The ledger already identifies the pattern — an untyped v-for prop leaves the whole template unverifiable, so typing the prop is what converts a cluster of errors into zero rather than one
  3. Fix, do not cast. The strict-null family found real defects in P2 and will again. Every @ts-ignore or as carries a reason and the count is reported in the landing note
  4. Add the pragma to every SFC that reaches zero, so the gate holds. An area that is clean and not opted in is not protected
  5. Rewrite the ledger with a recount rule. It has now drifted twice, and both times because it was edited rather than re-derived. Record the command that produces each number next to it, so the next sprint recomputes instead of incrementing
RATIONALE · ALTERNATIVES · COMPATIBILITY
  • Why the application tier and not the library. It is the smallest area that can go to zero, and a partial area cannot carry a pragma, which means it cannot be protected. The library's 367 do not have that property at any granularity short of a whole file
  • Alternative rejected — start with three/main.js. 108 errors, a quarter of the surface, in the most load-bearing file in the render path. Highest count is not highest value when the risk of touching it is this asymmetric
  • Alternative rejected — turn on checkJs globally and suppress. That converts 427 findings into 427 suppressions and calls it progress
  • Compatibility. Annotations and deletions of dead directives. Nothing here changes what any code does, which is also why it is the sprint to cut if time runs short
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • M-14 = 0. npx vue-tsc --noEmit --checkJs reports nothing under src/app, and the whole-tree total is 356
  • Every SFC carries the pragma, so npm run typecheck — not just the audit command — would fail on a regression
  • The ledger's parts sum to its total, and each number has the command that produced it written beside it
  • Suppression count reported, and it is a number the next sprint can only lower
  • Rollback. Annotations revert individually; nothing depends on anything else here
  • DoD. Three tiers green · tsconfig.json ledger recounted with its rule · CHANGELOG
LANDED — B3
  • M-14 = 0, and the whole-tree total is 356 — the number §21 G-3 predicted after the correction, arrived at the same way it was predicted: 427 minus 11 deletions minus 60 fixes. Every one of the 29 single-file components carries the pragma now, which matters more than the count
  • Most of the 60 was one omission repeated five times. A prop declared {type: Array} types every element unknown and takes the whole template with it: eight of CatalogDrawer's nine errors were that single missing annotation, and four other components had the same. §21 named the pattern from the outside and it held — typing the prop is what turns a cluster into zero rather than into one
  • The types went next to the data, not next to the component. CatalogSection and CatalogItem are declared in useCatalog.js where they are built, UnitChoice in useDisplayUnit.js, GridSpacing in useZoom2D.js. A typedef beside the consumer drifts from the producer the first time the data changes, which is the same failure the ledger itself keeps having
  • Zero suppressions added, and it is asserted rather than claimed. The 7 @ts-expect-errors in the tree are all P2's, each pinning a preserved-bug arity in utils.js. B3 added 7 type ASSERTIONS instead, each carrying a docblock for why the narrowing is sound — six of them the same shape, event.target narrowed to the input the component itself renders
  • A break that was supposed to fail did not, and it found the real hole. Removing a component's pragma and reintroducing the error it used to have gave a completely clean npm run typecheck: 0 errors, in a file containing a type error. checkJs is off, so the pragma IS the protection, and a pragma is one line anybody can delete with nothing anywhere noticing
  • So the opt-in stopped being documentary. tests/type-coverage.test.js asserts that every area which reached zero stays opted in, that no file carries @ts-nocheck, and that the suppression count can only fall. It deliberately does not pin the remaining 356: a ratchet that punishes somebody for improving something is worse than no ratchet
  • The ledger was wrong for the third time and now carries the command that produces each number. A2 found it had drifted since before A1; B3 found it claiming 53 SFC errors against a tree with 60 and an itemisation summing to 61. Both times the cause was editing rather than re-deriving, so every figure in tsconfig.json now sits next to the shell that regenerates it, under a rule that says recount, do not increment
  • 11 of the 71 were deletions, not annotations. Eight /// <reference path="....ts" /> directives pointing at pre-migration TypeScript files that have never existed here, and three @see <url> comments whose angle brackets made the JSDoc unparseable. They had survived because nothing resolved them until --checkJs did
  • One real defect, found by the checker as P2 said it would be. Typing InspectorPanel's selection prop as nullable — which its own default: null already said it was — surfaced a template reading props.selection.object with no guard. Reachable whenever the panel renders with nothing selected
  • 1,232 headless tests (7 new) and 54 in the browser, all green; 356 library errors remain and three/main.js is 108 of them. Coverage 79.62 / 68.34 / 78.34 / 79.60 — statements and branches each moved a fraction and no threshold crossed a whole number, so none moved. Nothing here changes behaviour, which was the point: the suite passing unchanged is the evidence
B4

Texture memory

doneL-43–5 daysafter B1's measurement

Objective. Take the texture VRAM that B1's census exposed, starting with the files that are larger than anything draws them at. Decide KTX2 on evidence rather than on a published ratio.

LANDED — B4
  • Texture VRAM 104.67 MB → 43.00 MB, by resizing five files and changing no code. Five textures were over a 1024px cap and are now under it. No format change, no new runtime path, no .glb touched — because the filenames did not move, every reference to them stayed correct. Disk fell 4.13 MB → 2.64 MB alongside it, and five budget limits came down: texture-vram, public-total, demo-total, public-largest and catalog-item-largest. None went up
  • The measurement B1 added was wrong, and correcting it was worth more than the sprint. B1 reported 164.41 MB of texture VRAM over 202 images. 174 of those are DOM thumbnails — <img :src> in the catalog drawer, which the browser decodes lazily and never uploads — and the manifest's own kind was mislabelling 148 of them, so anything branching on kind was wrong about them too. The honest figure was 28 textures holding 104.67 MB. A ceiling with 39% headroom over a wrong number is not a ceiling (59188dc)
  • KTX2 was built, measured, and turned down. The encoder works: 25 of 28 textures encode, 4.13 → 2.16 MB on disk, 78.50 MB of VRAM against this pass's 61.67. Declined for three reasons, with the numbers kept so it can be revisited on evidence. (i) 19 of the 28 textures — 72.78 MB, 70% of the total — live inside .glb files, and GLTFLoader resolves their image URIs relative to the model, bypassing A5's resolver entirely; KTX2 changes the extension, so all 19 would need rewriting and B1's Draco output would go through the pipeline a second time. (ii) KTX2Loader.load() throws without detectSupport(renderer), and texture_cache is deliberately page-wide and renderer-free — A0 found that coupling and A4 removed it. (iii) It compresses upscaled pixels very efficiently, which is solving the wrong problem well
  • Because the defect was resolution, not compression. Ground_4K.jpg is 1822×1822 built from a 73 KB source — 0.023 bytes per texel — and skybox.js tiles it at repeat.set(40, 40), so each tile covers a few dozen screen pixels. That is not a detailed image that compressed well; it is a small image that was enlarged, and mip 0 of it is memory nothing ever reads. The two levers compose: a later sprint can still encode the resized files and take the remaining 32 MB from a smaller starting point
  • The fidelity gate went through four wrong shapes before one that measures anything. Comparing the output to resample(input, outputDims) is circular — that expression is the output, so it reports zero for a broken resampler too. Upscaling the output back and comparing measures the detail the resize removed, which is the intended effect. PSNR at full resolution is the same mistake in decibels, and would refuse exactly the textures that need resizing most. What survived asks a different question — not "how much detail was lost" but "is this still the same image" — by averaging both to 64px, where every structural failure survives and genuine detail loss does not
  • And then that gate turned out to have a limit worth stating. Measured against a maximum-contrast square wave at exactly the comparison's Nyquist: an honest resize reads 2.261 while a 5% gamma error reads 1.249 — so on that class of image no threshold whatsoever separates them. On a realistic texture the separation is more than tenfold. The gate stayed at 2.0, which refuses the adversarial case, because every gate here can only fall back: a false refusal costs a missed saving that gets written into the report, and a false acceptance ships a broken texture
  • The JPEG quality was a guess, and the guess was the number that looked fine. The first docblock asserted q92 would put generation loss "around 1/255" without measuring. It lands at 2.536 — 18% of margin against a 3.0 gate, which the next texture added to the catalog would trip for no reason. Measured properly, q95 is the knee: 1.763 for +260 KB, after which each ~140 KB buys 15%
  • A throwaway measurement script resized the catalog by accident, and that found a real defect. It imported the tool to reuse the resampler, and main() ran at import. The entry point is guarded now — which tests/texture-resize.test.js depends on, since it imports resample and lowFrequency and would otherwise rewrite five catalog textures as a side effect of running the suite
  • The pipeline reports are a chain, and two characterization tests assumed P6 was the last link. texture-compression.json records what P6 left on disk; B4 legitimately touched four of those files, and both tests read that as P6 having lied. Neither report was edited — they are historical records, and rewriting one to match a later tree destroys what it is for. The tests now check the current bytes against the last pass that recorded touching each file, so the ratchet survives: everything still has to match some recorded pass
  • A browser test pinned 2048×1024 and bundled two claims into it — that P6's re-encode did not resample, and that 2:1 is the ratio the sky shader's UV mapping assumes. Only the second is a property of the software. It now asserts the ratio as the invariant and the dimensions separately, so a future resize has to come and say so
  • The pixel oracle is the only measurement here that looks at what a user sees, and it does not say "invisible". 25 rendered-frame comparisons, decomposed into harness noise, codec loss and resolution — self is 0.00 everywhere, which is what makes the rest trustworthy. Ground_4K (≤1.43 at every size) and white_wood (≤1.24) are free. Garden is free to 512px and reaches 8.30 at its native 2048, a rate the sky dome never samples at. The two dark woods carry a real 2.3–3.7 rms difference at realistic sizes, worst pixels 23–39/255. Small, measurable, and not nothing — it is what 52.56 MB of the 61.67 actually cost. Read the curve rather than the worst number: below the resized texture's own resolution the difference is mip-chain alignment, not missing information, and at 669×1024 the resized wood is at 1:1 and therefore sharper than the original minified to meet it
  • 1,250 headless tests (18 new) and 54 in the browser, all green. Eight deliberate breaks — sRGB-space resampling, no premultiply, edge-sampled kernel centres, unnormalised weights, a blinded gate, an oversized texture, a drifted byte, a tightened budget — each reverted individually and each failed. The evidence is committed: asset-pipeline/resize-report.json for the transform, resize-oracle.json for the frames. The 4.13 MB of pre-resize originals are not, since they live in git history and the permanent protection is the cap test
B5

KTX2 for the textures inside containers

doneL-42–3 daysafter B4

Objective. Transcode the model textures a .glb reaches through images[].uri, which no saved design names, and repoint every container at the result without re-encoding the geometry B1 proved.

Read RM-006 §30 beside this. The gate this sprint used weighs disk against video memory and never renders the texture. Nine of the eighteen encoded here turned out to be past the project's own codec gate, and eight of them were reverted to JPEG two programmes later. What LANDED below says is what was true when it was written; what it measured is not what it thought.

LANDED — B5
  • Texture VRAM 43.00 MB → 12.54 MB. The 18 textures inside .glb containers are KTX2/ETC1S. A JPEG becomes RGBA8 on the way to the GPU whatever it cost on disk; a KTX2 transcodes to a format the GPU reads directly and stays compressed at about 1 bit per pixel. Across B4 and B5 together the figure is 104.67 MB → 12.54 MB, and the deployed tree fell from 7.45 MB to 5.32
  • The objection that stopped this in B4 was wrong, and reading the source is what showed it. B4 recorded that KTX2Loader.load() throws without detectSupport(renderer) and that wiring it would put back a renderer coupling A4 removed. detectSupport does not retain the renderer: it makes seven extensions.has(...) calls and assigns a plain object of booleans to workerConfig, a public field. The dependency is on what formats this GPU supports — a property of the device, exactly as page-wide as the texture cache. core/texture_formats.js produces that record and nothing gained a renderer reference
  • A different blocker was real, and it set the sprint's boundary. TextureLoader.load() returns a Texture synchronously, which is what lets texture_cache hand out clones and fill in pixels later. KTX2Loader.load() returns undefined and delivers a CompressedTexture whose data lives in mipmaps rather than in the shared source the clone trick depends on. Supporting it means changing what acquireTexture promises and rewriting Floor, Edge and Skybox with it — a redesign of a module A0 and A4 hardened. 8.75 MB left on the table against 22.84 MB taken, costed rather than hand-waved
  • The containers were edited, not re-encoded. The obvious implementation reads each .glb with glTF-Transform and writes it back, which re-runs Draco over geometry B1 spent a sprint proving. repoint-textures.mjs parses the container, rewrites the JSON chunk and copies the BIN chunk through byte for byte, so B1's guarantee holds by construction; the BIN hash of all 20 is in texture-repoint.json so it is checkable rather than claimed
  • One encode per file, not one per document. @gltf-transform/functions ships a ktx2() transform and it is deliberately unused: 21 containers reference these textures and two are shared, so a per-document transform encodes the shared ones twice, cannot guarantee the two agree, and writes two copies of pixels that were one file
  • A break that was supposed to fail did not — for the fourth time in this programme, and it invalidated a conclusion I had already drawn. Removing setKTX2Loader from Scene left all 54 browser tests green, and I had just cited those green tests as proof that KTX2 rendering worked. It proved nothing: the Draco browser tests use ik-kivine_baked.glb, one of the four models whose texture is missing from the source library, so the suite had never decoded an image at all. Three new tests assert the container declares the extension, that the texture arrives as a CompressedTexture at 669×1024 with a mip chain, and that the frame changes when it lands. Both breaks fail now
  • Three limits up, three down, and two changed what they measure. decoder-total became "Codec machinery" and covers both vendored decoders, 264 KB → 881 KB for the 580 KB Basis transcoder. public-largest now excludes them: a 515 KB WebAssembly binary would own that line forever while saying nothing about what P6 added it to catch, and it points at a .glb again. texture-vram is deliberately left at 72% headroom — the 12.54 MB assumes every KTX2 reaches a 1 bpp GPU format, and a device with none falls back to RGBA8 and climbs back toward 43 MB. Tightening it would fail on the hardware it helps least
  • The pipeline reports are a chain, and B5 was the third pass to prove it. P6 recorded a tree, B4 replaced some of it, B5 replaced more — and each time a characterization test read the earlier record as a lie. No report was ever rewritten: they are records of what their own pass did. Six assertions across four files now resolve through the chain, and shippedName composes all three passes rather than listing one's outputs, so a fourth pass will not need it rewritten again
  • 1,251 headless tests and 57 in the browser (3 new), all green. Five pipeline --checks pass from a clean tree. Two deliberate breaks — the loader unwired, the capability record blanked — each failed once the coverage hole above was closed

Sequence and effort

SprintWorkEffortDepends onMetrics moved
B1 doneAsset precision and delivery1–2 weeks—M-10, M-11, M-12 — all met. KTX2 measured, not shipped — see LANDED
B2 doneWall identity across a load3–5 days—M-13 — met, 15 of 15. A3's carve-out lifted and a listener leak with it
B3 doneType coverage, application tier1 week—M-14 — met, 0. Tree 427 → 356; the opt-in is a test now — see LANDED
B4 doneTexture VRAM3–5 daysB1 (measurement)VRAM 104.67 → 43.00 MB. Five limits lowered, none raised. KTX2 declined on evidence — see LANDED
B5 doneKTX2 for container textures2–3 daysB4VRAM 43.00 → 12.54 MB. B4's architectural objection disproved; a real one found and scoped around — see LANDED

Three to four weeks end to end, and less if they overlap, since nothing here waits on anything else. That independence is the difference between this program and the last two: RM-003 had a critical path because each sprint installed something the next one needed, and this one has three unrelated jobs that happened to be left over. If only one thing gets done: B1 — it is the only one of the three a user can feel, and the seam it uses was built by a sprint that has already shipped.

RESIDUAL & TYPE TIER · DRAWING Nº RM-005

Residual Program

Two sprints against the only two things three programs agreed to leave. Both were costed by the sprint that deferred them, and measuring found both costings wrong in the same direction — each deferral was described by its blocker rather than by its contents, and in each case the blocker turns out to guard the smaller half. Five findings, all produced by running something, and one of them is a crash reproduced at a prompt.

PROJECT
architect3d
SUCCEEDS
RM-004 — all 5 sprints landed
