§ 25 · EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

Two deferrals, each costed by its blocker

B5 wrote down what the remaining texture memory would cost and called it a cache redesign. B3 wrote down what the remaining type errors would cost and called it 355. Both numbers are in the repository, both were honest when written, and both describe the hard part rather than the whole part.

68.6%
of the remaining texture VRAM is behind a module that never calls the texture cache. Skybox uses TextureLoader directly, twice
0.00 MB
the Texture VRAM budget moved when a 669×1024 KTX2 was dropped into public/. It counts .png, .jpg and .jpeg, and B5 converted 18 textures out of that set
356
what the type ledger's NOT YET rows sum to, under a heading that says 355. Fifth drift, and this one landed in the commit that added the test
1
crash reproduced from the checker's output. RoofItem.closestCeilingPoint() throws on any design with no rooms, and the constructor calls it

What measuring changed. The texture job was recorded as “make acquireTexture asynchronous and change Floor, Edge and Skybox with it” — in tools/encode-textures.mjs, in docs/architecture.md, and in the note handed to the last session. Skybox does not call acquireTexture. It never has. It holds its own TextureLoader, fetches two files by string literal, and owns 8.00 MB of the 11.67 MB that is worth compressing. The redesign that was blocking the sprint reaches the other 3.67 MB.

The type job has the mirror shape. 355 is the count the audit command prints, but checkJs is off, so npm run typecheck does not see a single one of them — they live in the 30 files under src/scripts with no // @ts-check pragma. The count is not the deliverable; the pragma is. And 11 of those 30 files have no errors at all: they are clean, unprotected, and free.

Architecture health metrics

Continuing RM-004's numbering. Every row is computable today by a command in the repository. One target is deliberately conditional on a spike and is marked as such.

NºMetricTodayTargetHow it is measuredSprint
M-15Uploaded textures the VRAM budget can count10 of 2828 of 28 metCompare the file list textureVram() walks against the manifest entries whose kind is model-texture, texture or environmentC1
M-16Texture VRAM, on the corrected measurement20.15 MB16.15 MB partnpm run budget after M-15. The target assumes the encoder's 1 bpp model and drops to 14.15 MB if the C1 spike says the cache cannot share a compressed upload — see N-2C1
M-17Textures fetched without passing through AssetResolver20 metgrep -n "rooms/textures/" src/scripts/three/*.js, minus the constants that are resolved at their point of useC1
M-18vue-tsc --checkJs errors outside the checked set3550 metnpx vue-tsc --noEmit --checkJs 2>&1 | grep -c "error TS"C2
M-19src/scripts files carrying // @ts-check25 of 5555 of 55 metfind src/scripts -name '*.js' | wc -l against the same list filtered by grep -l "@ts-check"C2

Why M-19 and not M-18 is the one that matters. B3 proved this the hard way: it removed a component's pragma, reintroduced the error the component used to have, and got a completely clean npm run typecheck. With checkJs off the pragma is the protection. A count that reaches zero and is not opted in has bought a snapshot; a file that carries the pragma has bought a gate.

§ 26 · DECISION LOG

Five findings, with the evidence

All five are marked measured: four were produced by running a command against the tree at 5bfb795, and the fifth by calling the method and catching what it threw. Three of them contradict something the repository currently says about itself, and two of those three were written by the program immediately preceding this one.

NºFindingSeverityWhereSprint
J-1Most of the texture memory left is not behind the cache that was blocking itmedthree/skybox.js:104, 116C1
J-2Two textures are fetched by string literal, so the manifest cannot reach themmedthree/skybox.js:45, 116C1
J-3The VRAM budget stopped counting the eighteen textures B5 compressedmedtools/check-budget.mjs:264C1
J-4The type ledger drifted a fifth time, in the commit that added the test against driftlowtsconfig.json:65–71 · tests/type-coverage.test.js:146C2
J-5The checker names a reachable crash, and it is not one of the pinned onesmeditems/roof_item.js:95C2

J-1 · The memory left is not behind the blocker med measured

Ten pixel files remain in public/ outside the thumbnail directories. Reading each one's header for its dimensions and applying the same four-bytes-per-texel-with-mips arithmetic the budget uses:

FilePixelsDiskVRAMReaches the GPU through
rooms/textures/Ground_4K.jpg1024×102457.7 KB5.333 MBSkybox — its own TextureLoader
rooms/textures/envs/Garden.jpg1024×512249.8 KB2.667 MBSkybox — its own TextureLoader
rooms/textures/hardwood.jpg512×512142.2 KB1.333 MBacquireTexture — the default floor
rooms/textures/marbletiles.jpg512×512162.3 KB1.333 MBacquireTexture
rooms/textures/light_brick.jpg256×25659.3 KB0.333 MBacquireTexture
rooms/textures/light_fine_wood.jpg256×25640.6 KB0.333 MBacquireTexture
rooms/textures/walllightmap.png256×25616.8 KB0.333 MBacquireTexture — the shared wall lightmap
rooms/textures/wallmap.png256×2560.7 KB0.333 MBacquireTexture — below the encoder's gate
rooms/textures/wallmap_yellow.png256×2560.7 KB0.333 MBacquireTexture — below the gate
models/js-glb/textures/ps_7.jpg200×2001.4 KB0.203 MBcontainer — below the gate
Total731.5 KB12.54 MB

The last three fail B5's gate — encode only if the file shrinks or at least 1 MB of VRAM comes back — and two of them are 0.7 KB PNGs that a KTX2 container would make larger. So 11.67 MB across seven files is what is actually available, and it divides in a way nothing in the repository currently says:

  • 8.00 MB — 68.6% — belongs to Skybox, which does not use the texture cache. skybox.js:104 holds new TextureLoader() as an instance field and skybox.js:116 constructs a second one inline. Neither goes near acquireTexture, so neither is affected by what acquireTexture promises
  • 3.67 MB — 31.4% — goes through the cache, and is the part the redesign is for

Better than that: one of Skybox's two paths is already the shape the redesign is trying to reach. setEnvironmentMap() builds its ShaderMaterial inside the load callback (skybox.js:305–317), so the material is constructed after the texture exists and could take a CompressedTexture today without a line changing in how it is structured. The ground path is the one that uses TextureLoader.load()'s synchronous return, and restructuring it is a local edit in one file — the material is built twenty lines below the load, from a local variable.

What the repository says, and why it said it. tools/encode-textures.mjs and docs/architecture.md both name “Floor, Edge and Skybox” as the three modules the redesign has to change. The first two are right. Skybox was almost certainly included because it is the third thing in three/ that draws with a texture, and because nobody grepped: the claim is one grep -n acquireTexture src/scripts/three/skybox.js away from being checked, and it returns nothing. The costing was not wrong about the cache — the cache blocker is real and B5 read it correctly out of KTX2Loader's return contract. It was wrong about how much of the prize sat behind it.

J-2 · Two textures the manifest cannot reach med measured

Skybox fetches both of its textures by string literal:

  • skybox.js:45 — this.defaultEnvironment = 'rooms/textures/envs/Garden.jpg', handed to setEnvironmentMap(), which calls this.texture.load(url, …) directly
  • skybox.js:116 — new TextureLoader().load('rooms/textures/Ground_4K.jpg', …), the URL written inline at the call

Every other texture in the viewer goes through this.runtime.assets.resolve(url).url first — Floor at floor.js:115, Edge at edge.js:94 and edge.js:297. That indirection is the whole of A5: a logical name in a document, a physical URL at runtime. It is also the mechanism the hardwood retirement used. rooms/textures/hardwood.png is not on disk any more and every design that names it still opens, because the manifest carries "url": "rooms/textures/hardwood.jpg" against the old name and Floor asks the resolver. Skybox does not ask, so for these two files the retirement mechanism does not exist.

This is why J-2 is a prerequisite for J-1's 8.00 MB rather than a tidy-up alongside it. Converting Ground_4K.jpg to Ground_4K.ktx2 is a retirement — it is the same operation, on the same table, that hardwood.png went through — and a retirement only reaches code that resolves. Doing the encode first would delete a file that skybox.js:116 still names, and the sky would render on a missing texture.

The fix is small and the seam is already there. Skybox's constructor is (scene, renderer, profile), and scene is the app's Scene, which holds this.runtime (model/scene.js:49) and already reaches this.runtime.assets twice for decoder paths. The resolver is one property access away from both call sites.

Why this has cost nothing so far. B4 downscaled every oversized texture in place — same filename, new contents — and the reason that worked at all was that a name which does not move needs no reference rewriting. Both of these files were rewritten by B4 and Skybox picked up the change without knowing there had been one. A bypass is invisible until the first change that moves a name, and C1 is that change.

J-3 · The budget stopped counting what B5 compressed med measured

textureVram() walks public/ and sums w × h for every file whose extension is in PIXEL, which is .png, .jpg and .jpeg (check-budget.mjs:264). B5 converted 18 textures to .ktx2. Those 18 now contribute zero.

Demonstrated rather than argued: copying public/models/js-glb/textures/oak_wood.ktx2 — a 669×1024 texture, 3.65 MB uncompressed — into public/rooms/textures/ and re-running the budget moves the line by 0.00 MB. It reports 12.54 MB before and 12.54 MB after.

So the figure in tools/budget.json is not what its own note says it is. The note reads:

What the note saysWhat the code does
“The number it reports, 12.54 MB, assumes every KTX2 transcodes to a 1 bpp GPU format” Skips .ktx2 entirely. At 1 bpp the 18 files are 7.61 MB, so the number the note describes would be 20.15 MB

Both halves of the note's reasoning survive this; only the arithmetic is wrong. The headroom really is there to cover a device with no compressed format at all, where Basis falls back to RGBA8 and the tree costs 43.00 MB — and the limit, 45.15 MB, is still exactly 1.05× that fallback. Nothing about the ceiling needs to change. What needs to change is that the line cannot currently see a regression in the format it exists to encourage: someone adding a 4K KTX2 environment map would move this budget by nothing at all.

The correction makes the number go up, and that is the precedent. B4 hit this same line and corrected it rather than moving it — B1 had counted 202 images at 164 MB when 174 of them were DOM thumbnails no GPU is asked for. The honest figure was smaller and the limit came down with it. This time the honest figure is larger, 12.54 → 20.15 MB, and the limit stays where it is. A budget that improves because the measurement stopped looking is the one failure mode this program's method exists to catch, and it has now happened twice in the same file.

J-4 · A fifth drift, in the commit that added the test low measured

The ledger in tsconfig.json has been wrong four times, and the fourth — B5 adding core/texture_formats.js without moving either count that touched it — is why tests/type-coverage.test.js now parses the numbers instead of trusting them. Recounting at 5bfb795:

AreaLedgerTreeNote
src/scripts/three148148108 in main.js, 22 in controller.js
src/scripts/model11111142 in floorplan.js, 20 in corner.js, 18 in room.js
src/scripts/items555531 in item.js, 13 in wall_item.js
src/scripts/floorplanner414118 in floorplanner_view.js, 15 in floorplanner.js
src/app/main.js10Stale. The row the same commit's own prose says it removed
Stated total355355Correct — and its own rows sum to 356

The prose four lines above the table says it plainly: “The 356 became 355 when RM-004's follow-on gave the checker vite/client, so src/app/main.js stopped reporting TS2882.” The heading was updated to 355 and the row that went to zero was left in place. git log -S puts all three changes — the new heading, the new test, the surviving row — in b596fd8, one commit.

The test does not catch it because the test does not look. It parses CHECKED - (\d+) files, verifies four named directories against the tree, and sums the itemisation with itemised.slice(0, 7). The CHECKED block has exactly seven numbered rows, so that slice isolates it correctly — positionally. The five NOT YET rows are never read. Two consequences, and the second is the one that will bite:

  • The NOT YET itemisation can say anything, because nothing compares it to anything. Its total is asserted as a ceiling elsewhere, deliberately, so that improving the tree cannot fail the build — but a ceiling is satisfied by 356 as readily as by 355
  • slice(0, 7) is a constant that has to change whenever a CHECKED row is added. Add one and the sum silently omits it while picking up the first NOT YET row instead. The test would still pass roughly as often as not, which is worse than failing

One smaller inaccuracy in the same note, recorded rather than fixed: it states that grep -rn "@ts-ignore\|@ts-expect-error" src “returns 7 and all 7 are P2's deliberate ones”. The grep does return 7, but only 4 are directives — the other three are the docblock explaining them. The test has this right and asserts ≤ 4; only the prose is loose.

J-5 · A reachable crash the checker already names med measured

Two of the 355 sit on one line, items/roof_item.js:95: 'result' is possibly 'null' and 'result.closestPoint' is possibly 'null'. Reading the method around them:

StepWhat happens
roof_item.js:80var result = null;
roof_item.js:81for (var i=0; i< roofs.length; i++) — the only place result is assigned
roof_item.js:93if (globalResult.point == null) — true when the loop never ran
roof_item.js:95return result.closestPoint.clone();

roofs is this.model.floorplan.roofPlanes(), which pushes one plane per room. A design with no rooms gives an empty array, the loop body never executes, and result is still null at line 95. Called with a stub floorplan returning []:

TypeError: Cannot read properties of null (reading 'closestPoint')

RoofItem's constructor calls this — roof_item.js:24, var co = this.closestCeilingPoint(); — so the crash is not in a corner of the API. It is on the path that adds a ceiling item, and the condition is “the floorplan has no rooms”, which is the state every design starts in.

It is worth being precise about what makes this different from the four arity bugs P2 found in core/utils.js. Those are preserved: each is pinned with @ts-expect-error, each carries a docblock naming what it breaks and why the library depends on the broken behaviour, and each has a characterization test. They are decisions. This one is pinned by nothing, documented nowhere, and lives in a file with no pragma — so npm run typecheck is silent about it, and the only reason it is visible at all is that somebody ran the audit command and read the output.

The shape of the other 353

Bucketed by code, across 19 files and 16 distinct codes:

ClusterCountWhat it is, and what closes it
TS2339 — property does not exist169Fields assigned outside a constructor and never declared. Concentrated: 32 on OrbitControls, 26 on Item, 12 each on Main and Element, 11 on Floorplan. A declaration each
Strict-null family — TS2531/18046/1804775The family that found real defects in P2 and has found J-5 here. Each needs reading, not annotating
TS2304 — cannot find name43JSDoc naming Wall (10), Floorplan (5), Room (4), Corner (4) and eleven others with nothing importing the type. An import('…') in the comment
TS2322 / TS2769 / TS2345 — assignability42Mixed. Some are genuine widening, some are three's overloads being stricter than r98's
TS2554 — wrong argument count13The same family as P2's preserved bugs. Each is a fix-or-pin decision, and pinning raises an asserted number
TS2551 and eight singletons13Misspelled property, two TS7014, and one each of six others

Two of these clusters are the same fix applied twice. 43 errors are about OrbitControls and PointerLockControls, which since S5 are not vendored copies at all — they are 97-line and 204-line wrappers over three/addons that add the app's own fields (needsUpdate, the walk-through physics) and declare none of them. 16 of the 43 are inside the wrappers; the other 27 are elsewhere, mostly main.js, touching properties the wrapper owns. Declaring the added fields in one place closes all 43.

§ 27 · RISKS & DEPENDENCIES

What could go wrong

C1 and C2 are independent of each other. C1 is not internally independent — it has a strict order, and getting it wrong deletes a file something still names by literal. That ordering is the largest risk in this program.

NºRiskImpactLikelihoodMitigation
N-1A texture is retired before its literal is removed. Encoding Ground_4K.jpg deletes it; skybox.js:116 still names it; the sky renders on a 404. This is J-2 fired rather than avoidedhighmedOrdering, and a test rather than a habit. J-2's fix is task 2 of C1 and the encode is task 3. A test greps src/scripts for rooms/textures/ string literals and asserts every match is resolved at its point of use — so a future bypass fails the suite instead of waiting for the next retirement to expose it
N-2The spike says clones cannot share a compressed upload, and the refcount stops buying anything for these seven textures. Texture.clone() shares .source, which is what makes one decode serve many walls; a CompressedTexture keeps its data in .mipmaps and it is an open question whether two clones are one upload or twomedmedThe spike is task 4 and its output is a number, not an opinion: read WebGLTextures.uploadTexture for how a compressed texture is keyed, then count GPU allocations for two clones in the browser tier. If the answer is no, C1 stops after task 3 having banked 8.00 MB of the 11.67 and the remaining 3.67 is re-costed against what per-surface uploads would actually weigh. M-16 carries both numbers for that reason
N-3The async contract is a public API break. acquireTexture, releaseTexture, clearTextureCache and textureCacheStats are re-exported from blueprint.js:114. An embedder can hold a handlehighlowKeep the synchronous return. Whatever the internals become, acquireTexture(url, onLoad) must still hand back an object at call time and still accept the same two arguments — which is what keeps this a minor version rather than a major one. If the spike says that is impossible, the signature change goes in its own release with the break written down, and it is a decision to bring back here rather than to take inside a sprint
N-4A frame is drawn before the texture arrives, and the first frame of a design shows an untextured floor where it used to show a textured one. The synchronous handle exists precisely so that never happensmedmedThe pixel tier is the oracle and it already exists — P5's browser tests read whole frames, B4 added 25 rendered-frame measurements. C1 adds a first-frame case per changed surface. B5's own break-the-gate found the suite had never decoded a room texture at all, so this is coverage the program needs regardless of the outcome
N-5The corrected VRAM figure is read as a regression. 12.54 → 20.15 MB, in a program whose stated purpose is to lower itlowmedIt goes up in the same commit that explains why, the limit does not move, and B4's correction of this exact line is the precedent to cite. The CHANGELOG states the before-and-after of the measurement separately from the before-and-after of the tree
N-6A type annotation changes behaviour, or a cast hides the defect the checker found. L-5 restated, and J-5 is the evidence that the defects are realmedmedP2's rule unchanged: fix or annotate, never cast away. The mechanism is already installed — tests/type-coverage.test.js asserts the suppression count at ≤ 4 and asserts the only file allowed to hold one. Any new pin fails that test until somebody raises the number deliberately and says why in the commit
N-7355 small edits across 19 files is a review nobody can do. The failure mode is not a bad change, it is a change nobody readmedhighCommit per cluster, not per file, and land the clusters in the order §28 gives — each is one git revert from gone. The pragma goes on a file only once that file is at zero, so a half-finished cluster cannot land silently claiming protection it does not have

Sequencing

Between the sprints: none. C1 is public/, three/ and the budget tool; C2 is annotations and tsconfig.json. They can run in either order or together.

Inside C1 the order is load-bearing and it is: correct the measurement, remove the literals, take the 8.00 MB, then spike the cache. Every step earns something on its own and every step is revertible without the next one, which is the property that lets C1 stop after task 3 if N-2 comes back badly.

There is one small overlap worth taking deliberately. edge.js, floor.js and skybox.js are three of the eleven files that already have zero errors and no pragma. C1 touches all three. Adding // @ts-check to them as it goes is C2's cheapest task done early, on exactly the files where C1 most wants a checker watching.

If only one thing gets done: C1, and specifically C1 through task 3 — it is the only work here a user can feel, and 8.00 of the 11.67 MB needs no redesign of anything. If only one thing gets done and the tree has to be safer afterwards: C2's first two tasks, which are the ledger and J-5.

§ 28 · IMPLEMENTATION

Two sprints

C1 is ordered internally and can stop after any task with something banked. C2 is unordered after its first two and is the one to cut if time runs short, because by design it changes no behaviour except where the checker names a defect.

C1

The last of the texture memory

doneJ-1J-2J-31 weekno dependencies

Objective. Take the 11.67 MB that B5 left, cheapest two-thirds first, and fix the measurement before changing the thing it measures. Put Skybox behind the resolver on the way, because the encode is a retirement and a retirement only reaches code that resolves.

ScopeExcluded
A VRAM budget that counts KTX2 · Skybox behind AssetResolver · KTX2 for its two textures · a spike on compressed-texture sharing · the cache contract if the spike says yes · retirement entries for every name that moves · a first-frame browser test per changed surface The three files below the encoder's gate, 0.87 MB · re-encoding anything already KTX2 · a texture eviction policy · the RGBA8-fallback headroom in the budget limit · any change to acquireTexture's signature — see N-3

Tasks

  1. Fix the measurement first, and let the number go up. textureVram() reads .png, .jpg and .jpeg; teach it .ktx2, counting at 1 bpp with the same 4/3 mip factor the rest of the function uses. ktx2Dimensions() already exists in tools/encode-textures.mjs and reads the header. Reported VRAM goes 12.54 → 20.15 MB, the limit does not move, and M-15 goes from 10 of 28 to 28 of 28. Doing this after the encode would let the sprint claim a saving the tool could not see
  2. Remove the two literals. skybox.js:45 and skybox.js:116 through this.scene.runtime.assets.resolve(url).url, the same call Floor and Edge already make. Add the test N-1 describes: every rooms/textures/ literal in src/scripts is resolved at its point of use. That test is what makes this a fixed bypass rather than a fixed instance
  3. Take the 8.00 MB that needs no redesign. setEnvironmentMap() already builds its material inside the load callback and can hold a CompressedTexture as it stands; the ground path needs its material construction moved into the callback, which is a local edit in one file. Neither touches texture_cache. Encode both, retire both names the way hardwood.png was retired, and the sprint has banked 69% of the prize
  4. Spike the cache before redesigning it. One question, and it decides the rest: does CompressedTexture.clone() share one GPU upload the way Texture.clone() shares .source? Read WebGLTextures.uploadTexture for how a compressed texture is keyed, then count allocations for two clones in the browser tier. A yes means only the load path changes and the refcount survives intact. A no means the refcount buys nothing here, and the honest answer may be per-surface uploads — which is a number to produce, not a conclusion to assume
  5. Then the contract, if the spike earned it. The constraint from N-3 is hard: acquireTexture(url, onLoad) keeps its signature and keeps returning an object at call time. The manifest already records codec per entry, so the cache can know a URL is KTX2 before its first load lands and decide synchronously which kind of object to hand back — which is the seam that makes a same-signature change possible at all
  6. Retirement entries for every name that moves, and the RETIRED table's existing guard covers the rest: it throws if a target is missing or if a name is both live and retired. Seven names at most, five if the spike says stop
  7. A browser test that decodes a room texture. B5's break-the-gate found that removing setKTX2Loader left all 54 browser tests green, because the one model the suite loads has no texture in the library. The room textures are the surfaces a user actually looks at and the suite has never decoded one
RATIONALE · ALTERNATIVES · COMPATIBILITY
  • Why the order is the design. Every prior texture sprint could be reverted as a unit. This one cannot: task 3 deletes files that task 2 stops something from naming. Sequencing it as measure → unblock → earn → spike means each step is independently valuable and independently revertible, and the sprint can stop after any of them with a real number banked
  • Alternative rejected — do the cache redesign first, as B5 scoped it. That is the 31% half and the risky half, and it puts the sprint's hardest work in front of its cheapest. It is also the order that produces nothing at all if the spike comes back badly
  • Alternative rejected — leave Skybox on its own loader and just encode. Tempting, because Skybox's loader works. It also means two textures that no manifest can redirect, which is the property J-2 is about; the next retirement would find them the same way this one did, only in production
  • Alternative rejected — lower the VRAM limit to sit 5% over the new number. The headroom is the RGBA8 fallback and it is documented as such. Tightening it makes the budget fail on precisely the hardware compressed textures cannot help — and a ratchet that fires on the weakest device is a ratchet nobody will keep
  • Compatibility. Every retired name keeps resolving, which is the hardwood shape and is asserted by tests/asset-integrity.test.js. acquireTexture's signature and synchronous return are unchanged, so no embedder breaks. Designs saved before this sprint open after it
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • M-15 = 28 of 28, proved the way J-3 was: drop a KTX2 into public/, and the budget moves
  • M-16 = 11.40 MB, or 14.15 MB with the spike's refusal recorded next to it. Either number is acceptable; an unexplained one is not
  • M-17 = 0, and it is a test, not a grep somebody remembered to run
  • The pixel tier is unchanged for every surface touched, first frame included. A texture that cannot match ships uncompressed, per-asset rather than all-or-nothing — B1's rule, and it applied then too
  • The VRAM budget limit is untouched, and the CHANGELOG says why a line in a program about lowering VRAM went up
  • Rollback. Per task. Task 3 reverts by restoring two files and two manifest rows; task 5 reverts to the current cache wholesale
  • DoD. Three tiers green · five pipeline --checks green · ten budgets green · docs/architecture.md corrected on both counts — the Skybox claim and the 8.75 MB — · CHANGELOG
LANDED — C1
  • 4.00 MB banked against a projected 8.75, and the shortfall is the sprint's finding rather than its failure. Every one of the seven candidate textures was rendered against its own transcode and differenced in the framebuffer — a thing no previous texture sprint did — and five of the seven refuse. B5's 8.75 MB was arithmetic on file sizes, and it assumed a codec nobody had looked through.
  • The measurement was wrong before anything was encoded, and correcting it moved the number the wrong way first. textureVram() read .png, .jpg and .jpeg; B5 had turned 18 textures into .ktx2 and the extension list did not follow. The line fell 43.00 → 12.54 MB after B5 and roughly a quarter of that fall was the measurement letting go. Proved by dropping a 669×1024 KTX2 into public/ and watching the budget move by 0.00 MB. Corrected to 20.15 MB with the limit untouched — the second correction to this line, and the two went opposite ways.
  • J-1 held, and it was the whole of the win. Skybox owns 8.00 MB of the 11.67 and has never called acquireTexture. Its ground photograph is a KTX2 now, 5.33 → 1.33 MB, and the sprint's entire saving is that one file. Its environment map was measured and refused: RMS 4.483 against B4's 3.0 gate with 4.08% of channels off by more than 8 — banding in a sky gradient, on the surface a first-person viewer looks at longest.
  • Then the second half found something better than the memory it was after. N-2's spike came back YES: two clones of a compressed texture differing in repeat cost one _gl.createTexture(), measured, with a break proving the counter can see a second. So the cache change was built — an empty CompressedTexture handed out and cloned as before, payload adopted into the master and every live clone on arrival, signature and synchronous return intact. It worked.
  • And then all five textures behind it refused, so it was reverted. 7.441, 5.532, 10.171 and 4.145 RMS against a gate of 3.0, two of them past double. A quality sweep first, because B5 chose qualityLevel 128 out of 255: at maximum, hardwood is still 1.8× the gate and light_brick 2.8×, for nearly twice the file. The cache was never the binding constraint — the content was, and these textures would have refused ETC1S with any cache at all. Machinery with no consumer is a liability, so it went.
  • The fifth texture is the one worth remembering. walllightmap.png passes on absolute error — RMS 1.269, nothing off by more than 8 — and it was nearly taken. It is a hand-painted vignette spanning bytes 232 to 253, so its entire dynamic range is 21 levels: 1.269 is 6.0% of that range and its worst pixel 23.8%. Against its own content it is the most damaged of the five, where hardwood's 7.441 is 2.9% of a full-range image. An absolute gate is the wrong instrument for a low-contrast texture, and nothing in the tree would have said so.
  • J-2 was a prerequisite and behaved like one. The encode is a retirement and a retirement only reaches code that resolves, so the two literals were removed first. Three tests hold it: no loader takes a bare asset path, every fetch argument is resolved at the call or bound from a resolve, and the tree's five texture literals are enumerated so a sixth is a decision.
  • Two of those tests failed to fail on the first attempt, and both were the same mistake. One asked "does this file mention resolveAsset anywhere?" — satisfied by the method's own DECLARATION, so removing both call sites left it green. The other asserted a KTX2 costs less than a JPEG, and a texture the budget cannot see costs zero, which is less than everything. A check on the existence of a tool rather than on its use, twice in one sprint.
  • The real browser found what nothing else could. KTX2Loader.load() THROWS on a missing workerConfig rather than failing soft, and jsdom has no WebGL to produce one — the one place B4's architectural objection was pointing at something real, and it lands in Skybox rather than Scene because Scene only attaches its loader. Four browser tests now prove the transcode: container magic on the wire, a compressed format reported by the device, isCompressedTexture with an 11-level mip chain, and the material recompiled.
  • Raised and not settled. B5 encoded 18 model textures gating only on disk and VRAM and never rendered one. If ETC1S is destructive enough on wood grain at 512×512 to fail at 7.4 RMS, some of those 18 are likely degraded and nobody has looked. The sources are in git history rather than in the tree, so the measurement is recoverable and is not free.
  • 1,259 headless tests in 30 files and 62 in the browser (5 new), five pipeline --checks green, ten budgets green, lint and typecheck clean. Texture VRAM 20.15 → 16.15 MB on the corrected measurement, Runtime assets 5.32 → 5.28 MB, Deployed tree 12.48 → 12.44 MB. No limit moved: 40 KB of headroom is not a ratchet, and the VRAM ceiling still guards the RGBA8 fallback.
C2

Type coverage: the library tier

doneJ-4J-52 weeksno dependencies

Objective. Take the 355 to zero and put the pragma on all 55 files in src/scripts, so that npm run typecheck — not just the audit command — is what fails on a regression. Fix the defects the checker names on the way, starting with the one it has already named.

ScopeExcluded
355 errors across 19 files · // @ts-check on all 55 · the NOT YET ledger asserted the way CHECKED is · slice(0, 7) replaced with something that cannot silently mis-sum · J-5 and the 13 arity mismatches strict: true · converting anything to .ts · new suppressions without raising an asserted number · behavioural change beyond the defects the checker names · the four preserved bugs in core/utils.js, which are decisions and stay

Tasks

  1. The ledger first, because it is what will be wrong again otherwise. Delete the stale src/app/main.js row, assert the NOT YET itemisation against the tree the way CHECKED already is, and replace itemised.slice(0, 7) — a positional constant that mis-sums the moment somebody adds a CHECKED row — with a split on the two headings. This is the fifth drift; the fix is not another rule, it is the assertion the fourth drift was supposed to install and reached only half of
  2. J-5, and the 13 arity mismatches with it. The crash first, with a test that constructs the empty-floorplan case, because RoofItem's constructor is on the path that adds a ceiling item. Then each TS2554: fix, or pin with @ts-expect-error, a docblock saying what depends on the broken behaviour, and a characterization test — P2's shape exactly. Pinning raises the asserted suppression count, which is the point of asserting it
  3. The 11 free files. items/factory.js, items/metadata.js, four items/ subclasses, and three/edge.js, floor.js, floorPlan.js, lights.js, skybox.js have zero errors and no pragma. They are clean and unprotected. Adding the pragma costs one line each and proves the gate works before any real edit lands — and three of them are the files C1 is editing
  4. 43 errors, one declaration site. Everything about OrbitControls and PointerLockControls is a field the app's own wrapper adds and never declares. Declare them on the wrappers; 16 errors inside those files and 27 elsewhere close together
  5. 43 TS2304, one mechanism. JSDoc naming Wall, Floorplan, Room, Corner and eleven other types with nothing importing them. An import('…') in the comment, and the types come from where they are defined rather than being restated — B3's finding, which put its typedefs next to the data and not next to the consumer
  6. 169 TS2339, by receiver rather than by file. 12 receiver types account for 146 of them. A field declared once closes every site that touches it, so the unit of work is the class, and the unit of commit is the receiver
  7. The strict-null family last, and read every one. 75 errors, and it is the family that found a defect in P2, another in B3, and J-5 here. This is the part that cannot be done quickly, which is why it goes after everything that can
  8. The pragma per file, as each reaches zero. Never before. A file carrying // @ts-check with errors in it fails the build for the next person, and a file at zero without one is protecting nothing
RATIONALE · ALTERNATIVES · COMPATIBILITY
  • Why now, when B3 said main.js was a sprint of its own. It still is — 108 errors, a quarter of the surface, in the most load-bearing file in the render path. What has changed is that the clusters are measured now: 43 of the 355 close at two declaration sites and 43 more at a comment mechanism, so the sprint is not 355 judgements. main.js's 108 are 45 TS2339 and 50 strict-null, which is the same two clusters and no new kind of problem
  • Alternative rejected — checkJs: true globally and suppress the remainder. B3's words, and they still hold: that converts 355 findings into 355 suppressions and calls it progress
  • Alternative rejected — highest count first. main.js is the largest number and the worst place to learn the harness. The 11 free files and the two wrapper declarations are the same technique against no risk at all
  • Alternative rejected — leave the library and raise the app's bar instead. src/app is at zero and fully opted in. There is no bar left to raise there, and the 355 are in the half an embedder actually links against
  • Compatibility. Annotations and declarations, plus the defects the checker names. J-5 changes behaviour on purpose: a method that throws stops throwing. That is the only intended behavioural change in the sprint and it gets its own commit
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • M-18 = 0 and M-19 = 55 of 55. The second is the one that holds; the first is a snapshot without it
  • The ledger's NOT YET block is asserted against the tree, and a break confirms it: add a file with an error, watch the test fail
  • Suppressions still ≤ 4, or higher with a commit that argues for each addition. The test is the mechanism and it is already installed
  • J-5 has a test that fails before the fix, constructed rather than found, since no fixture has a design with no rooms and a ceiling item
  • Coverage does not fall. Nothing here is meant to change behaviour, so the suite passing unchanged is the evidence — B3's standard, restated
  • Rollback. Per cluster. Each commit is one receiver type or one mechanism, and reverting one leaves the rest standing
  • DoD. Three tiers green · tsconfig.json ledger recounted, both blocks asserted · suppression count reported · CHANGELOG
LANDED — C2
  • 355 to zero, and 55 of 55 files carry the pragma. npm run typecheck covers every file in src/scripts now, so a regression fails the build rather than an audit command somebody remembers to run. M-18 and M-19 both met — and M-19 is the one that holds, which is the whole argument §25 made.
  • THREE HAD NO TYPE DECLARATIONS AT ALL, which reframes every number this program started with. three ships no .d.ts and @types/three was not installed, so import {Vector3} from 'three' resolved to any — and with it every three type this codebase names in JSDoc. A five-line probe settles it: new Vector3().notAThing type-checked without complaint. The check had been theatre for the half of this project that is three code, since P2 turned it on.
  • It also explains the 43 the plan blamed on the control wrappers. Properties were “missing” from OrbitControls because extending a value of type any produces a class with no inherited members. Not one was a real defect. Hand-declaring them, which is what §28 said to do, would have written down a copy of three's API and called it progress.
  • The count went UP twice on the way to zero — 342 → 267 when three got real types, then 267 → 300 when the project's own JSDoc names started resolving. Both rises say the same thing: 355 was the number visible through any, and a Wall that is actually a Wall has properties worth getting wrong.
  • Six defects, none of which anyone had hit. Two crashes of the same shape — RoofItem.closestCeilingPoint() on a design with no rooms (J-5) and WallItem.placeInRoom() on one with no walls — both called from a constructor, both reachable from the state every design starts in, and neither with a fixture empty enough to catch it. Corner.mergeWithIntersected's only parameter was passed as a fourth argument to a three-parameter function and discarded, so splitting a wall across N intersections ran N+1 full rebuilds. distanceFromWall had an implicit fallthrough returning undefined, which every caller compares against a tolerance. findRooms read a var declared inside the loop that assigns it, keying its dedup table under the string “undefined” for a room with no corners. And resetClipping aliased a frozen shared constant onto a mutable renderer field.
  • Three methods nobody could call, and two of them would have thrown. Controller.selectedObject() and Main.options() each share a name with a field the constructor sets, and an own property shadows a prototype method — so both were unreachable, and each typed its own field as a function for the whole file. Eleven of controller's nineteen errors were that one method. Main.rotatePressed() and rotateReleased() call methods Controller does not have, and never has.
  • Seven JSDoc tags were wrong about the code, which is worse than missing, because a wrong tag makes correct code look broken and sends the reader to the wrong end. getCenter() documented as returning Vector2 returns a Vector3 in all three branches. halfAngleVector documented two Vector2 parameters with the same name and takes two HalfEdges. transformCorner says Corner and is never given one. corners() says Corner[] where its own description says “x,y pairs”. And a family of malformed tags — the type on one line, the name on the next — typed loadFloorplan's parameter as the global JSON namespace and made three optional tolerances look required.
  • The remaining 67 in main.js were one shape: sixteen fields initialised to null, set by init(), cleared by dispose(). Every guard says the same thing — a viewer not yet built has nothing to do, and a torn-down one must not resurrect anything — and where a method returns a value the guard returns the empty one.
  • Two mechanisms that were prose became mechanisms. The ledger drifted a FIFTH time in b596fd8, the very commit that added the test against drift: it wrote NOT YET - 355, said in its own prose that src/app/main.js had gone to zero, and left that row in the itemisation. And the ceiling the note had claimed since B3 was asserted by nothing — removing @types/three took the tree to 342 and every test stayed green. npm run ledger:check is a tier-1 command now, in the shape of the four --check tools already in tools/.
  • The ledger caught me four times during the sprint, which is the first time in its history it has caught anybody: twice on a CHECKED total after adding pragmas, once on an itemisation that summed to one more than the tree had, and once on a NOT YET block I had recounted wrongly. Each was a mistake that would have shipped in any previous program.
  • Zero suppressions added. The count is still 4, all of them P2's preserved-bug pins in core/utils.js. Nothing was cast away that could be fixed, and the two places a cast was the honest answer — three's private _interceptControlDown, and this project's own events, which three's event map cannot know — each name exactly the one member they widen.
  • 1,265 headless tests in 30 files and 62 in the browser, lint and typecheck clean, five pipeline --checks green, ten budgets green, ledger:check at 0 of 0. One new devDependency, @types/three, which ships nothing.

What is different about this program is that it is the first with nothing named as deferred at the end of it — RM-002 left the type ledger, RM-003 left the payload, RM-004 left these two. If both sprints land, the backlog that has been handed forward since P2 is empty, and the next program will have to be about something somebody wants rather than about something somebody left.

CORRECTION PROGRAM · DRAWING Nº RM-006

The encode nobody looked at

RM-005 C1 ended by raising one thing and not settling it. Settling it took one sprint, found that nine of the eighteen textures B5 compressed are past the project's own codec gate, and found on the way that the instrument used to reach that verdict had been measuring the wrong picture for two programs. Both halves are corrections, and neither was available by reading anything.

PROJECT
architect3d
SUCCEEDS
RM-005 — both sprints landed
BRANCH
amit @ 3d21b81
SCOPE
21 GPU textures · 1 instrument · 3 budgets
DATE
2026-08-15
REV
A · delivered
§ 29 · EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

One deferral, and the instrument that would have prevented it

B5 encoded 18 model textures against a gate that weighs disk against video memory and never looks at the picture, and said so in its own docblock: the scope was “18 furniture textures seen at a few hundred pixels”. That is a claim about how something looks, made without rendering it. C1 refused four of five room textures on looks alone and wrote the question down. This answers it.

9 of 18
of B5's model textures are past the 3.0 RMS codec gate this project already had. Four past 4.5, and one at more than double
3×
the pixel oracle had been written as a throwaway script and deleted, before this. That is the direct reason B5's encode was never checked — the thing that looks at the picture did not exist anywhere a sprint could reach
7.441
the figure C1 published for hardwood.jpg, which the new harness could not reproduce — until outputColorSpace was set to Linear-sRGB, when it returns 7.441, max 66 and 19.88% over 8, to three decimals
0.000
the residual: a rendered source against its own decoded pixels. Every run now fails unless the render path is transparent, which is the check the previous three harnesses did not have

What was actually wrong, in two sentences. The earlier oracle differenced linear-light values quantised to eight bits, while the application renders in sRGB (three/main.js:262, asserted by two suites) — so it reported the error in a frame nobody looks at. The bias is not one-directional: it overstates mid-tones and understates highlights, and this repository holds one of each, so Ground_4K was too harsh at 1.098 (really 0.761) and Garden, a photograph of sky, was too kind at 4.483 (really 6.552).

What this program is not. It is not a new gate, a new threshold or a new policy. The gate is B4's codecRms of 3.0, unchanged; the rule applied is B1's — an asset that cannot match the pixel tier ships uncompressed — applied per file for the third time. Nothing is a judgement call: every refusal below carries the number that produced it, and every setting the encoder has was swept before any of it was reverted.

§ 30 · DECISION LOG

Five findings, all of them measured

Every row was produced by running something against the tree at 3d21b81. Four contradict something the repository currently says about itself, and three of those four were written by the two programs immediately preceding this one — which is the pattern, not a coincidence: a measurement nobody can re-run becomes a quotation, and a quotation is not checked again.

NºFindingSeverityWhereSprint
P-1Nine of B5's eighteen model textures are past the 3.0 codec gatehighpublic/models/js-glb/textures/D1
P-2The oracle that produced C1's verdicts differenced a frame the application never rendershighasset-pipeline/room-transcode-oracle.json · skybox-transcode-oracle.jsonD1
P-3The repointer only goes one way, so a refusal cannot be undonemedtools/repoint-textures.mjs:126D1
P-4Three size budgets were lowered on the strength of that encodemedtools/budget.json @ e6541a8D1
P-5The encoder's docblock is wrong about VRAM by a factor of eightlowtools/encode-textures.mjs:13D1

P-1 · Nine of eighteen high measured

Each texture rendered from the source it was encoded from and from its shipped container, through identical geometry, camera, sampler and colour-space state at 1:1, and differenced in the framebuffer. RMS is over RGB in 0–255; the gate is 3.0.

TexturePixelsRMSp95p99max>8Verdict
nyc2.jpg420×6007.0049331745.10%refused — 2.3× the gate
walnut-marin.jpg587×4664.8801014338.45%refused
oak_wood.jpg669×10244.8791014408.15%refused
grey-brown_wood.jpg669×10244.523913296.32%refused
ik-ekero-orange_baked.jpg512×5123.650614902.73%refused
we-narrow6white_baked.jpg512×5123.488715503.86%refused
bd-shalebedside-smoke_baked.jpg512×5123.456711852.29%refused
cb-archnight-white_baked.png512×5123.392714723.75%re-encoded at quality 192
cb-clapboard_baked.jpg512×5123.214614532.72%refused
The other nine measure 1.090 to 2.770 and are inside the gate. They stay compressed.

The sources are provably the right ones. They were recovered from e6541a8~1 — the commit before B5 deleted them — and one was re-encoded with the shipped settings and hashed: it reproduces the committed container byte for byte. So this is a measurement of what shipped, not of something like it.

Every setting was swept before anything was reverted, because a gate failure at one setting is not a codec verdict — C1 made that point about the room textures and it holds here. ETC1S at maximum quality rescues exactly one: cb-archnight-white_baked.png falls to 2.728 at quality 192, and at 55 KB it also replaces a 187 KB PNG, so the better-looking option is the smaller one. It gets a per-file entry rather than a global quality change, which would have cost the other seventeen 30–50% more disk to fix one. UASTC clears the gate for all eight of the rest, at 163–456% of source — for every one of them, shipping the source is smaller than shipping UASTC. The four settings tried per texture are recorded in settingsSwept, which also settles a C1 estimate: Garden under UASTC was quoted at “roughly 1.4 MB” from a catalog-wide ratio and measures 629 KB.

P-2 · The oracle was measuring the wrong picture high measured

The new harness was built to be calibrated: before trusting it against the 18, it was pointed at the five room textures C1 t5 published, and it reproduced none of them. Every figure came out low, and not by a constant factor.

TextureC1 publishedCorrectedWasNow
light_brick.jpg10.1717.379refusedrefused
hardwood.jpg7.4416.147refusedrefused
marbletiles.jpg5.5324.120refusedrefused
light_fine_wood.jpg4.1452.639refusedinside the gate
walllightmap.png1.2690.592passespasses
Ground_4K.jpg1.0980.761transcodedtranscoded
envs/Garden.jpg4.4836.552refusedrefused, harder

A sweep over the plausible causes found it exactly. With outputColorSpace set to Linear-sRGB instead of sRGB, the harness returns hardwood.jpg at 7.441, max 66, 19.88% of channels over 8 — the published row to three decimals. The earlier oracle wrote linear-light values into an eight-bit framebuffer and differenced those.

The last row is why this matters more than a rounding correction. Six of the seven moved down and one moved up, because an sRGB/linear confusion is not a scale factor: it stretches the shadows and crushes the highlights. Garden is nearly all highlight, so the harness that was too harsh on everything else was too kind to the one texture whose refusal C1 had to argue for. And light_fine_wood.jpg changes verdict: the pixel reason to refuse it has gone, and it stays uncompressed only for the architectural reason C1 recorded separately — texture_cache still cannot hold a CompressedTexture.

What now prevents it is not care. Every run renders the source and differences it against the source's own decoded pixels first. A transparent path returns zero and does: 0.000 across the calibration set, 0.014 worst case across the tree. Under Linear-sRGB output that residual is nowhere near zero, so the run fails before it reports a single codec figure. The check costs one extra frame per texture and would have caught this the first time it ran.

P-3 · The repointer only goes one way med measured

B5 wrote repointJson to move an image reference from a source to its transcode, because a texture that was encoded stayed encoded. A per-asset refusal is a verdict on a measurement, and measurements change — so the first attempt to revert eight textures left eight containers naming files that were no longer there. repoint --check named all eight, which is the safety net working; it is not the same as being able to fix them, and the fix by hand is editing the JSON chunk of eight GLBs.

It now returns an image to its source, moves the texture off KHR_texture_basisu back onto source, and drops the extension from extensionsUsed and extensionsRequired when the last KTX2 leaves a container — a container declaring an extension it does not use is refused outright by strict loaders. The BIN chunk is still copied through byte for byte, so B1's geometry guarantee holds by construction in both directions.

P-4 · Three budgets were lowered on this encode med measured

git log on tools/budget.json shows B5 (e6541a8) lowering three limits in the same commit that transcoded the eighteen:

LimitBefore B5 (17688b3)B5 setNeeded nowRestored to
public-total6,200,0005,870,000~6,122,0006,200,000
public-largest330,000271,000314,335330,000
demo-total13,860,00013,740,000~13,684,00013,860,000

Not one of them goes a byte past where it already was. A ratchet exists to stop a limit drifting up while nobody is looking; it does not exist to make a quality regression permanent by pricing the fix out, and a limit set by a saving that failed the project's own gate is not a limit that was earned. demo-total is restored even though the tree fits under B5's value, because 0.4% of headroom is a tripwire for whichever unrelated commit arrives next — applying the argument to two of the three and not the third would just move the failure somewhere it explains nothing.

P-5 · Eight times wrong, in the direction that does not matter low measured

encode-textures.mjs says a KTX2 “stays compressed in memory at roughly 1 bit per pixel against RGBA8's 32”, while the arithmetic three lines below charges one byte per pixel. The arithmetic is the one that runs, and it is right for ASTC 4x4 and BC7 and twice the true cost of the BC1/ETC2 this catalog actually transcodes to — conservative, which is the correct direction for a budget. The prose is simply wrong, and it is the sentence a reader would quote.

§ 31 · SPRINT

One sprint

Delivered in one pass. There is no second sprint because there is no second question: the deferral RM-005 handed forward was “has anybody looked at these”, and looking is one task.

D1

Measure the eighteen, and keep the instrument

doneP-1P-2P-31 weekno dependencies

Objective. Render every texture the encoder has ever considered against the source it was encoded from, act on whatever fails B4's 3.0 gate under B1's per-asset rule, and leave the measurement in the repository so the next encode cannot skip it.

ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • M-20 = 0. No texture ships compressed while measuring past 3.0 RMS, and the claim is a command rather than a spreadsheet
  • M-21 = 0.000. The rendered source matches its own decoded pixels, per texture, or the run reports nothing
  • M-22 = 21 of 21. Every GPU texture the encoder considered — shipped compressed or refused — has a rendered measurement on file
  • No limit moves past a value it has already held, and the commit says which commit each number comes from
  • Rollback. Per texture. Each refusal is one entry in REFUSED, one source file and one repoint
LANDED — D1
  • Nine of eighteen were past the gate, and the sprint's first act was to distrust its own instrument. The new harness was calibrated against C1's published figures before being pointed at anything, it failed, and the failure was worth more than the pass would have been — see P-2. Two programs of texture verdicts were reached through a frame the application never renders.
  • The oracle is a tool now, not a script. npm run oracle, in the shape of the five --check tools already in tools/. It recovers its own sources from the commit that deleted them, which is what makes it usable at all against a tree where the encode already happened — the encoder unlinks the source in the same run that writes the container.
  • Two traps are handled and both were real. TextureLoader hands back flipY = true and KTX2Loader hands back false, and a compressed texture cannot be flipped on upload — so comparing them as loaded differences an image against its own mirror. Both orientations are scored and mirrorTells records how confidently they were separated; it runs 10× to 86× on real content, so the trap would have quadrupled every figure. And the server 404s rather than serving an SPA fallback, which is the trap B4's oracle hit and had to be moved off vite preview for.
  • Eight ship as JPEGs again, one is re-encoded, nine were already fine. B1's per-asset rule, applied for the third time after the model catalog and Garden. cb-archnight-white_baked.png is the good case: quality 192 takes it from 3.392 to 2.728 and from a 187 KB PNG to a 55 KB container, so it is both better looking and smaller than either alternative.
  • The path bug the reverse repointer shipped with was caught by the check that exists for it. The first run wrote textures/textures/oak_wood.jpg — a doubled directory, because the finder returned a path and the caller joined it onto a directory again. repoint --check named all eight containers before anything else ran. A writer can be wrong; that is why the reader is a separate command.
  • Adding a KTX2 without measuring it now fails in four seconds. The render stays behind npm run oracle -- --check because it costs a browser and two minutes — the same reason ledger:check is a command. But the cheap half is a test: is there a measurement for this file, did it pass, and was the render path transparent when it ran. Confirmed by breaking all three.
  • Three budgets went back to values they had already held, and the VRAM ceiling did not move: Runtime assets 5.28 → 5.84 MB against 5.91, Largest single asset 252 → 307 KB against 322, Deployed tree 12.44 → 13.05 MB against 13.22, Texture VRAM 16.15 → 27.38 MB against an unchanged 45.15. The VRAM is the price of the correction and it is paid out of headroom that was already there.
  • 1,271 headless tests in 30 files (6 new) and 62 in the browser, five pipeline --checks green, ten budgets green, lint and typecheck clean, the type ledger at 0 of 0. No public signature, saved design or asset name changed — the eighteen live inside .glb containers and are never named by a document.
  • What this leaves, priced and then closed. Two room textures are now inside the gate rather than one — light_fine_wood.jpg at 2.639 and walllightmap.png at 0.592 — so the cache redesign C1 reverted “for want of a consumer” has two. Both were encoded to measure the prize rather than estimated: 43.7 KB of disk and 0.500 MB of VRAM. An earlier draft of this line said “exactly one consumer, worth about 0.7 MB”; it was wrong on the count and on the number, and the correction is the reason for encoding rather than arithmetic.
  • Against that, the cost of taking it, which C1 never had to price. acquireTexture would need a page-wide KTX2Loader, and formatSupport() returns null wherever the device cannot report its formats — Node and jsdom, which is every headless test that builds a Floor or an Edge. That is a failure mode the module does not have today. And unlike the eighteen, a room texture is named in saved designs: light_fine_wood.jpg is in src/catalog/textures.json, so compressing it is a retirement, and un-retiring one is harder than reverting a model texture. This program has just spent a sprint undoing an over-eager encode; committing a second one to a save-format surface for 43.7 KB would be the same mistake with the same shape.
  • So the answer is 0.500 MB against 17.77 MB of VRAM headroom, and no. Written down with both sides measured so it stops being rediscovered: the blocker is no longer the codec and no longer the absence of a consumer, it is that the consumer is worth 2.8% of headroom already sitting unused. If a room texture is ever added that is large and inside the gate, that number changes and this is the note to re-read.

One week. What is worth carrying out of it is not the nine textures — it is that a measurement nobody can re-run stops being a measurement. The oracle was correct all three times it was written and thrown away all three times, and in the gap the project made two texture decisions it could not check and one of them was wrong. The fix was not more care; it was git add.

PRODUCT ROADMAP · DRAWING Nº RM-007

A planner for anyone's home

Six programmes took a 2014 demo to a tool with gates, budgets and an instrument for everything it ships. None of them asked what a person who wants to design their own home needs from it. This drawing does. It audits what the application can do today, sets that against a Coohom-class planner and the free reference the field already has, and lays out eight programmes, 42 weeks, from a floorplan demo to a floor and house planner anyone can use — 2D and 3D, fully customisable, free without a catch.

Rev B, same day. The audience was fixed as web desktop only. Sprint L1 — the phone and tablet shell, 2.5 weeks — is withdrawn; Q-7 stays on the log, declined by decision rather than deleted; the shell was then measured at 1024×768, 1280×720 and 1366×768 and holds at all three with no overflow. 44.5 → 42 weeks, 26 → 25 sprints, M-31 re-pointed at a laptop viewport.

§ 32 · EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

What it takes to be the planner people reach for

The aim, in the words it was given: a floor planner like Coohom but not that deep — one anyone can use to design their home and view it in 3D, with everything a person may need, no premium tier, no product linkages, absolutely free. Every finding below was produced by reading the tree at 8510642 or by running the application in Chromium and looking at what it drew.

0 of 2
items placed in 3D that appear on the 2D plan. Two were added, the status bar counted two, the plan drew neither — the plan view has no code path for items at all (floorplanner_view.js:588)
5 + 2
wall and floor textures offered, two of them the same image at different scales. No paint colour, no ceiling material, no normal or roughness map anywhere
1
storey. Model owns one Floorplan (model.js:77) and the save format has no word for a level. Stairs are three furniture models
0
ways to get a picture out. No image of the 3D view, no panorama, no PNG, SVG or PDF of the plan. OBJ and glTF export the geometry, which is not what a person shows their family
42 wk
eight programmes at the single-developer cadence RM-001 was planned at. ±40 % at this altitude; each programme is re-estimated in its own drawing before it starts

What is already there is not small, and this roadmap gets all of it for free. A library that draws walls, straight and curved, joins them into rooms with names and areas, slopes them by corner elevation, textures both faces of each wall and every floor, cuts holes for doors and windows, and renders the lot lit, shadowed, tone-mapped and fogged, in orbit, orthographic and first-person — with undo across everything, autosave to IndexedDB with honest recovery, five display units, a traceable backdrop image, OBJ and glTF export, and a catalog of 168 models. Under it, the things a product programme usually has to build first and never gets to: 1,333 headless and 62 browser tests, coverage and size ratchets, an asset pipeline that compresses, measures and refuses, stable identity for walls and rooms across edits, one change event per gesture. RM-001 to RM-006 bought that. What they did not buy is a single feature a homeowner would notice, and that is what the next 42 weeks are for.

“Like Coohom, but not that deep” is a precise brief once Coohom is read literally. Its own floor-planner page names the product: draw or upload a layout, edit walls, rooms, doors and windows, wall thickness, door and window sizing, thousands of furniture models, 2D and 3D, high-resolution renders, 360° panoramas and walkthroughs, templates, a project dashboard, multi-storey with stairs, custom units, export of 2D drawings and renders. Behind that sits a 600,000-model commercial catalog, a cloud render farm, an AI that drafts a plan from a photo, and a login. Not that deep means the first list without the second. Everything in the first list is buildable on what is here, in the browser, against a static host, with no server and no account — and the four things in the second list are the four this roadmap declines by name (§ 36).

The three constraints decide the architecture, and it is the one the project already has. Free, no premium means nothing lives behind a login, so there is no login: designs live in the browser and in files the person owns, and sharing is a file or a URL. No product linkages means the catalog is a data file of CC0 assets with the licence recorded per item, not a feed — which the pipeline already treats it as. Anyone means any desktop browser — the audience is web desktop only, a decision taken with this drawing — offline after first load, no install, and a first hour that needs no manual. Local-first on a static host is what src/app is today; the roadmap keeps it, and it is why the effort is 42 weeks and not a company.

The shape. Five releases, each one a person can use: Plan any home (13 wk) puts furniture, doors and windows on the plan, makes drawing precise, makes openings parametric and gives designs a home; Make it yours (9 wk) is materials, colours and a catalog worth browsing; The whole house (7.5 wk) is levels, stairwells and a roof; Show it (5 wk) is light, photos, panoramas and sharing; Everyone (7.5 wk) is offline, help, languages, keyboard-only editing and a public address. The first sprint is E1 — furniture on the plan: the most visible gap, the best contained, and 2.5 weeks.

§ 33 · AUDIT

Read the way a person uses it, not the way a build checks it. present works and is reachable from the interface; partial exists in the library or the model but is not exposed, not persisted or not finished; missing has no code path. The last column is where to look.

Measured on the day

The application was booted in headless Chromium at 1440×900 and 390×844, driven through fifteen states and screenshotted at each. What the pictures showed, beyond the table:

  • Two items placed, none on the plan. A chair and a bed were added from the catalog and selected in 3D, with their dimension labels drawn in the viewport and the status bar reading 2 items. The plan beside them showed an empty room. This is the finding a first-time user makes in their first five minutes.
  • The split view does not re-frame. Zoom-to-fit runs once at mount, at full width; switching to Split leaves the plan half off the left edge and the 3D room cropped. Small, and the kind of thing that reads as broken.Not reproduced, and withdrawn by E1 on 2026-08-18. Measured on both paths a person takes — plan → split and plan → 3D → split — the plan is centred in the pane, off by 8 px and 1 px, clipped in neither: _recentreForResize already handles it. Read off a screenshot taken after a longer sequence, like the cross-view claim two bullets down. Nothing was written for it; a forced zoom-to-fit on every layout change would discard a zoom and pan the user chose.
  • Wall labels read m:5m. The prefix is the midline flag, and nobody outside this repository knows that.
  • Selecting a wall in 3D highlights it on the plan and opens the surface picker — the cross-view selection is already right, and is what E1 builds the other direction on.Wrong, and corrected by RM-008 T-2 on 2026-08-18. A 3D wall click changes 0 of 492,000 plan pixels and a plan wall click changes 0 of 432,000 pixels of the 3D view; the highlight seen here was hover state left over from the previous shot, which clicked the plan. Only the inspector half is true. E1 therefore builds both directions, at 3 weeks rather than 2.5, and programme E is 7.5 weeks rather than 7.
  • The phone viewport is unusable, not merely cramped. The rail, the top bar and a 300 px inspector leave a 40 px plan at 10 % zoom. Declined in rev B: the audience is web desktop only, and the laptop end of that range — 1024×768, 1280×720, 1366×768 — was then measured and holds.
  • The console warns Texture marked for update but no image data found on every frame until the images land. Harmless, and a sign that a needsUpdate is being set on a clone before its source has decoded; worth one look under E1 rather than a finding of its own.

The foundation is the asset — and two of its rules will be felt

The gates, the pipeline and the identity work mean the product programmes below do not start by building scaffolding. Two of the standing rules will bite, and are worth naming before they do. The size budgets are ratchets that may move up with a recorded reason and never down to pass; a catalog that triples and a material library that goes from seven images to ninety will need public-total and demo-total raised, several times, each with the reason in the commit — and they will need a budget that does not exist yet, first-load payload, so that growing the tree never grows what a person downloads before their first wall. The polygon predicates are pinned constants (core/utils.js:44): furniture cannot collide with anything today because pointInPolygon returns false and polygonPolygonIntersect returns false, by a preserved bug the ledger says is left alone on purpose. Collision is a feature people expect from a room planner, and J4 takes it as the deliberate re-baseline the ledger asks for — new predicate, feature flag, characterization tests updated in the same commit — not as a fix.

§ 34 · DECISION LOG

Fourteen gaps, ranked by what a person hits first

Severity here is not risk to the build; it is how soon someone designing their own home runs into it and whether they can work around it. Each row names the programme that closes it. Coohom's own feature page and Sweet Home 3D's — the free, open reference — were used as the checklist of what a planner is expected to do; neither was used for how.

NºFindingHitWhereProgramme
Q-1Furniture, doors and windows are invisible on the planfirst 5 minfloorplanner_view.js:588E1
Q-2Doors and windows are three baked meshes and seven kit pieces, with no width, height, sill, hinge or swing of their ownfirst hourcatalog.json · in_wall_item.jsF1
Q-3One storey; stairs go nowhere; there is no roof, so there is no housefirst housemodel.js:77G1 · G2 · F3
Q-4Seven textures, no colour picker for walls, no ceiling material, no PBRfirst hourtextures.json · edge.js:282H1
Q-5The catalog is browsed by placement type, not by room; no dimensions, tags or licence; twelve wall segments filed as furniturefirst hourcatalog.json · useCatalog.js:44J1 · J2
