Q-10The 3D view cannot take a picture: no image export, no panorama, no sun, no lamp that lights, no ambient occlusionwhen showing someoneuseDesignIO.js:225 · lights.jsH2 · H3
Q-11Furniture passes through walls and through other furniture; the predicates that would stop it are pinned constantsfirst sofafloor_item.js:57 · utils.js:44J4
Q-12No multi-select, copy/paste, mirror, align, group, stack, or item-to-item snapfirst room setcontroller.js · useItemActions.jsJ4
Q-13The plan cannot leave the app: no PNG, SVG or PDF, no scale barwhen printinguseDesignIO.js:219E4
Q-14No offline, no share without a file, no help written for users, one languagelow each—K2 · K3 · L2 · L3

Q-1 · The plan does not show the furniture high measured

Every planner people compare this to — Coohom, Planner 5D, HomeByMe, Floorplanner, Sweet Home 3D — is used mostly from the top-down view, with the 3D as the check. Here the plan is a wall editor. FloorplannerView2D.draw() walks rooms, walls, corners, the target and the grid; the word item does not occur in src/scripts/floorplanner/. The data is all there: every item knows its footprint (Item.getCorners('x','z')), its rotation and its wall edge, and Model holds the scene and the floorplan side by side. This is a drawing layer and a hit-test, not a model change — which is why it is first and why it is 2.5 weeks and not five.

Q-2 · Doors and windows are furniture high

An in-wall item cuts a hole the size of its bounding box (edge.js:471–482) and that is the whole door model: no sill height as a number, no hinge side, no swing arc on the plan, no frame that follows the wall's thickness. Coohom lists door and window sizing and wall thickness as first-screen features, and Sweet Home 3D computes the wall hole from a parametric opening. F1 replaces the three baked meshes and seven kit pieces as the default with a procedural opening — frame, leaf or sash, glass — that takes width, height, sill, hinge and swing, draws its symbol on the plan, and saves as numbers. The catalog models stay for anyone who prefers them.

Q-3 · One storey high

Model constructs one Floorplan and one Scene (model.js:77–78); every composable, both views and the file assume it. Adding levels touches every layer, which is exactly why it is programme G and not sprint E2: it should land on a single-level editor that is already complete, behind a flag, with a single-level file byte-identical before and after. Stairs (F3) and stairwells and a roof (G2) are what turn floor planner into house planner, and the brief said both.

Q-4 · Seven textures high

The surface picker offers five wall images and two floor images, and Checker and Bricks are the same JPEG. There is no way to paint a wall a colour, which is the first thing most people do; ceilings are one flat tint from the render profile; and every material is a single albedo, so the studio profile's lighting has nothing to catch on. H1 is a material model with albedo, normal and roughness, a colour picker that works on any surface, and a CC0 library of about ninety materials from ambientCG and Poly Haven — each one through the resize, encode and oracle pipeline that already exists, each one inside the 3.0 RMS gate or shipped uncompressed under B1's rule.

Q-7 · No phone, no tablet declined measured

At 390×844 the shell renders the desktop layout smaller: a 52 px rail, a 300 px inspector, and what is left for the plan. The pointer handling underneath is already unified — touch draws walls today — so it would have been a shell problem, not a library one, and rev A priced it at 2.5 weeks as L1. Rev B withdraws it: the audience is web desktop only. The finding stays on the log so it is not rediscovered as a bug, and the desktop end of the range was measured before the sprint was struck — at 1024×768, 1280×720 and 1366×768 the shell holds with no horizontal overflow, the catalog drawer overlays rather than squeezes, and both panes stay usable. The one desktop-width fault seen — the plan not re-framing on a layout change — is already in E1.

Q-11 · Furniture goes through walls med

FloorItem.isValidPosition is documented in the predicate ledger as intentionally inert: pointInPolygon is constant-false, so no item is ever “in a room”, so the early return true always fires and placement is left to the user. Blueprint3d meant that; a person furnishing a bedroom does not. J4 turns collision on as a re-baseline — pointInPolygon2, the sibling that works, is already used for room detection (room.js:277) — with a warning highlight rather than a hard stop, so a rug can still go under a bed.

The rest are smaller in code than in experience, and each is one sprint or less: Q-5 is a metadata pass over a JSON file plus a browsing UI; Q-6 is a second IndexedDB store and a grid of thumbnails; Q-8 and Q-9 are the drawing tools every CAD user assumes and per-wall fields the model already carries; Q-10 is a render-to-texture and a sun vector; Q-12 is controller work on selection; Q-13 is the plan drawn again into an SVG string at scale; Q-14 is four independent, deferrable pieces.

§ 35 · PROGRAMMES

Eight programmes, five releases, 42 weeks

High level on purpose. Each programme names its sprints and their cost at the single-developer cadence RM-001 was planned at, and stops there: the sprint contents, gates and rollback are for the programme's own drawing, written against the tree of the day it starts, as RM-002 to RM-006 were. Programme letters continue from D and skip I, as drawing revisions do. Effort is ±40 % at this altitude — the range narrows to what RM-004 and RM-005 achieved only once a programme is measured.

Release 1 · 13 wk
Plan any home
E · F1 · F2 · K1
Release 2 · 9 wk
Make it yours
H1 · J1 · J2 · J4
Release 3 · 7.5 wk
The whole house
F3 · G1 · G2
Release 4 · 5 wk
Show it
H2 · H3 · K2
Release 5 · 7.5 wk
Everyone
L · J3 · K3 · M

Every release is one a person can use, in that order, and none waits on a later one. Programmes are grouped by what they change; releases are grouped by what a person gets. The two are deliberately not the same list.

E

The plan is the product

proposedQ-1Q-8Q-9Q-137 wk

Objective. Make the 2D plan the place a design is made, not just where walls are drawn: everything in the design visible on it, drawing to a number, annotation, and a way out onto paper.

SprintWhatEffort
E1Furniture, doors and windows drawn on the plan as footprints and symbols; select, move and rotate them there; selection mirrored both ways between plan and 3D; re-frame on layout change; wall-label prefixes made legible2.5 wk
E2Precision drawing: typed length and angle while drawing, 15° angle snap, rectangle-room tool, wall split and join, alignment guides; per-wall thickness and height in the inspector and in the file (additive fields)2 wk
E3Annotation: dimension lines, text labels, room type with a styled label, north arrow, per-room ceiling height1.5 wk
E4Plan export: PNG and SVG at a chosen scale, print to PDF with a scale bar and title block1 wk
METRICS THIS PROGRAMME ADDS
  • M-23 — plan parity. Items in the scene equals footprints on the plan, for every fixture design, in a headless test and in the browser tier
  • M-24 — round trip. Per-wall thickness and height written by 3.x are read back exactly, and a 2.0.0 file loads with the configured defaults, byte-identical on re-save when nothing was set

Re-estimated by RM-008 (the programme’s own drawing, 2026-08-18) at 7.5 weeks, E1 at 3 rather than 2.5: cross-view selection turned out not to exist in either direction, not one (T-2). The figures above are rev B’s and stay as written; the programme total moves 42 → 42.5.

F

Openings and structure

proposedQ-2Q-36 wk

Objective. Doors, windows, openings, half walls, columns and stairs as things with numbers, generated in the browser, so a design never depends on a mesh the catalog happens to have.

SprintWhatEffort
F1Parametric doors and windows: procedural frame, leaf or sash and glass; width, height, sill, hinge side and swing in the inspector; swing arcs and sash symbols on the plan; a handful of styles; saved as numbers; catalog meshes remain an option3 wk
F2Openings without a mesh (archway, pass-through), half walls via per-wall height, columns and beams as parametric items; an audit of wall joins at thickness1.5 wk
F3Parametric stairs — straight, L, U — with a footprint on the plan and a stairwell hint for G21.5 wk
METRICS THIS PROGRAMME ADDS
  • M-25 — openings are numbers. Every door and window in a fixture design round-trips width, height, sill, hinge and swing through the file, and its wall hole matches them to the centimetre
G

Levels

proposedQ-3after E and F6 wk

Objective. A house, not a floor: storeys stacked in 3D and switched on the plan, stairwells that cut through, and a roof over the lot.

SprintWhatEffort
G1Levels in the model with elevation and height; levels[] in the file, additive, a single-level file unchanged; level switcher on the plan with the level below ghosted; 3D stacked with per-level visibility; behind a flag until the fixture suite covers it3.5 wk
G2Stairwells and floor openings; a simple roof generator (flat, gable, hip) over the footprint; an exterior view2.5 wk
METRICS THIS PROGRAMME ADDS
  • M-26 — two levels, one file. A two-storey fixture saves, loads and renders both levels; every existing single-level fixture is byte-identical on re-save

The widest change in this drawing. It touches Model, both views, the controller, undo, autosave and the file. It goes last among the structural programmes for that reason, and it does not start until E and F have settled the single-level editor it multiplies.

Surfaces and light

proposedQ-4Q-107 wk

Objective. Any surface any colour or material, lit like a room, and a picture at the end of it.

SprintWhatEffort

Catalog

proposedQ-5Q-11Q-127.5 wk

M3The first-load budget and lazy packs wired through the manifest; a Lighthouse floor0.5 wk

What comes first

E1. It is the finding a new user makes in five minutes, it needs no change to the model or the file, the data it draws already exists on every item, and the cross-view selection it completes is half built. It is also the sprint that teaches the most about the rest of E: once items are on the plan, typed lengths, dimension lines and a PNG of the plan are drawn against a plan that means something. Before E1 there is the drawing for programme E — RM-008 — measured against the tree of the day it starts, in the shape of the six that precede this one.

If time is short

The roadmap is 42 weeks because the brief is everything a person may need. It contracts without breaking. In the order they should go:

DropWhat is lostSaved
L3Languages other than English1 wk
H3Panoramas and the walkthrough polish; the walkthrough itself stays1.5 wk
J3Bringing your own model; the catalog still grows1.5 wk
K3Offline; the app still runs from cache in most browsers, just not by promise1 wk
G2Roof and stairwells; levels still stack, stairs still draw2.5 wk
All five — still a multi-level planner with materials, openings, a catalog, projects and a phone layout7.5 wk
§ 36 · RISKS & NON-GOALS

What could go wrong, and what this is not

Seven risks, all of them things the six earlier programmes either created on purpose or would recognise; and the list of what the brief's three constraints rule out, so it is not rediscovered one feature request at a time.

RiskWhere it bitesMitigation
The size budgets are ratchets and the asset tree will tripleH1, J2 — every pack and material libraryRaise up with the reason in the commit, as the rule allows; never lower; add first-load (M-30) so the tree can grow while the first download does not; packs lazy by manifest
The save format grows four times — per-wall fields, openings, levels, materialsE2, F1, G1, H1Every field additive; a 3.0.0 format version whose reader takes 2.0.0 files unchanged; the fixture suite gains one file per addition and the byte-identical re-save test on the old ones
Levels touch every layer at onceG1Last of the structural programmes; behind a flag; single-level files byte-identical; its own drawing before a line is written
A CC0 catalog looks low-poly beside a commercial oneJ2, and every screenshotSay so; procedural openings and PBR surfaces carry the realism a room needs; the Kenney kit is coherent, which matters more than polygon count at room scale
PBR, more lights and occlusion on an integrated GPUH1, H2The profile switch already exists; every effect behind it and off by default where M-28 fails; measured on the browser tier's software renderer, which is the floor
Re-baselining the polygon predicatesJ4A new predicate under a flag, the ledger updated, the characterization tests changed in the same commit, and the room-detection tests untouched because room detection never used the broken ones
42 weeks at one developer’s cadenceeverythingEach release is usable on its own; the contraction table above; each programme's drawing re-estimates before it starts, so the number is corrected five times rather than discovered once

Non-goals

Named so that they stay named. Accounts and cloud storage — a login is the first thing a free tool grows and the first thing that stops being free. An AI that drafts a plan from a photo — the backdrop tracer is the honest version, and it works today. DWG import — a closed format; DXF may come later as a K-programme item once there is a plan worth importing into. Kitchen and wardrobe configurators — the depth the brief declined. A cloud render farm — H2's Photo is what one browser can do well; a path tracer in the browser is a research sprint, not a promise. Collaboration, comments, multi-user — a server. Product catalogs, prices, links to buy — the brief, in its own words. A 600,000-model library — 500 items a person can find beat 600,000 they cannot, and the pipeline measures every one of the 500.

Forty-two weeks, and the first of them draws the furniture on the plan. What is worth carrying out of this drawing is not the number — it will be corrected by each programme's own sheet — but the shape: local-first, static, CC0, no login is not a constraint the brief imposed on the architecture; it is the architecture the project already has, and the reason a Coohom-class planner without Coohom's company behind it is a plan and not a wish.

PROGRAMME E · DRAWING Nº RM-008

The plan is the product

RM-007 put programme E first because the 2D plan is where a floor plan is made and this one is a wall editor. Measuring it before writing any of it found the reason underneath: the 2D view cannot see the furniture, because a Floorplan has no path to a Scene — and that cross-view selection does not exist in either direction, which RM-007 said was half built. Four sprints, 7.5 weeks, and the first decision is a contract rather than a drawing routine.

§ 37 · EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

What measuring the plan changed about the plan

Seven measurements against the tree at 1d53fc8, five of them run in a real browser against the running library rather than read off the source. Two contradict something this document set already says, and one of those two is RM-007's, written the same day.

0 px
change on the plan when a wall is clicked in 3D, and 0 px change in 3D when a wall is clicked on the plan. Cross-view selection does not exist in either direction — RM-007 said it was already right one way
6 keys
on a Floorplan: runtime, walls, corners, rooms, metaroomsdata, floorTextures. No model, no scene. The 2D view is handed exactly this object, so it cannot reach an item even in principle
0.79 ms
to draw a 36-room, 144-wall plan with 150 item footprints — 4.7 % of a 16.7 ms frame. Furniture on the plan is not a performance question, and measuring it first is how that stops being an assumption
36–69 %
statement coverage across the five files E touches — the least-covered in the library, against floors of 79/68/79/78. Every sprint here must carry its own tests or the ratchet breaks

The finding that shapes the programme. Floorplanner2D is constructed with this.model.floorplan (blueprint.js:234), and a Floorplan holds walls, corners and rooms. Items live on Model.scene, and Scene knows its Model while Floorplan does not — the reference goes one way, deliberately, and it is the same one-way rule that keeps the model layer plain. So “draw the furniture on the plan” is not a drawing task with a lookup in front of it; it is a question about which object is allowed to know about which, and answering it wrongly is how a clean layer boundary is lost for a feature.

The answer this programme takes is a projection, not a reference. Model already owns both halves and already dispatches a typed ChangeSet carrying CHANGE_ITEMS (RM-003 A2). E1 has Model publish a plan projection — a flat array of footprints, each one id, centre, half-extent, rotation, type and label, in centimetres — and has the 2D view subscribe to it. The view draws data it was given; nothing in src/scripts/model gains a back-reference; Floorplanner2D's two-argument constructor keeps working, because the projection arrives by event and not by argument. It is also the shape that makes E1 testable headlessly: a projection can be asserted without a canvas, which the five files this programme touches badly need.

The correction. RM-007 § 33 recorded that selecting a wall in 3D highlights it on the plan, and called the cross-view selection “already right”. It is not: clicking a wall in the 3D pane changes zero pixels of the plan, and clicking one on the plan changes zero pixels of the 3D view. What that screenshot showed was hover state left over from an earlier click on the plan itself — the plan's activeWall is set by its own mousemove and by nothing else. The inspector is the only surface the two views share. E1 therefore builds both directions rather than one, which is why this drawing prices it at 3 weeks where RM-007 priced 2.5, and programme E at 7.5 where RM-007 priced 7.

What is cheap, measured rather than hoped. The plan redraw is 0.112 ms for the default room and 0.593 ms for a 36-room, 144-wall plan — about 3.5 µs a wall. Item footprints cost 0.032 ms for twenty, 0.082 for sixty and 0.197 for a hundred and fifty, so the worst case measured here is 0.79 ms a frame. The 2D path has been coalesced to one draw per animation frame since P6 — nineteen invalidate() call sites against six direct draw() — so E1 hooks invalidate() and inherits that. And the plan's entire output goes through 77 raw canvas calls, 64 of them inside eight primitive and setup methods, with only two outliers (drawCornerAngles, 7; drawOriginCrossHair, 6). That is what makes E4's SVG export one week: implement eight primitives against a second backend, refactor two methods, and every existing drawing routine follows for free.

§ 38 · DECISION LOG

Seven findings, five of them run in a browser

Each row was produced against 1d53fc8. Where the method was a rendered comparison, the two screenshots were differenced pixel by pixel with a tolerance of 6 per channel rather than eyeballed — which is the specific mistake T-2 corrects.

NºFindingSeverityMethodSprint
T-1A Floorplan has no path to a Scene, so the 2D view cannot reach an itemshapes E1constructed in-browser, keys readE1
T-2Cross-view selection does not exist in either direction — RM-007 said it did one waycorrectionpixel diff, both directionsE1
T-3The five files E touches are the least-covered in the library, at 36–69 % statementsmedcoverage-summary.jsonall four
T-4Furniture on the plan is affordable: 0.79 ms for the worst case measuredclears E1timed in-browser, 60 drawsE1
T-5An SVG backend is eight primitives and two outlier methods, not a rewriteclears E477 ctx calls attributed to methodsE4
T-6The save path writes explicit literals, so a field not named in it is silently dropped on round tripmedfloorplan.js:723, readE2 · E3
T-7Only EVENT_ITEM_MOVE_FINISH fires for a moved item; nothing fires during the draglowcontroller.js:451, grepE1

T-1 · The plan cannot see the furniture shapes E1 measured

Constructed in a live page and read rather than inferred from the source: a Floorplan's own properties are runtime, walls, corners, rooms, metaroomsdata and floorTextures. There is no model and no scene. Model has exactly two — floorplan and scene — and Scene carries model, so the reference runs scene → model → floorplan and never back. BlueprintJS hands the 2D view the floorplan alone.

Three ways to close it, and why the third wins. Passing the Model into Floorplanner2D widens a public constructor and points the view at the whole document to read one list. Giving Floorplan a back-reference to Model puts the scene inside the layer whose whole discipline is that it holds plain data with no DOM and no GPU — and every test that builds a bare Floorplan would then be building half a document. The third is a projection: Model computes a footprint list and dispatches it on the ChangeSet it already sends for CHANGE_ITEMS; the view holds the last one it received and draws it. No new coupling, no constructor change, and the drawing code takes plain numbers — which is the only version of this that can be tested without a canvas.

T-2 · Neither view tells the other anything correction measured

RM-007 § 33 said: “Selecting a wall in 3D highlights it on the plan and opens the surface picker — the cross-view selection is already right, and is what E1 builds the other direction on.” The first half is wrong and the conclusion drawn from it is wrong.

ActionOther view, pixels changedInspectorVerdict
Click a wall in the 3D pane0 of 492,000opens “Wall surface”plan unaffected
Click a wall on the plan0 of 432,000opens “Wall”3D unaffected

How the mistake was made, since that is the reusable part. The RM-007 pass drove the application through fifteen states in one session and read the screenshots in order. Shot 13 clicked a wall on the plan; shot 14 clicked a wall in 3D. The plan showed a blue wall in both, and the blue in 14 was read as a response to the 3D click. It was the hover state from 13, still set: Floorplanner2D.activeWall is written by its own mousemove handler and cleared by nothing that a 3D click reaches. A screenshot compared against a memory is not a measurement. Both rows above come from differencing two images captured seconds apart in the same run, with everything else held still.

The consequence for the plan is small and worth stating plainly: E1 builds two directions rather than one, so it is priced at 3 weeks here against RM-007's 2.5, and programme E at 7.5 against 7. RM-007 keeps its figures with a correction note beside them rather than being rewritten.

T-3 · E adds code to the least-covered files in the library med measured

FileStatementsBranchesFunctionsTouched by
items/floor_item.js36.0020.0060.00E1
three/controller.js51.6735.9765.71E1
floorplanner/floorplanner.js63.4043.2478.37E1 · E2
items/item.js63.6044.2643.18E1
floorplanner/floorplanner_view.js69.2663.4782.69E1 · E3 · E4
Project floors: statements 79, branches 68, lines 79, functions 78. Current: 80.26 / 68.77 / 80.32 / 79.17 — branches sit 0.77 above the floor.

Adding uncovered code to files already below the project average moves the aggregate down, and branches have 0.77 % of room. This is not an argument for raising the floors — they are ratchets and they move up only when a change earns it. It is the reason every sprint below carries its own test budget explicitly rather than as an afterthought, and the reason the projection in T-1 is worth building for its testability alone.

T-4 · The cost is measured, and it is small clears E1 measured

PlanWallsRoomsms / draw
Default room410.112
Four rooms1640.172
Sixteen rooms64160.328
Thirty-six rooms144360.593
Item footprints, drawn as a rotated rect, a facing tick and a label: 20 → 0.032 ms · 60 → 0.082 ms · 150 → 0.197 ms

Sixty draws each, after a warm-up, in headless Chromium at 1200×800. The worst case anyone will hit — a 36-room plan with 150 items — is 0.79 ms, or 4.7 % of a 16.7 ms frame. Every draw is already coalesced to one per animation frame (P6), so this is a per-frame cost and not a per-event one. Measured before the sprint rather than after it, because “furniture on the plan will be too slow” is the kind of objection that is cheap to answer now and expensive to answer once half the sprint is written.

T-5 · The SVG backend is eight methods clears E4 measured

Every mark the plan makes goes through the 2D context, and there are 77 such calls in floorplanner_view.js. Attributing each to its enclosing method: 64 sit inside eight primitive and setup methods — drawLine, drawCurvedLine, drawPolygon, drawPolygonCurved, drawCircle, drawTextLabel, drawBezierObject, drawGrid, plus draw's own clear and transform — and 13 sit in two methods that reach past them: drawCornerAngles (7) and drawOriginCrossHair (6). So E4 is: define the primitive surface as an interface, implement it twice, and refactor exactly two methods onto it. The twelve drawLine callers, eight drawTextLabel callers and five drawCircle callers need no change at all.

T-6 · A field the save path does not name is lost med

Floorplan.saveFloorplan writes an object literal per corner and per wall — {x, y, elevation}, and the wall's own named set. Anything else on the object, and anything a third-party file carried in, is dropped on the next save; DesignDocument.parse does not reject unknown fields, it ignores them. Nothing is broken by this today, and it means E2's per-wall thickness and height and E3's annotations each need an explicit line in the writer as well as the reader. Named here so that “the field is on the model” is never mistaken for “the field is saved” — which is exactly the state per-wall thickness and height are in right now (RM-007 Q-9).

T-7 · Nothing fires while an item is being dragged low

The only movement event for an item is EVENT_ITEM_MOVE_FINISH, dispatched once from Controller.itemMoveFinished when the pointer is released. A footprint on the plan would therefore jump to its new place on mouse-up rather than follow the drag. E1 makes the plan follow live by having the controller mark the projection dirty during a drag — not by adding a per-move event, which would put a dispatch inside a pointermove handler and is the shape RM-002 R-05 spent a sprint removing. The existing event stays the commit point for undo.

§ 39 · SPRINTS

Four sprints, 7.5 weeks

Each carries its own acceptance, its own test budget — because of T-3 — and a rollback that is one revert. E1 is the only one with a new contract in it; E2 to E4 are additions on top of that contract and on top of what the plan already draws.

E1

The plan shows the design

doneT-1T-2T-4T-73 wkno dependencies

Objective. Everything in the design visible on the plan, selectable there, and selection shared with the 3D view in both directions.

  • The plan projection. Model publishes a footprint list — id, centre, half-extent, rotation, item type, label, and the wall edge for a wall-bound item — in centimetres, on the ChangeSet it already dispatches for CHANGE_ITEMS. Nothing in the model layer gains a back-reference (T-1).
  • Footprints drawn. A rotated rectangle with a facing tick and a name, at the plan's own scale; doors and windows drawn as openings in the wall run rather than as boxes on it; on-floor items (rugs) drawn under the rest.
  • Hit-testing and handling. Pick, move and rotate an item on the plan, with the same grid and axis snapping walls already use, and the same undo entry a 3D move produces.
  • Selection in both directions (T-2): a wall, room, corner or item selected in either view is highlighted in the other and shown in the inspector. One selection, two views.
  • Live during a drag (T-7): the controller marks the projection dirty as an item moves, and the plan repaints on the frame it already had scheduled.
  • Two repairs that belong here: re-frame the plan when the layout changes, so switching to Split does not leave it half off-screen; and drop the raw m: / i: / e: prefixes from wall labels, which are the wall-information flags and mean nothing to a reader.
ACCEPTANCE · OBSERVABILITY · DoD
  • M-23 = parity. Items in the scene equals footprints in the projection, for every fixture design, asserted headlessly — and rasterised once in the browser tier beside the existing plan-canvas case
  • M-32 = 0 px drift. Selecting an entity in one view changes the other view's pixels; the two directions asserted by the same pixel-diff method that produced T-2, so the measurement that caught the mistake is the one that guards it
  • Frame budget. A 36-room, 150-item fixture draws in under 2 ms in the browser tier — 2.5× the 0.79 ms measured, so the gate fails on a regression rather than on noise
  • Test budget. The projection is pure data, so it is tested without a canvas; floorplanner_view.js and item.js end the sprint no lower than they start (T-3)
  • Rollback. One revert. No file-format change, no public signature change, no saved design affected
LANDED — E1
  • The projection landed first, alone, before a line of drawing code. That was the commitment and it paid immediately: the contract is pure data, so it is pinned by 24 tests with no canvas, no renderer and no browser, and coverage went up on the commit that introduced the feature — branches 68.77 → 69.31. Model derives the footprints and hands them to the floorplan; nothing gained a back-reference and no constructor changed.
  • T-2 is closed, and measured the way it was found. A wall clicked in the 3D view now changes 5,732 plan pixels where it changed 0; an item clicked on the plan changes 20,224 pixels of the 3D view where it changed 0. Both figures come from differencing two rasters captured seconds apart in one run, which is the method whose absence caused the original mistake. The browser tier asserts the same claim on every build (M-32).
  • Four bugs, three of them older than this sprint, all found by driving the application rather than reading it. Controller.clickDragged guarded a null selection on the line after the call that dereferences it — unreachable until now, because the only way to hold state SELECTED with nothing selected was clearSelection() on teardown. setSelectedObject(null) leaves the state machine in SELECTED, which stops checkWallsAndFloors and makes every wall in the 3D view unclickable; the way out is a new deselect(), because doing it inside setSelectedObject re-enters through onEntry forever — tried, and the suite returned a stack overflow rather than a wrong picture. The pan branch's condition was a list of things that could be grabbed and an item was not on it, so the first drag of a chair panned the plan. And showItemSelected is handed whatever is selected, which useSelection documents may be anything at all.
  • The 3D view renders on demand, which nothing in the plan knew. A position written by a 2D drag sat in the scene graph until something unrelated asked for a frame, so the 3D furniture stayed where it used to be. Main subscribes to EVENT_ITEMS_PROJECTED and asks for one frame; the scene graph is already correct by the time it arrives.
  • Wall labels no longer read m:5m. The prefixes are the wall-information flags, and the m stands for midline. They are empty strings now and still configurable, because an embedder drawing interior and exterior lengths together does need to tell them apart. A characterization test pinned the old default; changing a characterization expectation is normally the wrong move, so the reasoning sits beside the assertion rather than in a commit message.
  • Every file this sprint touched ends higher than it started — the acceptance criterion T-3 asked for. floorplanner.js 63.40 → 68.31, floorplanner_view.js 69.26 → 73.37, model/model.js 78.49 → 82.83, plan_projection.js at 100. Project branches 68.77 → 70.09, statements 80.26 → 80.66. 38 new tests: 28 headless, 10 in the browser tier.
  • One budget went up, deliberately, with the reason in the file. lib-esm-gzip 50,300 → 53,500: the ESM entry grew 3.1 KB gzipped because E1 adds a feature to the library, not to the application. The property that limit guards — three and bezier-js external, an order of magnitude below the ~423 KB a lost externals config produces — is unchanged, and the new ceiling keeps the same ~5 % headroom the limits were set with.
  • What did not land, and why. The re-frame repair: RM-007 recorded that switching to Split leaves the plan half off the left edge, and it does not. Measured on both paths a person takes — plan → split, and plan → 3D → split — the plan is centred in the pane, offset by 8 px and 1 px respectively, clipped in neither. _recentreForResize already handles it. Nothing was written: forcing a zoom-to-fit on every layout change would throw away a zoom and pan the user chose, which is a worse behaviour than the one that was reported and is not happening.
  • 1,385 headless tests and 72 in the browser, lint, typecheck, the type ledger at 0 of 0, three builds, ten budgets and five pipeline checks green. No save-file change, no public signature removed, no saved design affected. Floorplanner2D still takes two arguments.
E2

Drawing to a number

proposedQ-8Q-9T-62 wkafter E1

Objective. Every dimension typeable, and the wall properties the model already carries reachable and saved.

  • Typed length and angle while drawing, not only after; 15° angle snap; a rectangle-room tool; split a wall at a point and join two collinear walls; alignment guides off existing corners.
  • Per-wall thickness and height in the inspector, and in the file — additive fields, explicitly written (T-6), a 2.0.0 file loading with the configured defaults and re-saving byte-identical when nothing was set.
ACCEPTANCE
  • M-24 = round trip. Thickness and height written by this build read back exactly; every existing fixture re-saves byte-identical
  • A wall drawn by typing 3.4 m at 30° measures 3.4 m at 30° in the model, in all five display units
E3

Annotation

proposedQ-8T-61.5 wkafter E2

Objective. A plan that can be read by somebody who did not draw it.

  • Dimension lines between any two points, with witness lines and the measurement in the active unit; free text labels; a room type alongside the room name; a north arrow; per-room ceiling height.
  • Two new persisted collections — dimensions and annotations — additive, written explicitly (T-6), ignored by any older reader.
ACCEPTANCE
  • M-33 = annotation round trip. Every annotation type survives save, load and undo, and a file carrying none is byte-identical to one written before the sprint
E4

The plan on paper

proposedQ-13T-51 wkafter E3

Objective. A drawing that leaves the application at a stated scale.

  • A backend interface over the eight primitives (T-5), implemented for canvas and for SVG; drawCornerAngles and drawOriginCrossHair refactored onto it.
  • PNG at a chosen resolution, SVG at a chosen scale (1:50, 1:100, custom), print to PDF through the browser, with a scale bar, a title block and the north arrow from E3.
ACCEPTANCE
  • M-34 = backend fidelity. The same plan through both backends produces the same geometry — SVG path data compared against the canvas primitives' arguments, not against pixels
  • A wall measured with a ruler on a printed 1:100 sheet is its modelled length, within the printer's own tolerance

Seven and a half weeks against RM-007's seven, and the half week is T-2: a claim that a thing was half built, checked, and found to be not built at all. E1 is the only sprint here that decides anything — the projection contract in T-1 is what E2, E3 and E4 all draw on top of, and it is the reason this drawing exists before the code rather than after it.

§ 40 · RISKS & COMPATIBILITY

What could go wrong, and what must not change

RiskWhereMitigation
The projection becomes a second model that drifts from the firstE1Derived on dispatch and never written to; the parity metric M-23 compares it against scene.getItems() on every fixture, so drift is a failing test rather than a wrong picture
Coverage falls through the branch floor, which has 0.77 % of roomall fourT-3 is why each sprint states a test budget; the projection is pure data and testable headlessly; coverage is checked per sprint, not at the end
Selection in both directions turns into two sources of truthE1One selection lives where it already lives, in useSelection; both views render it. Neither view keeps its own idea of what is selected — the plan's activeWall stays what it is today, hover
Live drag repaints put work back inside a pointermove handlerE1The controller marks dirty; the repaint happens on the frame already scheduled by invalidate(). No new event, no dispatch in a move handler (T-7, RM-002 R-05)
Additive fields silently lost on saveE2, E3T-6: every new field gets an explicit line in the writer and a round-trip assertion in the same commit
Two rendering backends divergeE4M-34 compares primitive arguments rather than pixels, so a divergence is caught at the call and not at the eye

What must not change

The one-way arrow: src/app imports src/scripts and nothing goes back, and inside the library the model layer stays plain data with no DOM and no GPU — which is precisely why T-1 is answered with a projection. The save format stays readable by any build that reads 2.0.0; E2 and E3 add fields and no reader is required to understand them. Floorplanner2D's constructor keeps its two-argument form; the projection arrives by event. The preserved polygon predicates are untouched here — item hit-testing on the plan is a rectangle test in plan space and needs none of them; collision is J4's re-baseline and stays there. And no metric moves down: the coverage floors, the size budgets and the ten asset budgets are ratchets, and the only number this programme is expected to raise is the count of tests.

First commit of E1 is the projection and its headless test, before a single line of drawing code — because the contract is the part that is expensive to change later, and it is the part that makes the rest of the programme testable at all.

Method — RM-004. Three measurements against the working tree at 3cf1c24 on branch amit, all of them produced by running something rather than by reading code. The payload — every one of the 370 manifest entries read and gzipped at level 9, and every .glb walked chunk by chunk, splitting the BIN into buffer views an accessor points at, views an image points at, and the remainder, then a census of accessor componentType per attribute semantic and of index width against each primitive's POSITION count. Wall identity — three fixtures loaded, censused, round-tripped through saveFloorplan() and loadFloorplan() and intersected, plus a constructed two-walls-one-pair case run through the same round trip. The type ledger — npx vue-tsc --noEmit --checkJs, bucketed by area, by error code and by file. What was not run: no codec. Draco, meshopt and KTX2 are named throughout and none has been executed against this catalog, which is why M-12 carries no target and why B1's first task is the trial rather than its conclusion. Two repository inaccuracies were found and are recorded in §21 G-3 rather than fixed, since this is a planning pass: tsconfig.json's SFC subtotal disagrees with the tree and with its own itemisation.

Sources — RM-004. KHR_mesh_quantization · KHR_draco_mesh_compression · EXT_meshopt_compression · KHR_texture_basisu · glTF 2.0 — Binary glTF layout · three.js — DRACOLoader · three.js — KTX2Loader · TypeScript — checkJs

Method — RM-005. Five measurements against the working tree at 5bfb795 on branch amit, all produced by running something. The textures — every .png, .jpg and .jpeg under public/ outside the thumbnail directories read for its header dimensions and costed at four bytes per texel with the 4/3 mip factor, then each file attributed to the module that uploads it by grepping the three call sites in three/. The budget hole — an existing 669×1024 .ktx2 copied into public/rooms/textures/, tools/check-budget.mjs run before and after, and the file removed; the line did not move. The type tier — npx vue-tsc --noEmit --checkJs, bucketed by directory, by file, by error code and by receiver type, cross-referenced against grep -rl "@ts-check" src to separate files that are clean from files that are protected. The ledger — the "//" block parsed the way tests/type-coverage.test.js parses it, its rows summed, and git log -S run on the stale row and the heading that contradicts it. The crash — RoofItem.prototype.closestCeilingPoint called against a floorplan stub returning no roof planes, and the TypeError caught and printed. What was not run: no encoder, and no renderer. The 8.75 MB C1 projects is the encoder's 1 bpp model applied to seven files, not an observed GPU figure — the same arithmetic that produced B5's 30.46 → 7.61 MB, whose disk half was measured at 0.459 and whose VRAM half was not. And the C1 spike's question — whether two CompressedTexture clones are one upload or two — is deliberately left open, because reading a signature is what produced the costing this program is correcting. Three repository inaccuracies were found and are recorded in §26 rather than fixed, since this is a planning pass: the Skybox claim in tools/encode-textures.mjs and docs/architecture.md, the KTX2 claim in tools/budget.json, and the stale NOT YET row in tsconfig.json.

Method — RM-005 C2, added on delivery. The type half was measured differently from everything above it, and the difference is the finding. Every count in §26 J-3 was produced by npx vue-tsc --noEmit --checkJs against a tree where three resolved to any — the package ships no .d.ts and @types/three was not installed, which a five-line probe confirms: new Vector3().notAThing type-checked without complaint. So 355 was never a count of the defects in this codebase; it was a count of what a half-blind checker could see. Installing the types took it to 267, resolving the project's own JSDoc names took it back to 300, and the sprint ended at 0. What was not run: nothing was measured about runtime behaviour. Six defects were found by reading the checker's output and two of them — the RoofItem and WallItem crashes — were reproduced by calling the method against a stub, which is the only place in this program a claim about behaviour is backed by an execution rather than by a reading. The other four are argued from the code and pinned by tests written for them.

Sources — RM-005. three.js — CompressedTexture · three.js — Source · KTX 2.0 container specification · TypeScript — checkJs · TypeScript — JSDoc reference

Method — RM-006. One measurement, run twenty-one times, against the working tree at 3d21b81 on branch amit. Every GPU texture the encoder has ever considered — the eleven now shipping as KTX2, the eight refused here, ps_7.jpg and envs/Garden.jpg — was rendered from its authored source and from its transcode through one WebGLRenderer under SwiftShader, same plane, same orthographic camera at 1:1, NearestFilter, no mipmaps, both textures tagged sRGB and the renderer outputting sRGB, and the two frames read back with gl.readPixels and differenced per channel over RGB. Sources for the eighteen were recovered with git log --diff-filter=D and git show against the parent of the commit that deleted each one; provenance was checked by re-encoding one with the shipped settings and comparing SHA-256 against the committed container, which matches byte for byte. The encoder sweep re-encoded each failing texture at qualityLevel 128, 192 and 255 and once as UASTC, and measured all four the same way. What was not run: no on-screen-size sweep. The gate is defined at 1:1 — codecRms in tools/resize-textures.mjs compares the decoded output against the pixels that went into the encoder — and re-measuring at the size furniture happens to be drawn at, in order to let a failure pass, would be moving the threshold rather than meeting it. Three repository inaccuracies were found and, unlike a planning pass, all three were fixed here: the Linear-sRGB frame behind C1's published figures, the one-directional repointer, and the “1 bit per pixel” claim in tools/encode-textures.mjs. The superseded oracle figures are kept in place with a corrected column written beside them rather than overwritten, because what C1 decided and why is still the record.

Sources — RM-006. KHR_texture_basisu · KTX 2.0 container specification · three.js — Color management · three.js — CompressedTexture · WEBGL_compressed_texture_etc

Method — RM-007. Two kinds of measurement against the working tree at 8510642 on branch amit, and one comparison. The code — every file under src/scripts and src/app read for what it does rather than what it says, with the line references in § 33 and § 34 pointing at that tree; the catalog and texture lists counted by script (168 items in eight placement types, 142 of them Kenney, 25 baked, one duck; five wall and two floor textures, two of them one file). The application — booted under Vite in headless Chromium at 1440×900 and 390×844, driven through fifteen states (plan, split, 3D, catalog open, two items added, item selected, walls drawn, wall selected on the plan and in 3D, walkthrough attempted, shortcuts dialog) and screenshotted at each; the “0 of 2” and “40 px strip” figures are read off those pictures, and rev B added 1024×768, 1280×720 and 1366×768 with the split view and the catalog open, reading the plan canvas width and scrollWidth against the viewport (462, 505 and 334 px of plan; no overflow at any). The comparison — Coohom's own floor-planner page and Sweet Home 3D's feature page were read for what a planner is expected to do, and used as a checklist and nothing more; no figure in this drawing comes from either. Effort is at the single-developer cadence RM-001 was planned at, stated at ±40 % because nothing here has been measured against a sprint yet; every programme gets its own drawing before it starts.

Method — RM-008. Seven measurements against the working tree at 1d53fc8 on branch amit, five of them run in headless Chromium against the library as the dev server serves it rather than read off the source. The structure — a Model constructed in the page and its own properties and its floorplan's read back, which is how “a Floorplan has no path to a Scene” became a fact rather than an inference. The two selection directions — the application driven to one click each way, screenshots of the other pane captured immediately before and after, differenced per channel at a tolerance of 6; both returned zero changed pixels, which is what corrects RM-007. The cost — plans of 1, 4, 16 and 36 rooms built through the real Floorplan and drawn 60 times each after a warm-up, and item footprints drawn 60 times at 20, 60 and 150 into a 1200×800 canvas. The coverage — coverage-summary.json from npm run test:coverage, per file. The primitive surface — all 77 this.context.* calls in floorplanner_view.js attributed to their enclosing method by script. Effort is at the same single-developer cadence as every drawing in this set, and it is the first in the set to have raised its own estimate on the strength of a measurement.
